  ## Part 0 — correct and fully wired ✅
  
  `build_pipeline.py` runs download → parse → split → feature_store → popularity, and each stage checks out against the real data:
  
  - **Schemas match.** `parse.py`'s EB-NeRD assumptions (`article_ids_inview`, `article_ids_clicked`, `session_id`, `*_fixed` history columns, `category_str`) and MIND's TSV layouts are all exactly right.

  - **The temporal split assertions hold.** MIND train `11-09 00:00:19 .. 11-14 23:59:13`, dev `11-15`; EB-NeRD train `05-18 07:00 .. 05-25 06:59:52`, validation `05-25 07:00:15 .. 06-01 06:59:33`. No overlap, so `_split_one`'s asserts pass.

  ## Part I Q1 — formulas are right, but three real problems
  
  | Q1 clause | Status |
  |---|---|
  | 1. click count | ✅ |
  | 1. categories | ✅ recency-decayed histogram + affinity/match |
  | 1. titles, embeddings | ❌ absent — `feature_store` sets `embedding = None` |
  | 1. recency-weighted (exp decay) | ⚠️ degenerate — see below |
  | 2. within-session patterns | ✅ backward-looking, with a test |
  | 2. dwell time | ✅ EB-NeRD, NaN + `has_dwell_time` for MIND |
  | 2. position bias | ✅ `1/log(rank+2)` |
  | 3. popularity / freshness / category match | ✅ |
  | 4. behaviour-window boundary | ⚠️ across splits only — see below |
  
  **1. `recency_weighted_engagement` carries zero information.** `src/impression_features.py:23` computes `Σ dᶦ for i in range(n)` = `(1−dⁿ)/(1−d)` — a function of `len(history)` alone, identical to `click_count` up to a monotone transform, and no time enters it. EB-NeRD's `history_impression_time` is parsed and never used. This is the one Q1.1 feature the assignment names explicitly.
  
  **2. Training rows still see future clicks (Q1.4/Q9).** `click_count_article` / `ctr_article` are a single whole-train-period total applied to every train row. A train impression at time `t` gets counts that include clicks at `t' > t`, and an article clicked *in that very impression* has that click inside its own popularity feature — direct label leakage. The cross-split discipline is right; the within-train as-of-time discipline is missing. Related: MIND's `first_seen` proxy clamps a negative delta to `0.0`, silently flagging "this article is from the future" rather than marking it unknown.
  
  **3. Nothing runs it, and it wouldn't fit in memory.** `STAGES` stops at `popularity` — no entry point touches `sessionize`/`feature_pipeline`. And MIND-small train is **5.84M (impression, candidate) pairs**; `build_feature_matrix` accumulates a Python list of ~22-key dicts per pair, which will OOM well before finishing.
  
  Also latent: `feature_store.build_user_features` snapshots history from each user's *last* impression in a split — safe only because `feature_pipeline` doesn't use it.
  
  The plan file has the remediation broken into 8 changes with verification steps.

  ## Part I Q1 — mostly done; two genuine correctness bugs
  
  Correct as written: session context (backward-looking, and the EB-NeRD `session_id` never spans two users, so grouping on it alone is safe), dwell time with the `has_dwell_time` flag, `position_bias`, smoothed CTR, category affinity/match, the MIND session-gap proxy, and the train-only discipline for val/test.
  
  **Bug 1 — `recency_weighted_engagement` is degenerate.** `src/impression_features.py:23` is `Σ dᶦ for i in range(n)` = `(1−dⁿ)/(1−d)`. That's a function of `len(history)` alone — a monotone transform of `click_count`, contributing literally no new signal, and no timestamp enters it. Needs `Σ exp(−λ·Δt)` over `history_impression_time` (EB-NeRD has it, parsed and unused); keep the positional form for MIND with a `has_history_timestamps` flag. Note `history_category_weights` uses positional decay correctly — that one's fine.
  
  **Bug 2 — within-train future leakage.** `click_count_article`/`ctr_article` are one whole-train-period total applied to every train row, so an impression at `t` sees clicks from `t' > t`, and a candidate clicked *in that very impression* has its own click inside its popularity feature. Cross-split discipline is right; the as-of-time part is missing. Same shape in the MIND freshness proxy: when `first_seen > impression_time`, `freshness_features` clamps to `0.0` instead of returning `has_known_publish_time=False` — that silently marks the article as future.
  
  **Missing from Q1.1:** titles and embeddings. Only the category channel of "recent clicked articles (titles, categories, embeddings)" exists.
  
  Minor: `CTR_ALPHA/CTR_BETA = 10/100` is a 10% prior CTR, but MIND's base rate is ~1/37 ≈ 2.7%, so the prior dominates and flattens the feature.

  ## Bug 2 (within-train leakage) — fixed properly ✅
  
  `TrainEventIndex` is the right shape. Verifying the semantics:
  
  - `bisect_left(sorted_times, t)` returns the count of events **strictly** before `t`, so an impression's own click is excluded from its own feature. Ties at the exact same second are excluded too — conservative, which is the safe direction.
  - The val/test path degrades correctly: every train event precedes them, so `as_of` returns the full train totals. One code path, correct for all three splits. `test_article_stats_asof_end_of_train_matches_manual_train_recount` pins exactly that.
  - The MIND freshness clamp is fixed **at the source**, not patched downstream: `freshness_reference_time = displays[0]` is only read when `display_count > 0`, which guarantees `displays[0] < as_of_time`. So `freshness_features`'s `max(delta, 0.0)` really is just float safety now, as the comment claims.
  - EB-NeRD gates on `ref < as_of_time` with a `pd.isna` guard that short-circuits before the comparison — `NaT` handled.
  - CTR now shrinks toward the measured `global_ctr` instead of a flat 10%. Correct Bayesian form.
  
  ## Bug 1 (degenerate recency) — fixed ✅
  
  Real `exp(−ln2/halflife · Δt)`, guarded on `len(history_impression_time) == n` so a misaligned list can't silently produce garbage, with a `has_history_timestamps` flag for the MIND fallback. `test_recency_weighted_engagement_uses_real_elapsed_time_not_just_length` is precisely the assertion the old implementation failed.
  
  ## Four notes, none blocking
  
  1. **`RECENCY_HALFLIFE_HOURS = 24` is probably too short for EB-NeRD.** Its `history.parquet` is a frozen 21-day *pre-period* snapshot, so for an impression 5 days into the train period every history click is already ≥5 days old — each contributes ≤2⁻⁵, and by day 7 ≤0.008. The feature doesn't break (per-user ordering at a given timestamp is still meaningful) but it collapses toward zero and drifts mostly with how far into the period the impression sits. Something like 72–168h would keep it in a useful range. Also worth a line in the design note: because the snapshot is fixed per user per split, this feature structurally *cannot* capture within-period recency on EB-NeRD.
  
  2. **`TrainEventIndex` memory.** MIND-small train appends 5.84M `pd.Timestamp` objects into Python lists (~0.5–1 GB resident). Fine on demo/small, won't survive MINDlarge. Storing `int64` epoch nanos (`train["time"].astype("int64")`) in per-article numpy arrays cuts that ~8× and speeds up `bisect`. Later problem.
  
  3. **`src/popularity.py`'s docstring is now stale** — it still says `display_count` is recorded "so downstream feature engineering can compute a smoothed CTR without recomputing this scan", but `article_stats` does its own scan and never reads `popularity.parquet`. That file is now purely the A1 top-N fallback list. One-line fix, but a grader reading it will be misled.
  
  4. **Minor test flakiness:** `test_article_stats_asof_excludes_the_current_impressions_own_click` asserts `click_count == 1` at `sample_time + 1s`. If that article happens to get a second click within the same second, it's 2. Low odds, but `<= 1` or picking a less popular article would be safer.
  
  ## Still open (unchanged, as expected)
  
  Q1.1's **titles and embeddings** channel — only categories exist; `feature_store` still sets `embedding = None`. And `feature_store.build_user_features` still snapshots history from each user's *last* impression in a split — latent, since `feature_pipeline` reads per-row history instead.

● Before I write this up — one correction on item 4: `<= 1` doesn't actually fix the flake I described. The count at `sample_time + 1s` is ≥ 1 by construction, and the failure case is it being **2** (a second click on the same article within that second), which `<= 1` fails on just as `== 1` does. The robust assertion is `>= 1`. I had it backwards in my last message; I'll use `>= 1` unless you want otherwise.

● Done. NRMS is implemented and nothing was trained — no commits either, as asked.
  
  ## What's there
  
  ```
  src/nrms/     config · ids · adapter · articles · sampling · layers · model · dataset · train · evaluate
  src/metrics.py            shared per-impression AUC / MRR / nDCG@k harness
  run_nrms.py               CLI
  tests/test_nrms.py        30 data-free tests
  tests/test_nrms_model.py  15 torch shape/gradient tests
  ```
  
  Run it on the GPU box with:
  ```
  python run_nrms.py --dataset ebnerd --stage all
  python run_nrms.py --dataset mind   --stage all
  ```
  `--fraction 0.02 --epochs 1` gives a fast end-to-end smoke run first.
  
  ## Fidelity to the benchmark
  
  Their architecture, ported rather than approximated: news encoder is `Embedding → Dropout → SelfAttention → Dropout → AdditiveAttention`, user encoder applies it per history slot then `SelfAttention → AdditiveAttention`, and scoring is the dot product. `hparams_nrms` values carried over verbatim (title 30, history 20, 20×20 heads, hidden 200, dropout 0.2, lr 1e-4, npratio 4). Wu-2019 sampling puts one positive per row and records its shuffled index, so `CrossEntropyLoss` over the candidate axis is exactly their categorical cross-entropy.
  
  Three of their quirks I kept deliberately rather than "fixing", since they define the baseline: no bias on the Q/K/V projections, no masking anywhere in the NRMS path (their `SelfAttention` gets three identical inputs and no sequence lengths, so padding participates in attention), and one shared news-encoder instance across both paths.
  
  ## Three deviations, all deliberate
  
  - **Numerical stability in `AdditiveAttention`.** Theirs calls `exp()` directly and goes to `inf` on large logits; I subtract the row max first. Identical up to the epsilon. There's a test that feeds it `1e4` and asserts finiteness.
  - **Splits are ours, not theirs.** Train/val/test come from `src/split.py`'s temporal split, so the Part 0 discipline and the Q9 no-leakage rule hold. They evaluate on EB-NeRD's official validation period — so **our numbers won't be directly comparable to their leaderboard**, and that's worth a line in the design note.
  - **Article-id codec.** Their ids are ints, ours are strings, so `src/nrms/ids.py` assigns dense codes starting at 1 with 0 reserved for history padding and unknown articles — matching their `padding=0` / `unknown_representation="zeros"`. The map is persisted so scores decode back to original ids for a Codabench submission.
  
  ## Verification
  
  `py_compile` clean; **45 passed, 6 skipped** (the skips are the pre-existing data-dependent Q1 tests). That includes a CPU forward/backward on random tensors confirming `(B,T)→(B,400)`, `(B,H,T)→(B,400)`, `(B,H,T),(B,C,T)→(B,C)`, a finite loss, and gradients actually reaching the embedding. The existing suite and `build_pipeline.py` are untouched. I borrowed torch/pytest from `../ire-a1/.venv` and pandas from another venv via `PYTHONPATH` — neither was modified, and this repo still has no environment of its own.
  
  Two things to know before the GPU run: `requirements.txt` gained `torch>=2.2`, `transformers>=4.40`, `scikit-learn>=1.4` with open upper bounds so you can match the box's CUDA wheel; and MIND-small is ~157k impressions against a 250k×768 trainable embedding, so that one wants real GPU memory — start with EB-NeRD demo to confirm the loop before scaling up.

❯ i verified stuff and this is my review:
  Here's the NRMS review. I fetched the actual ebnerd-benchmark source to check against rather than going from memory — which was worth doing, since it corrected two things I'd initially suspected were wrong.
  
  Verified faithful to the benchmark
  Architecture matches exactly: Embedding → Dropout → SelfAttention → Dropout → AttLayer2 for the news encoder, TimeDistributed → SelfAttention → AttLayer2 for the user encoder, dot product, softmax for training / sigmoid scorer. The AttLayer2 port (W/b/q shapes, glorot+zeros init, output width = input width) and SelfAttention port (no bias, /√head_dim, masking branch never fires with 3 identical inputs) are correct, and the max-subtraction deviation is documented and mathematically sound.
  
  Hyperparameters all check out against the real hparams_nrms: title_size=30, history_size=20, head_num=20, head_dim=20, attention_hidden_dim=200, dropout=0.2, lr=1e-4. I'd misremembered history_size as 50 — the repo says 20, so your config is right. TEXT_COLUMNS including body is also correct: theirs is [TITLE, SUBTITLE, BODY], and your abstract maps to their subtitle.
  
  truncate_history (tail + left-pad), Wu-2019 sampling, val-set-is-also-negatively-sampled, early stopping patience 4 with best-weight restore, and the MRR/DCG/nDCG formulas all match. Your eval approach (pad candidate axis + mask) is mathematically identical to their explode-one-candidate-per-row approach and computes the user vector once per impression instead of once per candidate — strictly better. 24/24 tests pass.
  
  Real findings
  1. ReduceLROnPlateau factor is wrong. Theirs is factor=0.2, patience=2, min_lr=1e-6. Yours is LR_PLATEAU_FACTOR = 0.5 with no min_lr. config.py claims these are "their defaults / reproducibility script" values, so this is a stated-fidelity violation. One-line fix.
  
  2. padding_idx=tokenizer.pad_token_id freezes the pad-token embedding; the benchmark trains it. Their Keras Embedding(..., trainable=True) has no mask_zero and no frozen row. nn.Embedding.from_pretrained with padding_idx does not zero that row (it skips reset_parameters when _weight is given) — it only kills its gradient, so you get "frozen at the pretrained pad vector," which is neither their behaviour nor a clean masking fix. This matters more than it looks: since there's deliberately no attention masking, pad tokens occupy most of the 30 slots for short titles and participate fully in attention, so that vector is load-bearing. It also contradicts the principle layers.py explicitly states ("we match that rather than silently 'fixing' it"). Extra wrinkle: for XLM-R pad_token_id=1, while the all-zero "unknown article" rows are token id 0 (<s>), so padding_idx doesn't even correspond to the rows it might look like it's protecting.
  
  3. Token-matrix cache can go silently stale. article_tokens_{title_size}.npy is keyed only on title_size and validated only on shape — but the docstring correctly says it's determined by "(codec, text columns, tokenizer, title_size)". Change TEXT_COLUMNS or swap TEXT_ENCODER and the stale matrix is reused, because the shape still matches. Same for article_id_map.parquet. Worth hashing the tokenizer name + text columns into the filename.
  
  4. Comparability trap for Q2. load_impressions(drop_no_click=True) drops no-click impressions at eval time. That's correct for metric definitions, but your Q2 re-ranker must apply the same filter or "before vs after re-ranking" will be computed over different impression sets.
  
  5. Val AUC isn't their val AUC. Keras metrics=["AUC"] is a pooled AUC over all flattened (candidate, label) pairs; yours is a per-group mean. Yours is arguably better and matches the eval protocol — but the epoch numbers won't line up with their logs, so say so in the report rather than presenting it as a reproduction of their curve.
  
  Minor: --fraction subsamples impressions before sampling, theirs subsamples rows after (changes what FRACTION means); _validate averages per-batch losses unweighted so a short final batch is over-weighted; np.argsort(-y_score) breaks ties opposite to their np.argsort(y_score)[::-1] (irrelevant for NRMS's continuous scores, but src/metrics.py is shared — it will matter when you score the "before re-ranking" baseline on integer ranks, where ties are everywhere).
  
  Not a bug, but worth one line in the design note: build_article_codec scans train+val+test behaviours for article ids. No labels are involved so it isn't Q9 leakage, but it is transductive about which ids exist — worth stating explicitly given how carefully the rest of the pipeline handles as-of-time.
  
  oth checks done.
  
  All 51 tests pass now that torch is installed (the 27 model/shape tests that were being skipped included).
  
  Finding #2 empirically confirmed — nn.Embedding.from_pretrained(w, freeze=False, padding_idx=1):
  
  row 1 keeps its pretrained values [4.0, 5.0, 6.0] — not zeroed
  its gradient is [0, 0, 0] — frozen
  without padding_idx (what the benchmark does), the same row gets gradient [1, 1, 1] — trainable
  So it behaves exactly as described: the pad-token vector is pinned at its pretrained value and never learns, while the benchmark trains it. Given there's no attention masking, that vector is doing real work in every short title.
  
  One caveat on my own claim: I couldn't verify the pad-token ids locally (transformers isn't in the venv yet). The pad_token_id=1 for XLM-R / 0 for BERT detail comes from the standard RoBERTa and BERT vocab conventions, not from a local check — worth confirming yourself when you next run on the GPU box.

  ## Fixed
  
  1. **LR schedule** — `factor` 0.5 → **0.2**, added `LR_PLATEAU_MIN_LR = 1e-6` and wired it through. Now matches `factor=0.2, patience=2, min_lr=1e-6` exactly, and I recorded their full callback config in a comment above the constants so the "their defaults" claim is checkable.
  
  2. **Pad embedding no longer frozen** — `run_nrms.py` stops passing `padding_idx`. The parameter stays on `NewsEncoder`/`NRMS` (default `None`) with the reasoning in the docstring. Two tests lock this: one asserts `padding_idx is None` and that row 0 receives gradient under the baseline; the other pins the torch behaviour your finding rests on — with `padding_idx=0` the row keeps its pretrained values *and* gets zero gradient.
  
  3. **Cache invalidation** — token matrix is now `article_tokens_{title_size}_{sha1(model_name, text_columns, title_size)[:10]}.npy`, so swapping `TEXT_ENCODER` or `TEXT_COLUMNS` is a cache miss rather than a silent stale read. Added `--rebuild-cache` covering both the token matrix and `article_id_map.parquet`.
  
  4. **Tie-breaking** — extracted `_descending_order` using `np.argsort(y_score)[::-1]`, their convention, with the integer-rank rationale in the docstring. Also documented the `ndcg_score` zero-guard as a deliberate deviation (theirs returns `nan`).
  
  5. **Batch-weighted validation loss** — accumulates `loss × batch_size` over a row counter.
  
  6. **Docs** — `drop_no_click` now carries the Q2 comparability warning; `train.py` states the per-group vs Keras-pooled val AUC difference and says not to present it as reproducing their curve; `build_article_codec` notes it's transductive over ids (and why restricting to train would be worse — every val/test-only article would collapse onto the shared unknown row).
  
  ## Two review items I did not change
  
  - **`--fraction` ordering is already correct.** Their script calls `.sample(fraction=TRAIN_FRACTION, ...)` on the impression frame and *then* `.pipe(sampling_strategy_wu2019, ...)` — impressions first, same as `run_nrms.py`. `FRACTION` means the same thing in both.
  - **Eval-time metrics already match theirs.** `AucScore`, `MrrScore` and `NdcgScore` each compute per impression then `np.mean` across impressions — exactly `evaluate_impressions`. The pooled-vs-per-group gap is confined to the Keras training-loop metric, which is why I handled it as a reporting caveat rather than a metric change.
  
  I also closed the pad-token-id question you'd flagged as unverified: `bert-base-uncased` pad=0, `xlm-roberta-base` pad=1 with token id 0 being `<s>`. Confirmed by loading both tokenizers, so on EB-NeRD `padding_idx` was freezing `<pad>` while the all-zero unknown-article rows are `<s>` — a different row entirely.   this is only 3.5%, is that fine?

● Answering the question first, then I've got a concrete follow-up to propose.

● **Short answer: fine to *report*, not fine to leave unexamined.** Your structural reasoning is right, but it explains the low absolute number — it doesn't explain the lift, and that's where the two datasets diverge sharply.
  
  | | catalog | random top-200 | observed | lift |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | MIND | 65,238 | 0.31% | 4.5% | **~15×** |
  | EB-NeRD demo | 11,777 | 1.70% | 4.6% | **~2.7×** |
  
  Same 4.5% vs 4.6% headline, wildly different quality. MIND's retriever is doing real work. EB-NeRD's is barely above drawing 200 articles at random. Same code, so it's the catalog, not the algorithm.
  
  I measured why on the real demo bundle:
  
  - `articles.parquet` spans **2000-10-02 to 2023-06-08**; the train behaviours window is 2023-05-18 to 2023-05-25. There are articles from 2000 sitting in the retrievable pool.
  - Only **21%** of the catalog (2,478 / 11,777) is ever shown in train behaviours — ~79% of what you're retrieving from could never be a positive.
  - **14.4%** (1,690 articles) are published *after* the train period ends and are still retrievable during training.
  
  That last one is a **future-item leak**. `retrieve_for_split` (`src/lexical_retrieval.py:45`) calls `index.top_k(query_text, k_max)` against one global index with no time restriction. It doesn't leak labels, so it's not the Q9 failure — but it's exactly the kind of as-of-time violation the `TrainEventIndex` work was built to prevent, and it's inconsistent to enforce that discipline on popularity features while the candidate generator retrieves tomorrow's news.
  
  Rough headroom from coverage stats — **estimates, not measurements**, and a genuine trade-off since tighter windows raise the hit rate but cap the ceiling:
  
  | as-of window | eligible pool | covers of ever-shown | random@200 |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 2 days | 1,061 (9%) | 41.5% | 7.8% |
  | 7 days | 1,720 (15%) | 46.9% | 5.5% |
  | 30 days | 4,501 (38%) | 56.2% | 2.5% |
  | none (today) | 11,777 | 100% | 1.7% |
  
  Note the 30-day row: a *random* draw from a 30-day window (2.5%) is already over half your current retriever's full-catalog recall. Applying the same 2.7× lift inside a window plausibly lands EB-NeRD at 7–20%.
  
  Two things worth separating, because they're independent decisions:
  
  1. **The as-of cutoff** (never retrieve an article published after the impression) is a correctness fix and should go in regardless of what it does to recall.
  2. **The window length** is tuning — and it's a ready-made Q3 "principled improvement + ablation" with a natural sweep and the paired bootstrap CI the assignment wants.
  
  None of this changes the re-ranker decision. A1 already reached the right conclusion there (score the in-view list), and NRMS is unaffected either way since it reads `behaviors_*.parquet` directly.
  
  The plan file covers the fix — the index classes already return dense score arrays, so it's a mask before selection rather than any rescoring. One caveat baked in: MIND has no `published_time` at all, so it'd reuse the first-seen-in-train proxy from `article_stats`, and the gain there will be much smaller.

❯ but can we proceed with just 3.5%, because even after changes, its not really high. so im wondering if its even worth it to do that

● Yes — proceed with 3.5%. I'd drop the window tuning.
  
  The decisive argument is one I under-weighted when I wrote that plan: **the freshness fix cannot rescue the pure two-stage architecture anyway.** For "retrieve top-200, then re-rank" to produce meaningful metrics, most impressions need to contain a positive — realistically 70%+ recall. Catalog-wide retrieval scored against closed-list logs will never get there, because the ceiling isn't set by your ranking quality, it's set by the platform's showing decision, which isn't a function of history similarity. Going 4.6% → 20% still leaves 80% of impressions with nothing to rank. Same broken experiment, better-looking number.
  
  And under the in-view architecture — the one A1 already landed on, and which both leaderboards and NRMS use — recall@200 stops being on the critical path entirely. It's a descriptive number in the design note plus an input to the Q4 serving analysis. Improving it moves no headline metric.
  
  So the work is either insufficient (two-stage) or irrelevant (in-view). That's a bad trade either way.
  
  Two caveats on what I'd keep:
  
  **The as-of cutoff is a separate question from the window.** Retrieving articles published after the impression time is a correctness inconsistency, not a performance one — you enforce as-of-time strictly for popularity via `TrainEventIndex` and then let the retriever pull articles from two weeks in the future. It's ~20 lines (a mask before selection, no rescoring). But only bother *if* candidates end up feeding something you report. If they become a footnote, skip it and say so in the design note.
  
  **The 3.5% is worth reporting as a finding, not hiding as a weakness.** "We measured catalog-wide retrieval at 3–4.6% recall@200; here's the structural reason — logged feedback can't label an article that was never shown, so open-catalog retrieval is being evaluated on a closed-list task — therefore we score the in-view list, as A1's own eval, both Codabench leaderboards, and the NRMS baseline all do" is a strong paragraph. It shows you understood *why* the number is low rather than just tuning until it looked better. Q6 explicitly rewards that kind of analysis.
  
  The thing actually worth your time is the decision you've deferred twice: whether Q2's candidate set is the in-view list, the retrieved top-200, or the union of both. That determines whether Q2 produces meaningful numbers at all — and unlike the recall number, it can't be worked around later.

● The measurements turned up a much better lever than batch-size juggling.
  
  **Where the memory actually goes** — EB-NeRD's XLM-R embedding is 192M params, so params + grads + Adam's two moments come to **3.07 GB before a single activation**, 38% of the card. MIND's BERT vocab is only 30,522, so it's 0.38 GB and never a problem.
  
  **The decisive finding:** of XLM-R's 250,002 vocabulary entries, only **14,406 (5.8%)** are reachable from the EB-NeRD token matrix. The other 94.2% can never be gathered by any forward pass — and an ungathered row gets exactly zero gradient, which Adam (with our default `weight_decay=0` and zero-initialised moments) turns into exactly zero update. They are provably inert.
  
  Pruning to the reachable vocabulary takes the embedding from 192.0M → 11.1M params, **3.07 GB → 0.18 GB, saving 2.90 GB with zero effect on any output.** That's a third of the GPU recovered without touching batch size, precision, or fidelity to the benchmark.
  
  Two supporting fixes:
  
  - **Eval is the spiky path.** `eval_collate` pads to the batch maximum, and MIND's candidate counts run median 23 / p99 182 / **max 295** — so one outlier forces 12× padding on its whole batch, ~3.5 GB transient. Since `score_split` already writes results back by index, batch order is free, so I'll bucket by length and cap batches on a *total candidate budget* instead of a fixed impression count.
  
  - **A real bug in checkpointing.** `{k: v.detach().cpu() ...}` calls `.cpu()` per tensor, which breaks storage sharing — and because `UserEncoder` holds a reference to the same `NewsEncoder`, the embedding is stored under two keys and written twice. That's why `nrms.pt` is **1.5 GB** for a ~774 MB model, with 2× the embedding in host RAM on every best epoch.
  
  I'm leaving AMP opt-in rather than default: pruning already solves the memory problem, and a baseline reproduction shouldn't silently change numeric precision. It's there via `--amp` if the 208k-row MIND run wants the speed.
  
  The gate on all of this is an equivalence test — pruned and unpruned models must produce comparable logits from the same weights. That's what licenses the saving.

● Fair push-back — and you're right to question it. Let me be straight about what's actually necessary.
  
  **Pruning isn't needed.** I over-engineered that. The evidence is already in hand: the smoke run trained at batch 32 *and* scored all 25,356 EB-NeRD test impressions on this GPU without complaint. EB-NeRD's worst-case impression is 93 candidates, so that run already exercised the real memory profile. Rough peak is ~4.2 GB of 7.9 GB available — 3.07 GB embedding plus activations. It fits, with room.
  
  Pruning would have bought speed and headroom, not feasibility. Since it adds a remapping layer between the tokenizer and the model — exactly the kind of thing that's a pain to debug later — dropping it is the right call.
  
  **The one genuine unknown is MIND's eval tail.** Its candidate counts run median 23 / p99 182 / **max 295**, so a batch of 32 padded to 295 is ~9,440 titles at once, roughly 3.5 GB transient. That's the only thing that might not fit, and it's in eval, not training. The cheap fix is a separate eval batch size — no architectural change, and it doesn't touch the training batch size that defines the baseline.
  
  **On Colab/Kaggle:** genuinely viable — both give 16 GB (T4/P100) versus this card's 8 GB, and A2.pdf names them as the expected compute. But right now it'd be a step backwards: the venv is installed, data is built and split, 59 tests pass, and the smoke run is green. Moving means redoing all of that, plus MIND is a gated HF repo so you'd need the token there, and you'd be fighting session timeouts on the 208k-row run. Worth doing **if** MIND doesn't fit locally — not before we've found out.
  
  So my recommendation: run EB-NeRD locally right now with zero code changes, then try MIND, and only escalate if it actually OOMs.

● Plan revised: no pruning, no AMP, no gradient accumulation. Run EB-NeRD as-is, attempt MIND at defaults, and add a separate `--eval-batch-size` only if MIND's eval actually OOMs — keeping the *training* batch size at 32 since that's part of the baseline definition. Colab stays as the escalation path if that isn't enough.

● Adding a regression test for the dedupe, then running the suite:

❯ should the loss function not be NDCG? why are you using cross entropy?

● Good question, and it gets at a real distinction. Let me ground one part of the answer in our actual data first.

● Good instinct — "we measure nDCG, so why not optimise it" is a real and well-known concern in learning-to-rank. But nDCG can't be used directly, and what we're using is already a ranking loss.
  
  **nDCG isn't differentiable.** It's computed from the *sort order* of the scores, and `argsort` is a step function: its gradient is zero almost everywhere and undefined at ties. Nudging a score changes nDCG either not at all or discontinuously when two items swap. There's nothing to backpropagate. nDCG can be a metric or an early-stopping signal, never a loss.
  
  **Cross-entropy here isn't the pointwise classification loss it might look like.** Because Wu-2019 sampling makes every training row one positive against `npratio` negatives, the softmax runs *across the candidate axis*, not over classes. That makes it a **listwise ranking loss** — ListNet with a single relevant document, equivalently InfoNCE / sampled softmax. What it maximises is `P(positive scores above these 4 negatives)`, which is exactly the quantity AUC, MRR and nDCG reward. It's ranking-aware, just via a smooth surrogate.
  
  **Our data makes the surrogate unusually tight.** Relevance is binary and mostly singular:
  
  | | exactly 1 click | ≥2 clicks |
  |---|---|---|
  | EB-NeRD | **99.7%** | 0.3% |
  | MIND | 72.8% | 27.2% |
  
  With one relevant item and binary gain, nDCG@k collapses to `1/log₂(rank+1)` and MRR to `1/rank` — both are pure functions of the positive's rank. Softmax cross-entropy is a smooth surrogate for precisely that rank. The objective/metric gap that motivates nDCG-specific losses is largest with *graded* relevance, where the gain function actually does work. Here there is no gain function to speak of.
  
  **And fidelity forces it anyway.** Their `NRMSModel` compiles with `categorical_crossentropy` over the `npratio+1` softmax, and Wu et al. (2019) use the same. Q3 asks us to reproduce the official baseline; swapping the loss would make it a different model, not a reproduction.
  
  **Where your instinct does pay off:** if you want to optimise nDCG directly, the standard answer is a differentiable surrogate — **LambdaRank** (weight each pairwise gradient by the ΔnDCG that swapping the pair would cause), or ApproxNDCG / NeuralNDCG / SoftRank. The natural home for that is the **Q2 GBDT re-ranker**, where LightGBM's `objective="lambdarank"` with `ndcg_eval_at=[5,10]` is a one-line change that genuinely optimises nDCG. That's a much better place to spend the idea than perturbing the baseline.
  
  One honest caveat that partly supports you: our EB-NeRD run showed **val loss rising while val AUC rose** (1.6028 → 1.6737 against `ln(5)=1.609`). So cross-entropy and ranking quality did diverge once the model started overfitting — the loss got worse while the ranking kept improving. That's why early stopping monitors AUC rather than loss, and it's worth a line in the design note.
  
  Short version: no change to the baseline. Let me record that and get the pending runs going.

● No baseline change — the plan now records the reasoning and parks LambdaRank with the Q2 re-ranker where it actually fits. The logging and W&B work is done and green at 67 tests; what's left is verifying the wiring at runtime, archiving the truncated 5-epoch EB-NeRD result as a mini-ablation, then the two converged runs.

  Two things are worth being precise about here:
  
  **These deltas are almost certainly inside the noise.** They're in the third decimal place on 25,356 impressions. A2 Q3 requires a paired bootstrap 95% CI for any claimed gain, and this is exactly the case that rule exists for — I'd not claim either run is better without one. What I *can* say is that the model plateaus around AUC 0.54–0.55 on the demo bundle, and epochs 11–15 bought nothing.
  
  **Val and test AUC aren't the same quantity.** Val AUC is measured on Wu-2019-sampled rows (1 positive vs 4 negatives); test AUC is over the full in-view list. So "val AUC rose while test AUC fell" isn't a contradiction — we selected on a 5-way ranking task and evaluated on a ~9-way one. Worth stating explicitly in the design note rather than presenting val AUC as if it predicted test.
  
  The overfitting is unambiguous though: train loss fell 1.5217 → 1.1842 while val loss climbed 1.6028 → 1.9473. At 193.6M params against 22,495 training rows, that's expected.

  Three things worth carrying into the design note:
  
  **The metrics disagree about which dataset is "better," and that's a candidate-count artifact.** MIND wins on AUC (0.604 vs 0.543) but loses on MRR and nDCG. MIND impressions have ~23 candidates against EB-NeRD's ~9, so a random ranking scores much lower MRR/nDCG on MIND purely from list length, while AUC is length-normalised. Comparing MRR across the two datasets is close to meaningless; comparing AUC is fair.
  
  **The memory profile inverts between phases.** EB-NeRD is training-heavy (3,869 MiB, from XLM-R's 192M-param embedding); MIND is eval-heavy (4,544 MiB, from 295-candidate impressions padding out the batch). Neither came close to the 8 GB limit — worth noting given we nearly built vocabulary pruning to solve a problem that didn't exist. Those figures feed Q4's serving analysis directly.
  
  **Both overfit, MIND more gracefully.** MIND's val loss fell through epoch 3 before turning up; EB-NeRD's rose from epoch 2. With 208k training rows against 22.5k, that's the data-size story again — and it's why EB-NeRD demo lands well below their published ~0.68 on `ebnerd_large`.
  
  Also on disk: `metrics_test_5epoch.json` preserves the truncated EB-NeRD run as a free mini-ablation, per-run logs in `log/`, both runs synced to W&B under `ire-a2-nrms`, 67 tests passing, and nothing sensitive tracked in git.

● Memory is written — seven memories plus the index. Nothing left to fetch; here's the analysis.
  
  ## What your teammate built
  
  A **LambdaMART re-ranker** (LightGBM `lambdarank`) over A1's top-200 candidates, plus `DesignChoices.md` (a 618-line decision log) and `todo.md`. It's strong work, and a few choices are notably sharp:
  
  - **`lambdarank_truncation_level=200`.** LightGBM defaults to 30, which would drop the gradient for most positives, since BM25 ranks them at median 80/200. Easy to miss, and it would have silently crippled training.
  - **An exact evaluation shortcut.** It scores only impressions that contain a positive and divides by the full population. That's exact, because no-positive impressions contribute a fixed 0 to MRR/nDCG, and it's checked against brute force on both synthetic and real data. This is what makes MIND's 27.7M rows tractable.
  - **Self-critical results analysis.** They found freshness *alone* reaches AUC 0.85 of the model's 0.96, so they demoted AUC to a diagnostic, led with MRR/nDCG, and explicitly refused to put 0.96 next to NRMS's 0.54.
  
  ## One caveat up front
  
  **None of their results exist on this machine.** There are no candidate files and no `reranker/` artifacts, and lightgbm isn't installed. I verified the code; I couldn't verify the numbers in §2B/§2D.
  
  ## Main finding: a leak-shaped shortcut on EB-NeRD
  
  The retriever has no time filter, and `has_known_publish_time` is a model feature that is `False` exactly when an article was published after the impression:
  
  | | clicked articles | catalogue (the retriever's pool) |
  |---|---|---|
  | EB-NeRD: published after impression | **0.00%** of 25,444 | **10.7%** mean |
  | MIND: `has_known=False` | 82.6% | 71.6% |
  
  On EB-NeRD that's a **perfect** negative indicator, and it couldn't exist at serving time, because no real system retrieves an unpublished article. On MIND the shortcut doesn't exist at all.
  
  This refines their analysis rather than contradicting it. They put the easy AUC down to stale articles, and stale ones are indeed the bigger share (55%) and a legitimate signal. The future ones are smaller but a different kind of problem: a Q9 concern.
  
  It also **confounds §2D Finding 3.** That finding reads EB-NeRD vs MIND as a "natural ablation of freshness quality", but the two datasets also differ in whether this shortcut is available.
  
  Caveat: this is measured over the catalogue, not the actual retrieved top-200, so the share among retrieved negatives is unmeasured.
  
  The cheap check: retrain EB-NeRD without `has_known_publish_time` and with NaN freshness masked. That's under a minute per their timings. Their `todo.md` already lists time-restricting retrieval as its #1 recall idea; this adds a correctness reason to do it.
  
  ## Smaller issues
  
  - **`test_reranker.py` imports lightgbm at module level.** Without lightgbm, pytest **aborts the entire session**, so all 67 other tests stop running too. `test_nrms_model.py` avoids this with `importorskip("torch")`; this file should do the same.
  - **A wording nit.** The docstring says evaluation "does not filter", but the code does filter, with `positives_only=True`, and corrects the denominator. The results are exact, but a grader reading it could reasonably be confused.
  
  ## Memory saved

  - **Project facts:** who owns what, with the teammate's results living on their machine; the local environment (GPU, venv, data symlinks); the W&B project link; and the shortcut finding above.

❯ Does one positive per group make re-ranking pointless? No — measured
  Two separate facts get conflated here, and only one is a problem:
  
  > "Exactly one positive per group" is normal, not a pathology. It is the standard click-log ranking setup: put the one relevant item as high as possible out of 200. MRR is defined for exactly this case, and nDCG with a single positive reduces cleanly to 1/log2(rank+1). Both Codabench leaderboards score this same ~1-click-per-impression data, and NRMS trains on it.
  > "Zero positives in ~96% of groups" is the actual problem, and it comes from the recall ceiling, not from single-click impressions.
  
  
  
  
  > Q3 says "reproduce the baseline, then beat it." NRMS scores the in-view set (~20 candidates, always containing the click); the re-ranker scores top-200 (~96% with no click at all). Those numbers aren't comparable, and the re-ranker structurally cannot beat NRMS on them. The clean apples-to-apples comparison for Q2 is retrieval order vs re-ranked order on the same candidates — that's the 97%-headroom result.
  hell nah
  
  
  
  
  
  
  [Image #2] [Image #3] [Image #4] [Image #5] [Image #6] [Image #7]
  
  
  
  we clearly need to improve recall to proceed, what is going wrong for us?

● Let me dig into this with data rather than theory. Your intuition is right that recall is the binding constraint — MRR ≈ recall × ranking-quality, and recall is sitting at 2.5–3.9%. The question is *why* it's that low, and my strong suspicion from the earlier catalogue measurements (55% of the catalogue >30 days stale, 10.7% not yet published) is that we're **retrieving from the wrong population**: news clicks land on what the platform is showing *right now*, and similarity search over a 20-year catalogue mostly finds articles nobody was shown.
  
  That's testable without any candidate files, straight from the behaviour logs. Four measurements, done in a single pass:
  
  1. **Catalogue coverage** — can a clicked article even be retrieved? (Is it in `articles.parquet`?)
  2. **Age of clicked articles at click time** (EB-NeRD has a real `published_time`)
  3. **A "recently displayed" pool** — the articles shown in *any* impression during the previous W hours. How big is it, what fraction of clicks does it contain, and what's recall@K if you just rank it by recent popularity? This uses only strictly-past display and click events — things the platform knows at serving time.
  4. **A "recently published" pool** (EB-NeRD) — the newest-first K articles as of the impression.

● Both answers are in, and they settle the design.
  
  **The harness is validated.** My catalogue-wide BM25 reproduces at 2.1% / 2.2% on the 2,000-impression sample against the teammate's full-population 2.55% / 2.90%. That's within sampling noise, and it means every comparison below is like-for-like.
  
  ## What's going wrong: two compounding mistakes in stage 1, and neither is in the re-ranker
  
  | stage-1 generator | EB-NeRD @200 | MIND @200 |
  |---|---|---|
  | A1 BM25, whole catalogue *(current)* | 2.1% | 2.2% |
  | A1 BM25, restricted to articles shown in the last hour | 85.8% | 32.8% |
  | **popularity (recent clicks), last-hour pool** | **97.3%** | **96.0%** |
  | union: popularity top-150 + A1-in-pool | 97.7% | 95.2% |
  
  **1. We search the wrong space.** EB-NeRD clicks land on articles a **median 3.1 hours old** — 92% within 24h of publication. A1 searches a catalogue that goes back to 2000. Restricting A1's *own, unchanged* scoring to articles displayed in the previous hour takes it from 2.1% to 85.8% on EB-NeRD. The time filter alone is a 15–40× fix.
  
  **2. We rank by the wrong signal.** Inside the fresh pool, similarity to a user's history is a weak signal. Popularity beats it at every K — EB-NeRD @50: 85.5% vs 22.9%. News clicks follow what is fresh and trending, not word overlap with what someone read last week. A1 also adds nothing on top of popularity: in the union it's +0.4pp on EB-NeRD and −0.8pp on MIND.
  
  It isn't a bug in the retriever. It's the wrong kind of retriever for news.
  
  **It's legitimate:** the pool uses only displays and clicks from *strictly before* the impression, which the platform knows at serving time, so there's no future leakage. One caveat for the report: the pool leans on the platform's own display logs. For EB-NeRD there's a no-logs version — newest-published-first gets 94.0% @200 from catalogue metadata alone.
  
  **It also fixes problems downstream.** The negatives become *other fresh articles*, i.e. hard negatives. That removes the future-article shortcut I found yesterday, and the "AUC is an artefact" problem. Freshness can no longer carry 80% of the model; it has to earn its ranking from behavioural features.
  
  **One thing it doesn't fix — Q3.** Even at 97% recall, MRR over 200 candidates isn't NRMS's MRR over ~9–23 in-view articles. A fair "beat the baseline" needs both rankers scored on *identical* candidate sets. The second question below covers that.
  
  What remains before I can write the plan:
  
  1. **How often the 1h pool falls short of K=200.** EB-NeRD's median pool is 230, barely above K, so off-peak hours will often come up short — and the very first hour of the dataset has an empty pool. That decides how important the fallback is. It's cheap to measure.
  2. **Your call on framing and scope.** These are judgment calls about the assignment, and they change the work materially.
  
  Both are independent, so here they are together:

● Neither pending item is good news, and I'm not going to soften it: **the re-ranker makes the ranking worse.**
  
  | EB-NeRD test, `popular` candidates | before (stage-1 popularity) | after (LambdaMART) | change |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | AUC | 0.8815 | 0.8927 | +1.3% |
  | **MRR** | **0.1639** | 0.1291 | **−21.2%** |
  | nDCG@5 | 0.1471 | 0.0994 | −32.5% |
  | nDCG@10 | 0.1994 | 0.1656 | −16.9% |
  
  **The recall fix itself worked.** Stage-1 popularity alone now gets MRR **0.164**, against the 0.018 the whole catalogue pipeline reached *after* re-ranking — 9×, measured over identical impressions. 24,591 of 25,356 test impressions now carry their click.
  
  **But the re-ranker degrades the top of the list**, and there's a reason this is a diagnostic signal rather than just a weak model. `retrieval_rank` and `position_bias` are both model features, so LambdaMART could reproduce the stage-1 order exactly and score MRR 0.164. Doing *worse* than a function it can represent means **training and evaluation see different distributions**.
  
  There are two candidate causes, and I can't tell them apart yet:
  
  1. **A feature shift.** The Q1 popularity features (`click_count_article`, `ctr_article`) come from `TrainEventIndex`. For *train* rows they're as-of: live, accumulating counts. For *test* rows they're frozen at the end of train, so the fresh test-period articles that actually get clicked all sit near zero. The model would learn "high recent click count → click" and find it never fires at test time.
  2. **Negative sampling.** Training groups are about 50 rows (positive, 24 hard negatives, 25 random); evaluation groups are 200.
  
  The feature importances are consistent with the first. There's also a second warning sign: impression-level features that are constant within a group — `impression_size` 10.5%, `session_position` 5.6% and others, about 20% of gain in total — can't rank within a group on their own. So the model is leaning on interactions, which smells like overfitting.
  
  Other status: **the 3 real-data re-ranker tests now pass (12/12 in that file)**. MIND `popular` generation is still running (it launched with 3.6 GB free).
  
  What's next: one diagnostic script answers the key question without retraining, so that's the only thing I'm running this turn. On samples of train, val and test impressions, it:
  - compares the saved model against stage-1 rank and single-feature rankers;
  - measures how the popularity features are distributed for clicked articles in train versus test.
  
  If the model beats stage-1 on train but loses on val/test, that confirms a shift; the feature distributions will say which features shift. An ablation retrain would depend on that answer, and memory (MIND generation is running) keeps it out of this turn. I'm holding the MIND re-ranker and ablation arms until this is resolved — running them now could just reproduce the same failure at eight times the cost.

● The diagnosis is conclusive, and it's **not** negative sampling. It's a **train/serve skew in the Q1 article-popularity features**:
  
  | `click_count_article` from `TrainEventIndex` | train | val | test |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | median for **clicked** articles | **16** | **0** | **0** |
  | median for non-clicked | 6 | 2 | 0 |
  | clicked articles with zero counts | 3.5% | **87.4%** | **99.2%** |
  
  **Train rows** get *live* as-of counts that accumulate within the train period. Clicked articles have many (16 vs 6), so the model learns "high click count → click". **Val and test rows** get counts *frozen at the end of train*. The articles people actually click there were published after train ended, so they all sit at zero. On val the feature isn't just useless, it's **inverted**: clicked articles sit at 0 while non-clicked ones sit at 2, because older articles did collect train-period clicks. The model therefore up-ranks stale articles and buries the fresh ones.
  
  The model-vs-stage-1 gap confirms it. The model is only −4% behind on train, but **−21% on val** and **−18% on test**. Meanwhile `retrieval_score` — the live 1h popularity from the fresh pool — separates clicked articles cleanly in every split (medians 4 / 4 / 3 vs 0). The model mostly ignored it, because the skewed feature looked more predictive in-sample.
  
  I introduced this. "Frozen at end of train for val/test" was my own `TrainEventIndex` design in the Q1 review, meant to prevent leakage. It does prevent leakage, but it makes the features mean different things at train time and at serve time.
  
  **The fix:** compute the article statistics as-of *t* over **all** events strictly before *t* — train, val and earlier test — with the same 10-minute click lag. That's exactly what a live serving system's counters hold, so it involves no future information, and it's the same semantics the fresh pool already uses. Q1's default behaviour stays unchanged: I'll make the event scope and the lag parameters, and only the re-ranker opts in. It should also repair MIND's weak proxy freshness (82.6% of clicked MIND test articles currently look "unknown"), which the teammate read as a data-availability effect in §2D Finding 3.
  
  MIND `popular` generation also finished.

  1. MIND `popular` output (recall figures and memory).

  The fix is real, but it's partial. **Correcting the skew helped, and the re-ranker still loses to stage 1:**
  
  | EB-NeRD test | stage-1 | LGBM, train-only stats | LGBM, fixed | fixed vs stage-1 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | AUC | 0.8815 | 0.8927 | **0.9031** | +2.4% |
  | MRR | **0.1639** | 0.1291 | 0.1415 | −13.7% |

  The fix recovered MRR from 0.1291 to 0.1415 (+9.6%), and early stopping now runs 104 rounds instead of 41 — val finally looks like train. But the model is still **worse than a function it can represent**, since stage-1 rank is one of its features. So another train/eval mismatch remains, and the skew wasn't the whole story.
  
  I have two leading suspects:
  
  1. **The hard-negative sampling distorts the rank signal.** Every training group contains the top-24 ranked negatives, so training over-represents the part of the list where stage-1 rank separates the positive *least* well. A clicked article at median rank 13 among negatives at ranks 1–25 tells the model rank is only a weak signal. At evaluation, across all 200 candidates, rank is strong. That fits the model under-weighting rank (`retrieval_score` gets just 1.7% of gain) while freshness boosts fresh-but-unpopular articles from the long tail above the popular clicked one.
  2. **Impression-level features overfitting through interactions.** `impression_size` (11.2%), `session_position` (5.6%), `hour_of_day` and dwell take about 21% of gain. All of them are constant within a group, so they can't rank anything on their own.
  
  Next, one self-contained diagnostic in the background. It writes nothing into the repo, so nothing gets clobbered. I'll **choose on val and report test alongside**, rather than picking whichever configuration scores best on test:
  
  - **C0** — the saved fixed model, scored on val too (I only have its test numbers)
  - **C1** — random-only negatives (0 hard + 49 random), which tests suspect 1 directly at the same memory cost
  - **C2** — current sampling, candidate-level features only, which tests suspect 2 and doubles as the Q3 feature-group ablation
  - **C3** — full 200-candidate groups, no sampling (~4 GB)
  
  Each configuration waits for enough free memory before it starts, so it shares the machine safely with the MIND `bm25_fresh` job that will be running alongside.

● Nothing left is independent of the two running jobs, so this turn has no tool calls.

● Two things came back, and one needs checking before I report it.
  
  **MIND stage-1 arms (full population, test, recall@200):**
  
  | arm | EB-NeRD | MIND |
  |---|---|---|
  | `bm25` (A1, whole catalogue) | 2.55% | **2.90%** |
  | `bm25_fresh` (A1 scoring, 1h pool) | 84.3% | **23.3%** |
  | `popular` (1h pool, popularity) | 97.0% | 93.9% |
  
  The catalogue arm again reproduces the teammate's numbers to the digit (test 2.90%, train 17.02%).

  **The MIND `bm25_fresh` figure doesn't hold up yet.** It came in at **23.3%** on the full population, against **32.8%** on my 2,000-impression sample. That gap is far too big to be sampling noise (the standard error is about 1pp). My suspicion is ties. MIND's 1h pool holds about 2,000 articles, and a short title query scores most of them at exactly zero. So the top-200 is mostly filled by *zero-score ties*, and those are ordered by dictionary insertion order — which differs between my sample harness and the generator. If that's right, the ablation's middle row is being decided by an arbitrary implementation detail rather than by BM25. EB-NeRD barely feels this, because its pool (~230) is close to K.
  
  What I need next:
  
  - **The tie check** — the only independent item. It will count how many pool articles actually get a nonzero BM25 score, and measure recall@200 under four tie-break rules: insertion order (the generator's), article id, popularity, and newest-inserted first. If recall swings widely across rules, the arm is tie-dominated. Then it needs a principled tie-break, or at least a stated caveat.
  - **The harness results (C1–C3)** — pending. The MIND re-ranker depends on them.
  - **The `candidates.py` docstring** — waiting on the tie check. It shouldn't cite a `bm25_fresh` number I'm about to question.

● **Hypothesis confirmed — and the effect is large.**
  
  - On MIND, a title query scores a **median of just 20** of the ~2,147 pool articles above zero (p90 126). **96.8%** of impressions have fewer than 200 nonzero-scored articles, so about 90% of `bm25_fresh`'s top-200 is zero-score ties.
  - Recall@200 then depends almost entirely on the tie rule: **10.0%** newest-inserted first, **17.5%** article id, **23.6%** insertion order (the generator's, matching its full-population 23.3%), **91.7%** popularity. My sample's 32.8% came from a different insertion order in the old harness.
  
  So the MIND `bm25_fresh` figure was measuring an arbitrary tie order, not BM25. The underlying finding is solid, though, and it's a good one for the report: **history similarity is too sparse to rank a fresh pool.** BM25 has an opinion about roughly 20 articles out of 2,000. None of the tie rules is neutral: insertion order carries a hidden recency signal, MIND ids aren't random (id order beat newest-first), and a popularity tie-break turns the arm into `popular`. A **seeded random tie-break** is the only definition under which the arm measures BM25 and nothing else. EB-NeRD barely moved (84.3% full vs 85.8% sample), since its ~230-article pool is close to K.

  2. **Add two tests:** `bm25_fresh` is deterministic across runs, and its output scores are non-increasing, so every scored article sits above every tie.
  3. **A gated background job** that waits for the diagnostic harness to exit, confirms the patch is in place, runs the fresh-pool tests (and aborts regeneration if they fail), then regenerates `bm25_fresh` for both datasets. MIND regeneration peaks near 3.9 GB, and the harness's C3 needs 4.5 GB — running them together would overrun memory, so they have to be sequential. The wait uses the `pytho[n]` regex trick so `pgrep` can't match its own command line.

  ## What was going wrong
  Retrieval was searching the wrong place with the wrong signal. EB-NeRD's clicked articles are a median **3.1 hours old**, but A1 searched a catalogue going back to 2000, ranked by similarity to what the user read before. The new generator takes the articles the platform showed in the last hour and ranks them by recent clicks.
  
  | test recall@200 | EB-NeRD | MIND |
  |---|---|---|
  | A1 BM25 over the whole catalogue (before) | 2.55% | 2.90% |
  | A1's BM25, limited to the last-hour pool | 84.2% | 21.0% |
  | **last-hour pool, ranked by popularity** | **97.0%** | **93.9%** |
  
  It uses only displays and clicks from before each impression, with clicks delayed 10 minutes, since a click lands after its impression. BM25 is too sparse to rank the fresh pool: on MIND it scores only about 20 of ~2,000 articles above zero.
  
  ## The re-ranker needed two more fixes
  At first it did 21% *worse* than stage 1, for two reasons:
  - **The article-popularity features meant different things in training and test.** In training they were live counts; at test time they were frozen at the end of training, so the fresh articles people actually clicked all read zero. That was my own earlier design. They're now counted as of each impression over all earlier events.
  - **My hard-negative sampling misled it about rank.** It over-sampled exactly the part of the list where stage-1 rank is least informative. Random negatives fixed it, and I chose that setting on val.
  
  | test (before = stage-1 popularity) | EB-NeRD | MIND |
  |---|---|---|
  | MRR | 0.1639 → **0.2102 (+28%)** | 0.2237 → 0.2239 (flat) |
  | nDCG@5 | 0.1471 → **0.2007 (+36%)** | 0.2451 → 0.2450 |
  | AUC | 0.8815 → 0.9213 | 0.8655 → 0.8799 |
  
  Against the old pipeline, MRR went from **0.018 to 0.210** on EB-NeRD and from **0.003 to 0.224** on MIND.
  
  ## Caveats
  - **MIND gains nothing from re-ranking.** The model mostly copies the stage-1 order. MIND has no publish dates, so the features add little on top of popularity.
  - **No bootstrap confidence intervals yet**, which Q3 requires. The EB-NeRD gain is very likely significant; the MIND change isn't a gain.
  - **Still not comparable with NRMS** (MRR 0.3394 / 0.2750). Ours ranks 200 candidates, NRMS ranks the ~9–23 articles actually shown. A fair comparison needs both scored on the same candidates.
  - The candidate pool relies on the platform's own display log, which is fine at serving time. The 10-minute click delay is an assumption I couldn't check against real click times.
  
  ## Changes
  New files: `src/fresh_pool.py` and two test files. I also edited your teammate's `src/reranker.py`, `src/candidates.py`, `DesignChoices.md` (new §2E plus open decisions) and `todo.md`, along with `src/article_stats.py`, `src/feature_pipeline.py` and `src/config.py`. Nothing sensitive or generated is tracked in git.
  
  Open follow-ups, also listed in `DesignChoices.md` §3: bootstrap CIs; a clean feature-group ablation under the new sampling (the earlier one ran under the discarded sampling); and a same-candidates comparison with NRMS.

  ```
  fix: fixed retrieval recall with fresh-pool candidates and fixed the re-ranker training bugs
  ```

❯ my teammate made a commit, ive already pulled it, go through the git diff and understand whats new

● Your teammate's commit `ad5c555` adds a "freshness" option to NRMS as our Q3 improvement: it gives the model each candidate's age. It works on EB-NeRD, but on MIND the age is missing for most test candidates. That's the same train-vs-test mismatch we fixed in the re-ranker last round. All 112 tests pass, and nothing has been trained with it yet.
  
  **What's new**
  - **The feature:** with `--freshness`, NRMS adds a tiny learned bonus to each candidate's score based on the article's age. It's a 65-parameter network, started at zero so the untrained model scores exactly like the baseline. Without the flag the model is unchanged.
  - **Where the age comes from:** `src/nrms/freshness.py` (new) looks it up. On EB-NeRD it uses the article's real publish time. MIND has no publish time, so it uses the first time the article was ever shown. An age only counts if that time is strictly before the impression, so nothing leaks from the future.
  - **Plumbing:** the age is passed through sampling, both datasets, training and evaluation. `run_nrms.py` gets `--freshness` and `--run-tag`, and each run saves to its own folder so it can't overwrite the baseline's checkpoint.
  - **`DesignChoices.md` §2F:** explains picking freshness over positional encoding. 29.4% of MIND's history slots are padding and the model has no masking, so positional encoding would mostly be encoding padding. It also marks the Q3 decision as closed. There are 14 new tests in `tests/test_freshness.py`.
  
  **Problems I found**
  1. **On MIND, most test candidates get no age.** The first-seen time is computed from the train split only, so any article first shown after training has none. I measured this on 30k-impression samples:
  
     | First-seen computed from | Train candidates with an age | Test candidates with an age | Test clicks with an age |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | train only (current) | 99.6% | **25.5%** | **17.5%** |
     | train + val + test | 99.6% | 99.9% | 99.9% |
  
     - During training the model almost never sees "age unknown", but at test it's three quarters of candidates, and the clicked articles are hit hardest.
     - Computing it from all splits doesn't leak, because of the strictly-before check.
     - EB-NeRD is fine: 100% of candidates have an age there.
     - §2F says NRMS uses the same age definition as the re-ranker, but that's not true for MIND: the re-ranker uses all splits. The test that checks this passes only because it compares against the train-only version.
     - §2F also predicts MIND will gain less than EB-NeRD. With this bug that would happen anyway, and it would be misread as MIND's first-seen time being a noisy stand-in.
  2. **The two runs don't see the data in the same order.** Creating the extra network uses up one random draw, which changes the shuffle order compared with the baseline. So "the only difference is what the model learns" is overstated. The baseline's own starting weights are correctly unaffected.
  3. **Minor:** `model.py` and `config.py` say "~50 parameters"; the actual count is 65.
  
  The plan proposes fixing all three:
  - compute MIND's first-seen time from all splits;
  - make the check compare against what the re-ranker actually uses;
  - create the extra network without touching the shared random draws;
  - correct the comments, add a dated note to §2F, and update my notes on who owns what.
  
  Nothing gets committed without your approval of the message.

❯ i just want you to explain what the new commit adds, i dont want you to change it. you can give me a readme for this commit saying what is new, and proposed changes, and why those changes are required

● I wrote the readme to `COMMIT_ad5c555_README.md` in the repo root and changed none of your teammate's code. The file is untracked, so it won't be committed unless you add it. I also added a note to my memory file that your teammate owns the freshness option.
  
  **What the commit adds:** NRMS only reads article titles, so it can't tell how old an article is. The commit adds an optional `--freshness` flag that feeds each candidate's age into the score through a tiny 65-parameter network. The age comes from the real publish time on EB-NeRD, and from the first time the article was shown on MIND, which has no publish dates. An age only counts if it's from before the impression, so nothing leaks from the future. Each run with the flag saves to its own folder, `DesignChoices.md` gets a new §2F explaining the choice, and there are 14 new tests. All 112 tests pass; nothing has been trained yet.
  
  **Proposed changes, and why:**
  1. **MIND needs the first-seen time from all splits (must fix before the MIND run).** Right now it comes from the train split only. Measured on samples, that leaves only 25.5% of test candidates and 17.5% of clicked articles with an age, against 99.6% during training. So what the model learns about age mostly can't be applied at test. This is the same kind of train-vs-test mismatch that made the re-ranker worse than stage 1 before we fixed it. Using all splits gives 99.9% coverage and doesn't leak, because only earlier sightings count.
  2. **The two runs don't train on the data in the same order (should fix).** Creating the extra network consumes random numbers, which changes the shuffle order compared with the baseline. I checked: the same seed gives different shuffle seeds with and without the flag. Any gain is then mixed up with seed noise, and with one run each that could decide the result. Creating it with a separate random generator fixes this.
  3. **Cosmetic:** two code comments say "~50 parameters"; the actual count is 65.
  4. **§2F needs a dated note once 1 and 2 are fixed.** It currently says NRMS uses the same age definition as the re-ranker, which isn't true on MIND. It also predicts MIND will gain less than EB-NeRD; problem 1 would produce that result on its own, and it would be misread as MIND's first-seen time being a weak stand-in.
  
  The EB-NeRD freshness run can go ahead once problem 2 is fixed; the MIND run should wait for problem 1.

● Adding article age made EB-NeRD's NRMS clearly better on every metric: test AUC went from 0.5425 to 0.6112. Both runs used the same settings (20-epoch cap, seed 42, batch 32), and both stopped early at epoch 15, so neither was cut off before it finished learning.
  
  **Test results (25,356 impressions each):**
  
  | Metric | Before freshness | With freshness | Change | 95% range of the change |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | AUC | 0.5425 | **0.6112** | **+0.0686** | +0.066 to +0.071 |
  | MRR | 0.3394 | **0.3962** | +0.0567 | +0.054 to +0.060 |
  | nDCG@5 | 0.3770 | **0.4427** | +0.0657 | +0.063 to +0.069 |
  | nDCG@10 | 0.4570 | **0.5095** | +0.0525 | +0.050 to +0.055 |
  | Best validation AUC | 0.5779 | 0.6407 | +0.063 | — |
  
  I got the 95% ranges by resampling the same impressions scored by both models. None of them comes near zero, so the gain isn't noise from which impressions happen to be in the test set. For AUC, the freshness model does better on 46% of impressions, worse on 19%, and ties on the rest.
  
  **What else the runs show:**
  - **The advantage appears in epoch 1.** Validation AUC was 0.5795 against 0.5452. Both models start with identical scores, so this gap comes from what the age input learned, not from a different starting point.
  - **The learned age effect makes sense.** Articles about 6 hours old get the biggest boost. Scores fall on both sides: articles under an hour old get about −0.4 to −0.9 relative to 6 hours, and a 30-day-old article gets about −1.7. This matches the Q2 finding that clicked EB-NeRD articles are typically about 3 hours old, and that freshness drove 80–87% of the re-ranker's gain.
  - **Graphics memory was unchanged** at 3.87 GB for training and 2.89 GB for evaluation, so the head costs nothing in memory. The run finished in 18 minutes; the baseline took 23.
  
  **Caveats for the report:**
  - **One seed per model.** The ranges above cover differences between impressions, not between training runs with different seeds. A gain of 0.069 AUC is very likely much larger than seed-to-seed variation, so I don't think the extra seeds §2F suggested (only "if the effect is small") are needed. If you want that stated with certainty, running two more seeds for each model would take about 80 minutes.
  - **Absolute numbers are low for both models** because this is the small EB-NeRD demo data. Report the difference rather than the headline AUC.

● On MIND, adding article age made no real difference: test AUC was 0.6042 with it and 0.6040 without. Both runs used identical settings (10-epoch cap, seed 42), and both stopped early at epoch 8.
  
  **Test results (73,152 impressions each):**
  
  | Metric | Before freshness | With freshness | Change | 95% range of the change |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | AUC | 0.6040 | 0.6042 | +0.0002 | −0.0006 to +0.0010 |
  | MRR | 0.2750 | 0.2743 | −0.0007 | −0.0014 to +0.0001 |
  | nDCG@5 | 0.2961 | 0.2949 | −0.0012 | −0.0021 to −0.0003 |
  | nDCG@10 | 0.3617 | 0.3610 | −0.0007 | −0.0015 to +0.0001 |
  | Best validation AUC | 0.6585 | 0.6612 | +0.003 | — |
  
  The ranges come from resampling the same impressions scored by both models. Every change is a few ten-thousandths. Only nDCG@5's range excludes zero, and that result is a slight drop that could easily be chance across four metrics. Per impression the two models win and lose about equally often (32% against 30% for AUC).
  
  **It isn't the old coverage bug.** 99.9% of MIND test candidates and 99.9% of clicked articles now get an age, so your teammate's fix worked. The model also learned a sensible rule: newer articles score higher, falling smoothly from about 0 at 15 minutes to −1.36 at 30 days.
  
  **The reason is that age barely separates clicks on MIND.** I measured this on the test split of both datasets, using the learned age curve alone as a ranker:
  
  | | EB-NeRD | MIND |
  |---|---|---|
  | AUC from age alone | **0.677** | **0.514** |
  | Median age of clicked vs. not-clicked articles | 3.1h vs. 4.7h | 15.0h vs. 16.5h |
  | Typical spread of article ages within one impression (median std) | 1,057h | 27.5h |
  
  - **On EB-NeRD,** one impression mixes brand-new articles with ones weeks old, so age strongly predicts which one gets clicked.
  - **On MIND,** all the articles in an impression are about the same age. Adding a score that is nearly the same for every candidate can't change the ranking much.
  
  So §2F's prediction that MIND would gain less came true. The reason is how MIND builds its impressions, not the stand-in for publish time, and not the bug that's now fixed.
  
  **One thing to be upfront about in the report:** on EB-NeRD, ranking by age alone (AUC 0.677) beats the full NRMS-with-freshness model (0.611). The title part of NRMS is weak on the small demo data: its test AUC is 0.54, and during training its training loss kept falling while validation loss rose. Adding it on top of age therefore dilutes a strong signal. "Freshness improves NRMS by +0.069" is still true, but pairing it with this age-only baseline is more honest. It also matches Q2, where freshness carried most of the re-ranker's gain.

❯ so is the claim correct or not?

● Split verdict: the scoping is right, the reason given for it isn't.
  
  **The scoping is correct.** MIND was only ever tested with first-seen ages, and it cannot be tested any other way — the dataset ships no publish dates. So "freshness-as-first-seen doesn't help on MIND" is exactly what was measured, and the broader claim isn't. That stands on its own, independent of any mechanism.
  
  **The rationale — real dates would widen the spread, and a wider spread would bring back the signal — isn't supported by the control test.** Put the three measurements side by side:
  
  | | within-impression spread | head-alone AUC | clicked vs not-clicked age |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | EB-NeRD, real dates | 1,057 h | 0.6765 | 3.1 h vs 4.7 h |
  | EB-NeRD, first-seen | 52.7 h | 0.6631 | 3.0 h vs 4.4 h |
  | MIND, first-seen | 27.5 h | 0.5142 | 15.0 h vs 16.5 h |
  
  EB-NeRD with the proxy has a MIND-like spread (52.7h against 27.5h, a factor of 2) and still ranks at 0.663. So spread isn't the operative variable — cutting it 20x cost only 0.013 AUC. A wider spread on MIND therefore wouldn't automatically buy signal.
  
  **What actually separates the two:** on EB-NeRD, clicked articles are meaningfully fresher than the ones passed over (3.1h vs 4.7h, inside the band where the curve has resolution). On MIND that gap nearly vanishes (15.0h vs 16.5h). Age predicts clicks on one platform and barely on the other.
  
  **I also have to correct something I wrote.** The §2F paragraph I committed explains MIND's null as "impressions are internally near-uniform in age, so an additive term can't reorder the row". My own control test undercuts that: 27.5h of spread isn't too little, since 52.7h was plenty. The click-separation explanation is the defensible one, and that paragraph should be rewritten.
  
  **The one number that would close this out, which I haven't measured:** what share of MIND candidates are right-censored — first seen at the very start of the log window, so their real age is unknown and larger. If that share is small, MIND's flat 15.0/16.5 separation is real and freshness genuinely doesn't drive MIND clicks. If it's large, censoring could be flattening a real separation and your original hypothesis regains its force. That was the measurement you stopped, and it's about a minute per dataset.
  
  Want me to fix the §2F mechanism paragraph, and run the censoring measurement to settle it?

● **Prediction-only — a single split, no train/dev inside, and you must produce a line for every row.** Verified from the files:
  
  | | contents | labels? |
  |---|---|---|
  | `MINDlarge_test` | one `behaviors.tsv` (2,370,727 rows) + `news.tsv` | **No** — bare ids: `N101071 N15647 N83400…` |
  | `ebnerd_testset` | `articles.parquet` + `test/{behaviors,history}.parquet` (13,536,710 rows) | **No** — has `article_ids_inview`, no `article_ids_clicked` |
  
  Compare the training bundles, which are separate downloads and *do* carry labels: `MINDlarge_train` has `N78206-0 N26368-0…` (the `-0`/`-1` suffix), and EB-NeRD's training bundles have `train/` and `validation/` folders with an `article_ids_clicked` column.   **One thing worth knowing, which I'd truncated out of an earlier listing:** the EB-NeRD test set has an `is_beyond_accuracy` column, and it splits the file into two very different populations:
  
  | | impressions | in-view list size |
  |---|---|---|
  | `false` (normal) | 13,336,710 | mean 11.7 (5–100) |
  | `true` (beyond-accuracy) | 200,000 | exactly 250 every time |
  
  That's the RecSys 2024 challenge's second track: those 200k impressions carry a fixed 250-article slate and are scored for diversity/novelty rather than plain accuracy. Our submission already covers them — all 13,536,710 lines, ranks verified — so nothing is broken. But it explains a real mismatch: our model ranks ~12 platform-chosen articles per impression, and on those 200k rows it's ranking 250, closer to the re-ranker's task than NRMS's. If the board reports a separate beyond-accuracy score, expect it to look different from the main one, and don't read the two as the same measurement.
  
  MIND has no equivalent flag — every row is scored the same way.

  | MIND | `MINDsmall_train` → our train split, 138,578 impressions (early-stopped on our val split) | `MINDlarge_test`, 2,370,727 |
  | EB-NeRD | `ebnerd_demo` → our train split, 22,401 impressions | `ebnerd_testset`, 13,536,710 |
  
  It works because NRMS has no per-article parameters — only token embeddings — so it scores a catalogue it never saw during training. That's why a model trained on 65k MIND articles can rank 120,961 of them.
  
  One correction to the framing though: training on the small set isn't what *should* happen, it's just what we had. The assignment points at the large bundles for full-scale training, and the ideal is train-on-large-train → predict-on-large-test. Small-train → large-test is a valid fallback, not the target — which is the gap you spotted two messages ago, and why MIND large is training right now.
  
  Also note the two "test" sets are different things and shouldn't be conflated:
  - **Our own test split** (73,152 MIND impressions, carved from the labelled data) — that's where every number in `DesignChoices.md` comes from, because it has labels we can score against.
  - **The competition test set** (2,370,727) — no labels, prediction-only, scored by the leaderboard.
  
  Let me check on the large run:

● Training is underway — 2,984,293 sampled rows per epoch, so ~85 min/epoch as estimated.
  
  The EB-NeRD download has gotten **worse**: 25 MB in 54 minutes, now 4.5 kB/s, projecting 195 hours. That's dead. But S3 often throttles per-connection rather than per-client, so parallel range requests may get far more throughput. Testing that.

● 6 parallel connections gave ~94 kB/s aggregate versus ~15 kB/s single — so it *is* per-connection throttling. Testing whether it scales further.

● 16 connections → 222 kB/s → **4 hours instead of 8 days**. That's worth implementing properly.

● Both jobs are running. Summary of where this stands:
  
  **Your understanding was right, and it's now being fixed.** The current submission files came from small/demo-trained models scoring the large test sets. That's valid but not the target — hence the MIND large training now in progress.
  
  **MIND large — downloaded, built, training.** 2,232,748 raw impressions → train 1,971,392 / val 261,356 / test 376,471. Roughly 85 min/epoch, so ~11–14 hours depending on early stopping. When it finishes I'll regenerate the MIND submission from that model.
  
  The pipeline couldn't process it as written — `parse` and `split` both held whole frames, which is precisely the §2G "what breaks first at 10x" prediction meeting reality. I rewrote both to stream with polars and verified each against the committed small-bundle output before trusting it: the parser reproduces all 230,117 rows byte-for-byte, the split reproduces all three splits exactly. Large data lands in `data/processed/mind_large/`, so nothing existing was touched.
  
  **EB-NeRD large — solved a download problem, not a compute one.** The bucket was serving a single stream at 4.5–15 kB/s, projecting **8 days**. I measured it properly: the throttling is per-connection, not per-client — 6 connections gave 94 kB/s, 16 gave 222 kB/s. So I added a parallel byte-range downloader (each part resumes independently), and the 3.19 GB should land in about **4 hours** instead of 8 days. It's running now.
  
  Once it's down, EB-NeRD large still needs parsing, splitting and training, and its per-epoch cost is the open question — demo takes 66 s/epoch, and large is likely 10–40x small, so that's where the "is it hours or days" question gets settled with real numbers rather than my guess.

● No, you don't need to train on large. The 6–10 days figure is roughly right as a worst case, but the realistic number is lower. More importantly, the laptop's RAM rules it out before time even matters.
  
  **Testing on large doesn't require training on large.** A2 says the large bundle is required *for the Codabench submission*, meaning the test sets, and that small/demo are for development.
