  **Time estimate, from the throughput your own runs logged** (RTX 5050, batch 32):
  
  | | Measured on your laptop | Scaled to large |
  |---|---|---|
  | MIND | small: 208k training rows, 324 s/epoch. Your 1% MINDlarge smoke run confirms the rate: ~45 ms per training step, ~35 ms per validation batch | 2.98M training rows (from your aborted 2026-09-16 run) → **~77 min/epoch**. Small stopped at epoch 8 of 10, so ~8–13 h, plus ~30 min to score 376k test impressions |
  | EB-NeRD | demo: 22.5k rows, 66 s/epoch (~85 ms/step; slower because XLM-R's 192M-parameter embedding is updated every step) | Assuming ~11M training impressions (EB-NeRD large's train week, taken from the dataset paper, not measured here) → **~8–9 h/epoch**. Demo stopped at epoch 15; with far more data, convergence plus the 4-epoch patience is maybe 6–9 epochs, so **~2.5–3.5 days** plus several hours of test scoring. Running to the 20-epoch cap would be ~7.5 days |
  
  So "6–10 days" is what you'd get if both datasets ran to their epoch caps. Early stopping would probably cut that to **3–4 days**. You have ~3 days until the 20th.
  
  **The bigger blocker is memory, not time.** You have 14 GB of RAM with ~5 GB typically free. The committed pandas `parse.py` and `split.py` hold every history and candidate list as Python objects:
  - The deleted streaming code's own note said that is tens of GB for MINDlarge alone, and EB-NeRD large is several times bigger again.
  - Your MIND-large run on 2026-09-16 stopped right after sampling. The log doesn't say why, but memory is the likely suspect.
  - That streaming code is exactly what we just dropped, so with the committed code large data can't even be parsed on this laptop.

❯ can you explain the current pipeline in simple terms

● The project builds two separate recommenders on the same prepared data. Each answers the same question: "which articles will this user click right now?"
  
  ```
  raw logs ──► 1. prepare data (A1) ─┬─► 2. two-stage recommender (Q1, Q2) ─► 4. measure it (Q4, Q5)
                                     │
                                     └─► 3. NRMS neural model (Q3) ─────────► 5. leaderboard files (Codabench)
  ```
  
  ## 1. Prepare the data (`build_pipeline.py`, from A1)
  - **Download** MIND-small and EB-NeRD demo.
  - **Parse** both into one common format:
    - articles: title, abstract, category, publish time
    - impressions: who saw which articles, when, and what they clicked
  - **Split by time, never at random:** oldest 90% of the early period → train, the rest of it → validation, the later period → test. Nothing is ever learned from the future.
  - **Extras:** popularity counts, article embeddings, and a small feature store.
  
  ## 2. The two-stage recommender
  Like a shop assistant who first grabs a shortlist, then carefully orders it.
  
  **Stage 1: pick 200 candidates** (`fresh_pool.py`, `candidates.py`)
  - A1's original approach searched the whole archive for articles similar to what the user read before. That found the actual click only ~3% of the time.
  - The reason: people click what's in the news right now (median clicked article is ~3 hours old), not something from years ago.
  - The shipped method, `popular`, takes articles the site showed in the last hour, ranked by recent clicks. That finds the click ~94–97% of the time.
  - The BM25/embedding versions stay as comparison arms.
  
  **Stage 2: re-rank those 200** (`reranker.py`)
  - Each candidate gets a set of features:
    - **User:** how much they read, recency, session activity, time spent reading.
    - **Article:** click count, click rate, age.
    - **Match:** whether it's in a category the user likes.
  - **Key rule:** every feature only uses events from *before* the moment being scored. Tests enforce this.
  - A LightGBM model (LambdaMART) learns to push the clicked article to the top.
  - **Result:** EB-NeRD MRR 0.164 → 0.210 (+28%). MIND stays flat, because stage 1 already does most of the work there.
  
  ## 3. NRMS, the neural baseline (`run_nrms.py`, `src/nrms/`)
  - **Setup:** a different task. It only ranks the ~10–40 articles the site actually showed in each impression.
  - **Baseline:** reimplements the official NRMS model, which reads article titles and the user's recent clicks.
  - **Improvement:** we added a tiny "freshness head" (65 parameters) that tells the model how old each article is. NRMS can't know that from titles alone.
  - **Result:** EB-NeRD AUC 0.543 → 0.611, with a 95% confidence interval that clearly excludes zero. MIND shows no gain, because articles within one MIND impression are all about the same age, so age can't reorder them.
  
  ## 4. Measuring it (`scripts/`)
  - **`extended_eval.py`:** all 7 metrics with confidence intervals, split into new vs returning users and popular vs niche articles.
  - **`serving_benchmark.py`:** speed and memory. A request takes ~9 ms at p99, against a 100 ms target. Building features is most of that time, not the model. The first thing to break at 10× traffic is memory for the article-statistics index.
  
  ## 5. Leaderboard files (`run_submit.py`)
  - The leaderboards score rankings of *given* article lists, which is exactly what NRMS does. So the freshness NRMS produces the submissions.
  - It encodes every article once and reuses those vectors, instead of re-encoding per impression. That turns ~19 hours of scoring into ~7 minutes.
  - `verify_fast_scorer.py` checks the shortcut gives exactly the same metrics as the slow path.
  
  **In one sentence:** clean and time-split the logs, shortlist fresh articles and re-rank them with behaviour features, separately improve NRMS by telling it article age, and use that NRMS for the leaderboard submissions.

● Doing Q9. First I need to decide which of the 22 features count as "unavailable at serving time", since that call is the real substance here. I'm checking the two candidates I flagged against the raw data definitions:

● Both datasets are done. Updating `todo.md` and the README, then running the tests:

● Q9 is done: metrics with and without features a live system wouldn't have, with paired 95% CIs, on both datasets. The main finding is that one feature was inflating the EB-NeRD re-ranker: without it, the gain over stage 1 drops to roughly 60% of the headline figure, though it's still clearly significant.
  
  **Features dropped** (evidence is in the script's docstring):
  - **`impression_size`:** on EB-NeRD this is the number of articles that came into view, which grows the longer the user stays on the page. It correlates 0.50 (Spearman) with that impression's own read time, which is only known afterwards.
  - **`has_known_publish_time`:** it reads a later snapshot of publish times that a live system wouldn't have.
  - **`session_clicks_before`:** a stricter third arm, since this feature applies no reporting lag.
  
  **Results** (test split; the `all` arm reproduces the committed reports to within 1e-13):
  
  | | EB-NeRD MRR | EB-NeRD nDCG@10 | MIND MRR | MIND nDCG@10 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | Stage 1 (before re-ranking) | 0.1639 | 0.1994 | 0.2237 | 0.2932 |
  | Re-ranker, all 22 features (reported) | 0.2102 | 0.2691 | 0.2239 | 0.2980 |
  | **Re-ranker, serving-safe** (20 features) | **0.1915** | **0.2407** | 0.2234 | 0.2951 |
  | Serving-safe minus all | **−0.0187** [−0.0210, −0.0163] | −0.0284 [−0.0310, −0.0258] | −0.0005 [−0.0018, +0.0007] | −0.0029 [−0.0041, −0.0017] |
  
  **What it means for the report:**
  - **EB-NeRD:** `impression_size` was inflating the headline. Without it, the gain over stage 1 falls from MRR +0.0463 to **+0.0275 [+0.0248, +0.0305]**. That's still clearly significant, just at about 60% of the size. The honest EB-NeRD claim is now "+17% MRR over stage 1" (0.1639 → 0.1915), not +28%.
  - **MIND:** there was no MRR gain to lose. The small AUC drop (−0.0043) matches `impression_size` holding 12% of the model's gain share, but ranking at the top barely moves.
  - **`has_known_publish_time` had 0% gain share on both datasets.** That confirms the §2E claim that the future-article shortcut is gone for fresh-pool candidates.
  - **`session_clicks_before`** made no measurable difference, so the stricter arm matches serving-safe.
  - **NRMS isn't affected.** It uses none of these features. It ranks the given in-view list, which is exactly what the leaderboards score, so the in-view list is its input by design rather than a leak.
  
  This also closes the separate todo item about missing paired CIs for the re-ranker's gains over stage 1: they're in each JSON under `deltas_vs_stage1`.
  
  **Files:** `scripts/serving_features_ablation.py` (new) and `results/serving_features_ablation_{ebnerd,mind}.json`. `README.md` and `todo.md` are updated. All 141 tests pass. None of it is committed yet.

❯ [ ] Q1 feature-group ablation under the chosen 49-random-negative sampling (§2E's candidate-level-only arm ran under the discarded hard negatives).
  
  
  wait what is this?

● It's a leftover from the re-ranker's original plan, and it's **no longer required**.
  
  **Where it comes from.** Back in §1.4 (signed off 2026-09-10), the plan was for the re-ranker to carry the Q3 "improve + ablation" requirement. The ablation would switch off groups of Q1 features (user history, session, article popularity, freshness, category match) and measure how much each group contributes.
  
  **Why it's marked unfinished.** §2E contains one attempt at it: a "candidate-level features only" arm that dropped all the per-impression features (history, session, dwell, time of day). It scored badly (test MRR 0.1018 vs 0.1415). But that run used **hard-negative sampling**, which §2E later found to be a bug and replaced with 49 random negatives. So that number was measured under a broken training setup and doesn't cleanly isolate the feature groups. Hence the note: redo it under the sampling actually shipped.
  
  **Why it's no longer required.** On 2026-09-11 (§2F) the Q3 improvement moved to **NRMS freshness weighting**. That has its own clean ablation (identical init and data order, only the 65-parameter head differs) and the paired CI Q3 asks for. So:
  
  - **Q3.3's ablation:** already satisfied, by NRMS.
  - **Q1:** asks you to *engineer* the features, not to ablate them.
  
  **It would still be useful** for the report's "choices and observations", because it answers "which behavioural signals actually matter?". The feature-importance numbers hint at the answer (freshness ~53% and `position_bias` ~23% of EB-NeRD gain), but gain share isn't a measured metric change, as Q9 just showed with `impression_size`.
  
  **It's cheap now.** The Q9 script is already a feature-ablation harness: train arms that differ only in their feature list, then report paired CIs. Adding "drop one group" arms would take about 15 minutes of compute for both datasets. Six groups:
  - history: `click_count`, recency, dwell
  - session: `session_*`
  - time: hour, day of week
  - popularity: clicks, displays, CTR
  - freshness
  - category match
  
  I'd run it on top of the serving-safe feature set, so the numbers line up with §2J.
  
  Want me to run it, or drop the item from `todo.md` as superseded by §2F?

❯ are you sure that titles and embeddings aren't being used? go through the codebase and see what is happening

● Good challenge. I made that claim from the re-ranker's feature list alone, so let me trace every place history titles and embeddings enter the pipeline:

● I overstated it. Titles and embeddings of the user's clicked articles *are* used, in three places. What's missing is narrower: a title/embedding signal inside the **shipped** re-ranker.
  
  ## Where they're used
  
  **1. Stage-1 retrieval, then passed into the re-ranker as a feature**
  - `bm25` and `bm25_fresh`: the query is built from the **titles of the user's last 10 clicks** (`BM25_CANDIDATE_CONFIG = {"fields": ["title"], "window": 10}`) and scored with BM25.
  - `semantic`: the user vector is the **pooled embeddings of their clicked articles** (MIND mean-pool, EB-NeRD max-pool), and candidates are ranked by cosine similarity.
  - In each case that similarity becomes the candidate's `retrieval_score` (plus `retrieval_rank`), which the re-ranker takes as an input feature (`candidate_features.py:77`). So the re-ranker trained on those arms *does* receive a history-title or history-embedding similarity per candidate.
  
  **2. NRMS, the Q3 model and your leaderboard submissions**
  - Its user encoder reads the **text of the user's last 20 clicked articles**: title+abstract on MIND, title+subtitle+body on EB-NeRD.
  - It turns each into a vector through a learned **word-embedding** layer (initialised from BERT / XLM-R), then attends over them.
  - That's the most direct form of "user's recent clicked articles (titles, embeddings)" in the whole project.
  
  **3. What's genuinely absent: the shipped `popular` re-ranker**
  - For `popular` candidates, `retrieval_score` is the article's **lagged click count** (`fresh_pool.py:142`), not a similarity.
  - No other feature compares a candidate with the user's history text or embeddings. The only history-content signal there is category (`category_affinity`, `category_binary_match`).
  
  ## What that means for Q1.1
  
  It's covered across the system:
  - **Titles:** BM25 arms and NRMS.
  - **Embeddings:** semantic arm and NRMS.
  - **Categories:** every re-ranker arm.
  
  The only fair criticism is that the **final shipped re-ranker** doesn't combine fresh-pool candidates with a history-similarity feature. That's why DesignChoices §2.6 and `todo.md` list the bi-encoder feature as a nice-to-have, not as a gap in Q1.
  
  So it isn't a missing requirement. It's an optional improvement, which I'd move to the "nice to have" list. Adding it as an extra arm is still possible if you want it, but the report can honestly state that Q1.1 is implemented and point to these three paths.

❯ okay for the commands i can run on GPU, but there are multiple GPUS, so i need to specify CUDA_AVAILABLE_DEVICES. ill run with manual python commands

● One correction first: **nothing in this rerun uses the GPU.** The re-ranker is LightGBM on CPU, feature building is pandas and numpy, and the new features are BM25 plus numpy dot products. The extended eval, Q9 and the serving benchmark are CPU too. Only NRMS uses CUDA, and it isn't part of this rerun.
  
  So you don't need to pick a GPU. The variable is `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES`, by the way, not `CUDA_AVAILABLE_DEVICES`. Setting it to empty is still a good habit on a shared box, so nothing grabs a GPU by accident. What matters more on a shared server is **CPU threads**: LightGBM uses every core by default, so cap it.
  
  Manual commands, in order, run from `~/IIIT-Assignments/ayush/IRE_A2` (swap `.venv/bin/python` for `python` if you use conda):
  
  ```bash
  export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=""      # CPU-only job; keeps GPUs free for others
  export OMP_NUM_THREADS=16           # cap LightGBM/numpy threads on a shared machine; adjust
  P=.venv/bin/python
  
  # 0. sanity + backup of what gets overwritten
  $P -m pytest -q tests/                                   # expect 148 passed
  mkdir -p data/backup_pre_history_content
  cp -r data/processed/mind/reranker   data/backup_pre_history_content/mind_reranker
  cp -r data/processed/ebnerd/reranker data/backup_pre_history_content/ebnerd_reranker
  cp -r results data/backup_pre_history_content/
  
  # 1. re-rankers: 4 methods x 2 datasets (~20 min); keep each summary row
  mkdir -p log/rerun_history_content
  for d in ebnerd mind; do for m in popular bm25_fresh bm25 semantic; do
    $P -u -m src.reranker --dataset $d --method $m 2>&1 | tee log/rerun_history_content/reranker_${d}_${m}.log
    cp data/processed/reranker_summary_test.csv log/rerun_history_content/summary_${d}_${m}.csv
  done; done
  $P -c "import glob,pandas as pd; pd.concat([pd.read_csv(f) for f in sorted(glob.glob('log/rerun_history_content/summary_*.csv'))]).to_csv('data/processed/reranker_summary_test.csv', index=False)"
  
  # 2. Q9 ablation incl. the no_history_content arm (~15 min)
  for d in ebnerd mind; do $P -u scripts/serving_features_ablation.py --dataset $d 2>&1 | tee log/rerun_history_content/q9_$d.log; done
  
  # 3. extended evaluation (~10 min)
  for d in ebnerd mind; do for m in popular bm25; do
    $P -u scripts/extended_eval.py --dataset $d --method $m 2>&1 | tee log/rerun_history_content/extended_${d}_${m}.log
  done; done
  
  # 4. tests against the new artifacts
  $P -m pytest -q tests/
  ```
  
  Run it inside `tmux` or `screen`, or wrap each loop in `nohup`, so a dropped SSH session doesn't kill it.
  
  **I deliberately left out the serving benchmark (step 4 of the script).** Its latency, QPS and cost figures describe whatever machine runs it, and §2G's numbers were all measured on your laptop. Timing on the server would mix hardware into the before/after comparison, and a many-core machine would flatter the numbers. Instead, once you've pulled the new models back with the step 4 rsync from before, run it here. It takes about a minute per dataset:
  ```bash
  .venv/bin/python scripts/serving_benchmark.py --dataset ebnerd
  .venv/bin/python scripts/serving_benchmark.py --dataset mind
  ```
  
  **Thread cap and reproducibility.** Keep `OMP_NUM_THREADS` the same for every step. LightGBM's result can differ slightly with thread count. Within one run, everything is consistent: the Q9 script checks that its `all` arm reproduces the report trained minutes earlier with the same setting.

  **Quick way to check instead of guessing.** Run this in the repo root on both laptops and compare the one hash each prints:
  ```bash
  cd /path/to/IRE_A2
  ( cd data/processed && ls */articles.parquet */behaviors_*.parquet */candidates_*.parquet \
      */popularity.parquet */feature_store/embeddings.parquet | sort | xargs sha256sum ) | sha256sum
  ```
  - **Same hash:** their data is byte-identical to yours, so `git pull` is enough and nothing needs sending.
  - **Different hash, or "No such file"** (e.g. no `embeddings.parquet` or no `candidates_semantic_*` files): either send the data with the rsync command, or they rebuild it themselves. The rebuild is deterministic, so it produces the same files:
    ```bash
    python build_pipeline.py --skip-download     # needs the raw data in data/raw; includes the embeddings stage
    python -m src.candidates --method all        # ~20 min
    ```
    After that, the hash should match yours.
  
  Their `reranker/` folders don't need to match: the rerun regenerates them.

EB-NeRD's provided BERT vectors sit in a narrow cone where random pairs already score 0.95, so cosine barely separates anything.

❯ first verify nothing is leaky, and then update the design note

● Good call — verify first. I'll do three things: audit what the new features read, run the leakage tests, and run an end-to-end check on real data that no feature depends on click outcomes.

● Now the strongest check: an end-to-end test on real EB-NeRD data that flipping *click outcomes* cannot change any feature. I'll copy a subset to a temp dir, flip the labels of the later impressions, rebuild the feature matrix, and compare. Any future-click leakage would show up as changed features.

● Clean result, but it needs one more step to be airtight. Reading it:
  
  - **The 150 earlier impressions: zero changes across all 24 features.** Flipping later clicks changed nothing about them, so there's no future-click leakage.
  - **The new features didn't change anywhere**, since they never read labels.
  - Four features changed *within the flipped set*: `session_clicks_before`, `click_count_article`, `log_click_count_article`, `ctr_article`. That's expected, not a leak: I flipped all 150 later impressions at once, so an impression late in that set legitimately sees changed clicks from *earlier* impressions. The open question is whether an impression's **own** outcome leaks into its own features.
  
  Testing exactly that: flip a single impression's clicks and check its own feature row is untouched.

● Leakage verification is clean on all four checks:
  
  | Check | Result |
  |---|---|
  | **Code audit** — what the new features read | Only `history`, catalogue titles and embeddings. No labels, clicks, read time or scroll anywhere in `history_content.py` |
  | **Behaviour-window boundary** on real data | **0 of 713,391** EB-NeRD history clicks happen at or after their own impression |
  | **No future leakage** — flip the clicks of 150 later impressions | **0 of 24 features changed** for the 150 earlier impressions |
  | **No own-outcome leakage** — flip one impression's own clicks | **0 of 24 features changed** in its own 200 rows |
  
  The last two are the important ones, because they test the built feature matrix on real data rather than the intent of the code. The four features that did move inside the flipped set (`session_clicks_before`, `click_count_article`, `log_click_count_article`, `ctr_article`) moved for the right reason: I flipped 150 impressions at once, so later ones legitimately saw changed *earlier* clicks. The single-impression test confirms an impression's own outcome never reaches its own features.
  
  Plus the 58 leakage-focused tests pass (`test_history_content`, `test_article_stats_scope`, `test_fresh_pool`, `test_freshness`, `test_feature_engineering`), which is what A2 Q9 asks you to ship.

  | Behaviour-window boundary, real data | **0 of 713,391** EB-NeRD history clicks at or after their own impression |

  | No own-outcome leakage: flipped one impression's clicks | **0 of 24 features changed** in its own rows |

  The last two matter most, because they test the built matrix on real data rather than the code's intent.   ## Design note updated

❯ can you explain the entire project step by step now

● Here's the whole thing, end to end.
  
  ## 0. What the assignment asks for
  
  Build a **two-stage news recommender** on two datasets (EB-NeRD, Danish; MIND, English), evaluate it honestly, and write it up. The nine questions map onto: features (Q1), retrieve-then-rank (Q2), a neural baseline plus an improvement (Q3), serving cost (Q4), evaluation beyond accuracy (Q5), the design note (Q6), submissions and logs (Q7), and leakage discipline (Q9).
  
  Everything is built on top of A1's retrieval pipeline, which is why a chunk of `src/` exists but isn't on A2's critical path.
  
  ## 1. Data pipeline — `build_pipeline.py`
  
  Five steps, both datasets, shared schema:
  
  1. **download** → `data/raw/`
  2. **parse** → one unified schema (`articles`, `behaviors`, `history`) in `data/interim/`
  3. **split** → temporal, never shuffled. The later provided period becomes **test** (untouched for model selection); the last 10% of the earlier period by time becomes **val**. Strict `train < val < test`.
  4. **feature store** → per-article text and embeddings
  5. **popularity + embeddings index** → `popularity_top.parquet`, plus BM25 and ANN indexes
  
  The split is the foundation of everything else: because it's temporal, "don't look into the future" is a real constraint rather than a formality.
  
  ## 2. Stage 1 — candidate generation (`src/fresh_pool.py`, `src/candidates.py`)
  
  For each impression, produce 200 candidates. Four methods exist; **`popular` is shipped**, the others are ablation arms.
  
  The important finding here: A1's catalogue-wide retrieval recovers the clicked article for only **2–4%** of impressions at K=200. News doesn't work like search — people click what's in circulation *right now* (EB-NeRD's median clicked article is 3.1 hours old). So `popular` draws candidates from **what the platform displayed in the hour before this impression**, ranked by click count, with a 24h backfill when the 1h pool is thin.
  
  That took recall@200 from ~3% to **97% (EB-NeRD) / 94% (MIND)**.
  
  Two anti-cheating details baked in:
  - a **10-minute click reporting lag** — clicks land after their impression, so very recent clicks wouldn't exist yet at serving time
  - the fresh pool cut the "future article" shortcut down to 4 re-stamped articles
  
  ## 3. Q1 — behavioural features (`src/{article_stats,sessionize,impression_features,candidate_features,feature_pipeline}.py`)
  
  **24 features**, in four groups:
  
  | Group | Examples |
  |---|---|
  | User / history | recency-weighted engagement, history length, category weights |
  | Session | `session_clicks_before`, position in session |
  | Candidate / article | smoothed CTR, click count, age, publish-time flags |
  | History × candidate | category match, **`history_title_bm25`**, **`history_embedding_cosine`** |
  
  The last two were the last gap and got added most recently. Q1.1 asks for history features from "titles, categories, embeddings" — titles and embeddings were already used by the `bm25` and `semantic` stage-1 arms and by NRMS, but the **shipped `popular` re-ranker had never seen them**, because its `retrieval_score` is just a click count. `src/history_content.py` recomputes exactly the A1 similarities against whatever candidates stage 1 produced.
  
  Every feature is computed **as-of strictly before the impression timestamp**. `NaN` always means "no signal", never "dissimilar" — an article with no embedding must not read as orthogonal.
  
  ## 4. Stage 2 — LambdaMART re-ranker (`src/reranker.py`)
  
  LightGBM with `objective="lambdarank"`, truncation level 200, 49 random negatives per positive during training, MIND capped at 60k train impressions for memory. Evaluated in chunks so the feature matrix never has to fit in RAM all at once.
  
  **Results (test, `popular`, stage 1 → re-ranked):**
  
  | | AUC | MRR | nDCG@5 | nDCG@10 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | EB-NeRD before | 0.8815 | 0.1639 | 0.1471 | 0.1994 |
  | EB-NeRD after | **0.9219** | **0.2115** | 0.2026 | 0.2705 |
  | MIND before | 0.8655 | 0.2237 | 0.2451 | 0.2932 |
  | MIND after | **0.8891** | **0.2403** | 0.2618 | 0.3150 |
  
  Paired bootstrap over impressions (2,000 resamples): EB-NeRD MRR **+0.0476 [+0.0442, +0.0510]**, MIND **+0.0167 [+0.0149, +0.0183]**. Both clear of zero.
  
  That MIND number is the headline of the last week of work. Before the history-content features, MIND's re-ranker gained **+0.0003** with a CI spanning zero — the design note said "MIND is a wash". It wasn't a property of MIND; it was a missing feature. Dropping the two features now costs MIND **−0.0164** and EB-NeRD only −0.0013.
  
  Why the split? **Embedding geometry.** EB-NeRD's provided multilingual BERT vectors have a random-pair cosine of **0.951** — the space is so anisotropic that everything looks similar to everything, so the cosine feature carries almost no information. MIND's MiniLM vectors sit at **0.055**. A bi-encoder feature is only as good as the geometry of the space it reads.
  
  ## 5. Q3 — NRMS baseline + the improvement (`src/nrms/`, `run_nrms.py`)
  
  A PyTorch reimplementation of NRMS (Wu et al. 2019): multi-head self-attention over title words → news vector; multi-head self-attention over clicked news → user vector; dot product for the score.
  
  The **improvement** is a 65-parameter `FreshnessHead` that feeds article age at impression time into the score.
  
  | | AUC | MRR | nDCG@5 |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | EB-NeRD baseline | 0.5425 | 0.3394 | 0.3770 |
  | EB-NeRD + freshness | **0.6112** | **0.3962** | **0.4427** |
  | MIND baseline | 0.6040 | 0.2750 | 0.2961 |
  | MIND + freshness | 0.6042 | 0.2743 | 0.2949 |
  
  65 parameters buy EB-NeRD +0.069 AUC. MIND gets nothing, because MIND ships no publish times — age has to be proxied by first-seen-in-logs, and `scripts/age_signal.py` quantifies exactly what that proxy costs.
  
  ## 6. Q9 — leakage, verified rather than asserted
  
  Two parts.
  
  **Part one: does anything leak?** Four independent checks, all clean:
  - code audit: the new features read only `history` plus static catalogue text/vectors
  - **0 of 713,391** EB-NeRD history clicks fall at or after their own impression
  - flip the click outcomes of 150 *later* impressions, rebuild the matrix → **0 of 24 features change** in the earlier ones
  - flip a single impression's own labels → **0 of 24 features change** in its own rows
  
  Plus 58 leakage-focused tests.
  
  **Part two: metrics with and without features a live system wouldn't have** (`scripts/serving_features_ablation.py`). Two features are flagged: `impression_size` (on EB-NeRD it's the in-view list, which grows with dwell time — Spearman 0.50 with the impression's own read time, an outcome) and `has_known_publish_time` (read off a later snapshot).
  
  | Arm | EB-NeRD MRR | MIND MRR |
  |---|---|---|
  | all (24 features) | 0.2115 | 0.2403 |
  | serving-safe (22) | 0.1897 | 0.2397 |
  | Δ, paired 95% CI | **−0.0218 [−0.0246, −0.0191]** | −0.0007 [−0.0019, +0.0006] |
  
  So EB-NeRD's headline gain **nearly halves** at serving time (+0.0476 → +0.0258) while MIND's holds. The `all` arm is checked against the committed report to machine precision, so the comparison can't silently drift.
  
  ## 7. Q4 — serving (`scripts/serving_benchmark.py`)
  
  Times the real shipped path, 1,000 requests, 200 candidates each:
  
  | | p50 | p99 | QPS |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | EB-NeRD | 5.97 ms | 10.50 ms | 170 |
  | MIND | 3.47 ms | 8.00 ms | 253 |
  
  Both well inside a 100 ms SLA. Feature building dominates (3.4 ms / 2.7 ms); candidate generation is ~0.1–0.5 ms. The history-content features cost roughly **+1 ms**, and the model growing from 99 to 174 trees costs the rest. Memory is measured per component in isolated subprocesses — and the BM25 and ANN indexes are now genuinely in the shipped path, which reverses an earlier claim in the design note that I left visible rather than quietly rewriting.
  
  The honest read: MIND pays ~25% latency for a real gain; EB-NeRD pays roughly 2× for nothing.
  
  ## 8. Q5 — beyond accuracy, and submissions
  
  `scripts/extended_eval.py` reports seven metrics (accuracy plus coverage, diversity, novelty) across cold-start/warm slices with bootstrap CIs. MIND's cold-start/warm gap has closed since the new features landed — warm users gained most, exactly as a history-derived feature should behave.
  
  `run_submit.py` + `src/submission.py` build the Codabench zips: rank permutations 1..n, per-board inner filenames (`prediction.txt` for MIND, `predictions.txt` for EB-NeRD). `scripts/verify_fast_scorer.py` gates the cached-article-vector fast scorer against the committed metrics before anything ships.
  
  ## 9. Tests — `tests/`
  
  **148 tests.** Unit tests on toy data plus light real-data checks. They run in ~25 s and are the reason the cleanup phases were safe to execute.
  
  ## Where to read more
  
  - `DesignChoices.md` — the decision log; §2E stage 1, §2F freshness, §2G serving, §2H extended eval, §2I submissions, §2J Q9, §2K history-content. Supersessions are dated, not deleted.
  - `CODEBASE_MAP.md` — per-file map with `[A1]` provenance and the design choices that make code *look* redundant but aren't.
  - `design_note.md` — the deliverable write-up.
  
  **Still open:** upload the 4 Codabench zips + screenshots, export the design note to PDF, and the AI usage log.
  
  If it'd help for the demo, I can turn this into a shareable page.

❯ what are the serving and scale analysis findings? (Serving & scale analysis — index memory, p99 retrieval latency, and a back-of-envelope
  cost/QPS at a target SLA)

● Here's the Q4 analysis in full. Methodology first, because it's what makes the numbers defensible: each component is built in **its own process**, so `rss_mib` is resident-set growth attributable to that component once its dependencies are loaded; latency is single-threaded CPU, 1,000 timed requests after 50 warm-up, K=200, on the **shipped `popular` model** (not a proxy).
  
  One deliberate exclusion: stage-1 log replay is timed separately (0.016–0.032 ms/impression) rather than counted in the request. A live system maintains those counters as events arrive, so folding the replay in would measure catch-up, not serving.
  
  ## 1. Index memory
  
  | Component | EB-NeRD | MIND | In shipped path? |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Semantic ANN index | 34.5 MiB | **95.6 MiB** | **yes** — history-embedding cosine |
  | BM25 inverted index | 4.1 MiB | **36.0 MiB** | **yes** — history-title BM25 |
  | As-of article statistics | 4.8 MiB | 68.1 MiB | yes |
  | LightGBM booster | ~2.7 MiB | ~3.1 MiB | yes |
  | Fresh-pool event log | 7.5 MiB | 26.5 MiB | yes |
  | **Total serving footprint** | **~53 MiB** | **~200 MiB** | |
  
  **The finding worth reporting is a reversal.** For most of the project the honest answer to "measure your ANN index" was *the shipped pipeline doesn't have one* — replacing similarity retrieval with a recency window (§2E) had removed both indexes from the serving path entirely. Adding the history-similarity features put them back. MIND's footprint went **68.1 → 199.7 MiB**.
  
  That makes the trade explicit rather than hidden: **132 MiB and ~1 ms per request is the price of making the MIND re-ranker work at all.** Worth it there; not worth it on EB-NeRD.
  
  ## 2. p99 latency
  
  | | stage 1 | features | scoring | **total p50** | **total p99** |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | EB-NeRD | 0.12 ms | 3.42 | 2.58 | **5.97 ms** | **10.50 ms** |
  | MIND | 0.56 ms | 2.72 | 0.14 | **3.47 ms** | **8.00 ms** |
  
  **p99 sits ~10× inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets.**
  
  The distribution is more interesting than the total. **Feature building dominates and stage 1 is negligible** — 0.12 ms of a 5.97 ms EB-NeRD request. The intuitive answer, that retrieval or the GBDT is the bottleneck, is wrong on both counts. Any latency work should target feature assembly, not the index and not the model.
  
  The history-similarity features roughly **doubled** EB-NeRD's p99 (6.44 → 10.50 ms) and raised MIND's by a quarter (6.99 → 8.00 ms), from two causes worth separating:
  - **~+1 ms** is the features themselves — one BM25 query over the catalogue plus a dot product per candidate
  - the rest is **the model growing**, because the extra signal delays early stopping (EB-NeRD 99 → 174 trees)
  
  Set against the ranking results, that's a clean statement: **MIND buys a significant gain for ~25% more serving cost; EB-NeRD pays ~2× for a gain whose CI includes zero.** On EB-NeRD alone these features would not be worth shipping.
  
  ## 3. Cost and QPS at the SLA
  
  | | single-core QPS | p99 | meets 100 ms SLA | $ / 1000 queries |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | EB-NeRD | 170 | 10.50 ms | yes | **$0.000278** |
  | MIND | 253 | 8.00 ms | yes | **$0.000186** |
  
  At $0.17/vCPU-hour. QPS is per core and scales with cores until memory or ingest becomes the limit.
  
  The back-of-envelope: **~$0.0002–0.0003 per thousand recommendation requests**, i.e. a million requests/day costs well under a dollar in compute. The caveat in the report is that this assumes a saturated core — real serving provisions for peak, so **divide by target utilisation to size a fleet** (at 30% utilisation, budget ~3× the cores).
  
  ## 4. How it scales
  
  Two axes, measured separately.
  
  **Candidate count K** (what grows per request):
  
  | K | EB-NeRD total | features | scoring |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 50 | 2.40 ms | 2.04 | 0.29 |
  | 100 | 3.50 ms | 2.59 | 0.81 |
  | 200 | 5.88 ms | 3.67 | 2.09 |
  
  Sub-linear in K — doubling candidates costs ~1.7×, because the per-impression work (building the history query and the user vector) is amortised across candidates. **K is a cheap knob**, which is why candidates are persisted at 200 and K stays tunable without re-running retrieval.
  
  *One caveat I'd flag rather than bury:* MIND's k=50 row reads 5.88 ms, higher than its k=100. The sweep runs without its own warm-up and k=50 is the first point, so that's LightGBM's first-predict allocation, not a real inversion. MIND's k=100 → k=200 (2.70 → 4.66 ms) is the trustworthy pair.
  
  **Event volume** (the axis 10× traffic actually moves along):
  
  | Splits | EB-NeRD index | MIND index | distinct articles |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | train | 2.1 MiB | 40.7 MiB | 2,350 / 18,500 |
  | +val | 2.3 MiB | 46.4 MiB | 2,478 / 20,288 |
  | +val+test | 4.8 MiB | 68.1 MiB | 3,729 / 22,771 |
  
  This is the key scaling result: the as-of statistics index grows with **distinct articles, not with event count**. MIND holds ~6× EB-NeRD's articles and ~14× its index bytes. So 10× the *traffic* barely moves memory; 10× the *catalogue* does — and the BM25 and ANN indexes now in the shipped path scale the same way.
  
  ## The three findings, compressed
  
  1. **Latency is not where you'd guess.** Feature assembly is 60–75% of the request; retrieval is under 10%. p99 clears the SLA by 10×, so there is no latency problem to solve — and the measurement is what establishes that rather than assuming it.
  2. **The index-memory answer changed mid-project, and the reversal is the substance.** A recency-window stage 1 had eliminated both indexes; the history features reinstated them at 132 MiB on MIND. Keeping the earlier claim visible with a dated supersession is more useful than a clean-looking rewrite.
  3. **Cost per query is negligible; memory scales with catalogue, not traffic.** The binding constraint at 10× is the number of distinct articles in the as-of and similarity structures — not QPS, and not the model.
  
  Raw numbers: `results/serving_{ebnerd,mind}.json`. Narrative: `design_note.md` §7 and §8, `DesignChoices.md` §2G.

❯ \section{Serving and scale}
  
  \subsection{How it was measured}
  Each component was built in its own process, so memory is the resident-set growth of that component after its dependencies are loaded. Latency is single-threaded CPU time on the shipped model, over 1,000 timed requests after 50 warm-up requests, with $K=200$ candidates. Stage-1 log replay is timed separately (0.016--0.032 ms per impression) and not counted in the request. A live system updates those counters as events arrive, so including it would measure catch-up and not serving.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      Semantic ANN index (history-embedding cosine) & 34.5 MiB & 95.6 MiB \\
      BM25 inverted index (history-title) & 4.1 MiB & 36.0 MiB \\
      As-of article statistics & 4.8 MiB & 68.1 MiB \\
      LightGBM booster & $\sim$2.7 MiB & $\sim$3.1 MiB \\
      Fresh-pool event log & 7.5 MiB & 26.5 MiB \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total serving footprint} & \textbf{$\sim$53 MiB} & \textbf{$\sim$200 MiB} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  The answer to ``how big is your ANN index'' changed during the project. Replacing similarity retrieval with a recency window (Stage 1) removed both indexes from the serving path. Adding the history-similarity features brought them back, and MIND's footprint went from 68.1 to 199.7 MiB. So the price of making the MIND re-ranker work is about 132 MiB and about 1 ms per request. This is worth it on MIND but not on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring} & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 ms & 3.42 ms & 2.58 ms & 5.97 ms & 10.50 ms \\
      MIND    & 0.56 ms & 2.72 ms & 0.14 ms & 3.47 ms & 8.00 ms \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  p99 is about 10$\times$ below the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. Feature building is the biggest part of a request (60--75\%) and Stage 1 is under 10\%. Retrieval and the GBDT are not the bottleneck, so any latency work should go into feature assembly.
  
  The history-similarity features roughly doubled EB-NeRD's p99 (6.44 $\to$ 10.50 ms) and raised MIND's by a quarter (6.99 $\to$ 8.00 ms). There are two causes:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item About +1 ms is the features themselves: one BM25 query over the catalogue and a dot product per candidate.
    \item The rest is the model growing, because the extra signal delays early stopping (EB-NeRD 99 $\to$ 174 trees).
  \end{itemize}
  Set against the ranking results, MIND gets a significant gain for about 25\% more serving cost. EB-NeRD pays about 2$\times$ for a gain whose confidence interval includes zero.
  
  \subsection{Cost per query}
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Single-core QPS} & \textbf{p99} & \textbf{Meets 100 ms SLA} & \textbf{\$ / 1000 queries} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 170 & 10.50 ms & yes & \$0.000278 \\
      MIND    & 253 & 8.00 ms  & yes & \$0.000186 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Cost is computed at \$0.17 per vCPU-hour. A million requests a day costs well under a dollar of compute. This assumes a fully used core, so a real deployment should divide by its target utilisation (at 30\%, budget about 3$\times$ the cores).
  
  \subsection{How it scales}
  \textbf{Candidate count $K$.} On EB-NeRD, doubling $K$ costs about 1.7$\times$ because the per-impression work (building the history query and user vector) is shared across candidates. $K$ is therefore a cheap setting to change, and candidates are stored at 200 so $K$ can be tuned without re-running retrieval.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lccc}
      \toprule
      $K$ & \textbf{Total} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring} \\
      \midrule
      50  & 2.40 ms & 2.04 ms & 0.29 ms \\
      100 & 3.50 ms & 2.59 ms & 0.81 ms \\
      200 & 5.88 ms & 3.67 ms & 2.09 ms \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Event volume.} This is the axis that 10$\times$ traffic moves along. The as-of statistics index grows with the number of distinct articles, not with the number of events.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Splits} & \textbf{EB-NeRD index} & \textbf{MIND index} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Distinct articles}} \\
      & & & EB-NeRD & MIND \\
      \midrule
      train        & 2.1 MiB & 40.7 MiB & 2,350 & 18,500 \\
      + val        & 2.3 MiB & 46.4 MiB & 2,478 & 20,288 \\
      + val + test & 4.8 MiB & 68.1 MiB & 3,729 & 22,771 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  MIND has about 6$\times$ the articles of EB-NeRD and about 14$\times$ the index size. So 10$\times$ the traffic barely changes memory, while 10$\times$ the catalogue does. The BM25 and ANN indexes now in the serving path scale the same way.

❯ \section{Serving and scale}
  
  \subsection{How it was measured}
  Each component was built in its own process, so memory is the resident-set growth of that component after its dependencies are loaded. Latency is single-threaded CPU time on the shipped model, over 1,000 timed requests after 50 warm-up requests, with $K=200$ candidates. Stage-1 log replay is timed separately (0.016--0.032 ms per impression) and not counted in the request. A live system updates those counters as events arrive, so including it would measure catch-up and not serving.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      Semantic ANN index (history-embedding cosine) & 34.5 MiB & 95.6 MiB \\
      BM25 inverted index (history-title) & 4.1 MiB & 36.0 MiB \\
      As-of article statistics & 4.8 MiB & 68.1 MiB \\
      LightGBM booster & $\sim$2.7 MiB & $\sim$3.1 MiB \\
      Fresh-pool event log & 7.5 MiB & 26.5 MiB \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total serving footprint} & \textbf{$\sim$53 MiB} & \textbf{$\sim$200 MiB} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  The answer to ``how big is your ANN index'' changed during the project. Replacing similarity retrieval with a recency window (Stage 1) removed both indexes from the serving path. Adding the history-similarity features brought them back, and MIND's footprint went from 68.1 to 199.7 MiB. So the price of making the MIND re-ranker work is about 132 MiB and about 1 ms per request. This is worth it on MIND but not on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring} & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 ms & 3.42 ms & 2.58 ms & 5.97 ms & 10.50 ms \\
      MIND    & 0.56 ms & 2.72 ms & 0.14 ms & 3.47 ms & 8.00 ms \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  p99 is about 10$\times$ below the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. Feature building is the biggest part of a request (60--75\%) and Stage 1 is under 10\%. Retrieval and the GBDT are not the bottleneck, so any latency work should go into feature assembly.
  
  The history-similarity features roughly doubled EB-NeRD's p99 (6.44 $\to$ 10.50 ms) and raised MIND's by a quarter (6.99 $\to$ 8.00 ms). There are two causes:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item About +1 ms is the features themselves: one BM25 query over the catalogue and a dot product per candidate.
    \item The rest is the model growing, because the extra signal delays early stopping (EB-NeRD 99 $\to$ 174 trees).
  \end{itemize}
  Set against the ranking results, MIND gets a significant gain for about 25\% more serving cost. EB-NeRD pays about 2$\times$ for a gain whose confidence interval includes zero.
  
  \subsection{Cost per query}
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Single-core QPS} & \textbf{p99} & \textbf{Meets 100 ms SLA} & \textbf{\$ / 1000 queries} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 170 & 10.50 ms & yes & \$0.000278 \\
      MIND    & 253 & 8.00 ms  & yes & \$0.000186 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Cost is computed at \$0.17 per vCPU-hour. A million requests a day costs well under a dollar of compute. This assumes a fully used core, so a real deployment should divide by its target utilisation (at 30\%, budget about 3$\times$ the cores).
  
  \subsection{How it scales}
  \textbf{Candidate count $K$.} On EB-NeRD, doubling $K$ costs about 1.7$\times$ because the per-impression work (building the history query and user vector) is shared across candidates. $K$ is therefore a cheap setting to change, and candidates are stored at 200 so $K$ can be tuned without re-running retrieval.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lccc}
      \toprule
      $K$ & \textbf{Total} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring} \\
      \midrule
      50  & 2.40 ms & 2.04 ms & 0.29 ms \\
      100 & 3.50 ms & 2.59 ms & 0.81 ms \\
      200 & 5.88 ms & 3.67 ms & 2.09 ms \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Event volume.} This is the axis that 10$\times$ traffic moves along. The as-of statistics index grows with the number of distinct articles, not with the number of events.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Splits} & \textbf{EB-NeRD index} & \textbf{MIND index} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Distinct articles}} \\
      & & & EB-NeRD & MIND \\
      \midrule
      train        & 2.1 MiB & 40.7 MiB & 2,350 & 18,500 \\
      + val        & 2.3 MiB & 46.4 MiB & 2,478 & 20,288 \\
      + val + test & 4.8 MiB & 68.1 MiB & 3,729 & 22,771 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  MIND has about 6$\times$ the articles of EB-NeRD and about 14$\times$ the index size. So 10$\times$ the traffic barely changes memory, while 10$\times$ the catalogue does. The BM25 and ANN indexes now in the serving path scale the same way.

● Two different problems here, and the second one matters more than the length.
  
  ## First: your totals row doesn't sum
  
  EB-NeRD's `~53 MiB` correctly adds all five rows (53.6). MIND's `~200 MiB` only adds three of them — 95.6 + 36.0 + 68.1 = 199.7, leaving out the booster and the fresh-pool log. That 199.7 figure came from `design_note.md` §7, where the table had no fresh-pool row and the number meant "the three indexes". You added a row and kept the old total.
  
  **MIND's five-row total is 229.3 MiB.** Use 53.6 / 229.3, or drop two rows and say "three indexes". A grader who adds the column will find this.
  
  ## What to cut
  
  The principle that resolves most of it: **cut project history, keep deliberate sweeps.** The ANN-index reversal and the 6.44→10.50 before/after are *what changed while you worked*. The K-sweep and the event-volume sweep are *measurements of the final system* — they're the actual scale analysis, so they stay, just as prose rather than floats.

  |---|---|---|

  | K-sweep table → prose | **a whole float** | Do it |

  Four floats to one. Roughly 1.5 pages → half a page.

That's the only sentence in the section that connects cost to whether the feature was worth shipping, which is the point of a serving analysis. One clause, no history.

  ```latex
  p99 sits roughly 10$\times$ inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets, at
  \$0.0002--0.0003 per thousand queries --- under a dollar of compute for a
  million requests a day, before dividing by target utilisation. Feature building
  is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 under 10\%: neither retrieval nor the GBDT
  is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature assembly. The two
  history-similarity features account for about 1 ms and 132 MiB of this, which
  buys MIND a significant ranking gain while EB-NeRD pays the same cost for a gain
  whose confidence interval includes zero.
  
  Cost grows sub-linearly in $K$ (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K=50 /
  100 / 200$), because the per-impression work --- building the history query and
  the user vector --- is shared across candidates; candidates are persisted at 200
  so $K$ stays tunable without re-running retrieval. Memory instead tracks the
  \emph{catalogue}: growing the event log from train to train+val+test moves the
  as-of index 2.1 $\to$ 4.8 MiB on EB-NeRD (2{,}350 $\to$ 3{,}729 distinct
  articles) and 40.7 $\to$ 68.1 MiB on MIND (18{,}500 $\to$ 22{,}771). MIND's
  6$\times$ larger catalogue costs 14$\times$ the index, and the BM25 and ANN
  indexes scale on the same axis --- so 10$\times$ traffic is nearly free, while
  10$\times$ catalogue is not.
  ```

❯ what about the memory footprint of the feature store? and also the local benchmark? and also remove the scaling subsection, i want a separate section for Where the Pipeline Breaks at 10x

● Three things, and answering the first two turned up an omission plus two stale numbers.
  
  ## The feature store is already in your table — split across two rows, with one row missing
  
  `load_embeddings_lookup` returns the embedding matrix *and* the ANN wrapper, and the benchmark measures `obj.embeddings.nbytes`. So your "Semantic ANN index" row (34.5 / 95.6 MiB) **is** the feature store's embedding table — it matches `embeddings.parquet` on disk (36M / 97M) almost exactly. There's no separate ANN structure; `BruteForceANN` is a dot product over that array.
  
  What's genuinely missing is `articles_lookup` — the feature store's **title/abstract text**, at **35.8 / 61.8 MiB**. It's in the shipped path (`benchmark_latency` takes it, `build_impression_features` uses it), so it belongs in the table. That's why I'd relabel both rows by what they hold rather than by the index name.
  
  `behaviors_split` (618 / 376 MiB RSS) stays out: it's the offline frame used to sample benchmark requests, not something a live server holds.
  
  Two corrections while I was in there:
  
  - **Booster is 4.0 / 3.9 MiB**, not 2.7 / 3.1. The old pair predates the retrain — the files are now 1.2 MB on disk with 174 / 168 trees.
  - **`design_note.md` §8 contradicts §7**: §8 still says "165–191 QPS per core" and `2.44 / 3.21 / 5.08 ms` for the K-sweep, against §7's 170 / 253 and the current `2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88`. §8 didn't get fully refreshed in `d5a86b3`. Worth fixing in the note too, not just the report.
  
  ## The local benchmark
  
  Worth stating, because it makes the cost number honest. This ran on an **AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores / 32 threads), 14 GiB RAM**, single-threaded — a laptop CPU with boost clocks, not a steady-state cloud vCPU. So 170 / 253 QPS is an optimistic per-core figure and the $0.17/vCPU-hour rate is borrowed, not measured. One sentence covers it.
  
  ## Revised LaTeX
  
  ```latex
  \section{Serving and scale}
  
  Components were built in isolated processes, so each figure is the exact array
  bytes where walkable and resident-set growth otherwise. Latency is
  single-threaded on an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores, 14 GiB RAM), 1{,}000 timed
  requests after 50 warm-up, $K=200$. Stage-1 log replay (0.02--0.03
  ms/impression) is excluded: a live system maintains those counters as events
  arrive.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      Article text, feature store (impression features) & 35.8 & 61.8 \\
      Article embeddings, feature store (ANN scoring)   & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
      As-of article statistics                          &  4.8 & 68.1 \\
      BM25 inverted index                               &  4.1 & 36.0 \\
      Fresh-pool event log                              &  7.5 & 26.5 \\
      LightGBM booster                                  &  4.0 &  3.9 \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total (MiB)} & \textbf{90.7} & \textbf{291.9} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  The feature store dominates: its text and embedding tables are 78\% of
  EB-NeRD's footprint and 54\% of MIND's. The two structures serving the
  history-similarity features (embeddings and BM25) cost 131.6 MiB on MIND, which
  buys a significant ranking gain there but none on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring}
      & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 & 3.42 & 2.58 & 5.97 & 10.50 \\
      MIND    & 0.56 & 2.72 & 0.14 & 3.47 &  8.00 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Milliseconds, single core, $K=200$.}
  \end{table}
  
  Feature building is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 is under 10\%. Neither
  retrieval nor the GBDT is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature
  assembly.
  
  \subsection{Cost and QPS at the SLA}
  
  p99 is roughly 10$\times$ inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. At 170
  (EB-NeRD) and 253 (MIND) single-core QPS and \$0.17 per vCPU-hour, that is
  \$0.000278 and \$0.000186 per thousand queries --- under a dollar of compute for
  a million requests a day. Two caveats: these are laptop cores under boost, so a
  steady-state cloud vCPU would be slower, and the figure assumes a saturated
  core, so a deployment divides by its target utilisation (at 30\%, about
  3$\times$ the cores).
  
  \section{Where the pipeline breaks at 10$\times$}
  
  Latency is linear in $K$ with a fixed per-impression cost of about 1.2 ms
  (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K = 50 / 100 / 200$; roughly
  $1.2 + 0.023K$). $K$ is therefore the throughput knob, and halving it costs
  little recall (93.8\% at $K=100$ against 97.0\% at $K=200$).
  
  Ranked by what fails first:
  
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{As-of article statistics --- the binding constraint.} It must be
      resident and grows with total events: 68.1 MiB holds 8.9M MIND events, so
      about 680 MiB at 10$\times$ traffic and about 4.4 GB at MINDlarge scale.
      This is the first thing to shard or move to a windowed counter store.
    \item \textbf{CPU for feature assembly.} 170--253 QPS per core means about ten
      cores at 10$\times$. Parallel across requests, so a cost line rather than a
      wall.
    \item \textbf{The feature store, on a different axis.} Its text and embedding
      tables grow with \emph{catalogue size}, not traffic, so 10$\times$ traffic
      does not touch them --- but a 10$\times$ catalogue does, and the embedding
      table would then need an ANN service rather than a resident array.
    \item \textbf{Nothing else.} The fresh pool is bounded by one hour of traffic
      rather than catalogue size, so it grows with QPS and not with corpus age.
      The booster is 4 MiB.
  \end{enumerate}
  
  Two things that break in deployment but are invisible offline: our pool is
  rebuilt by replaying a log, where production needs an incrementally maintained
  counter store with the same 10-minute click lag; and the model is retrained in
  batch, where a one-hour popularity window implies the article statistics drift
  within a single day.
  ```
  
  The one substantive wording change in the 10× section: **"linear in $K$ with a ~1.2 ms fixed cost"** instead of plain "linear". Your measured points aren't proportional to $K$ (2.40 ms at $K=50$ would predict 9.6 ms at $K=200$, not 5.88), but the *marginal* cost is linear — the fit is affine because building the history query and user vector happens once per impression regardless of $K$. Stating it that way is both accurate and explains *why* halving $K$ helps less than you'd expect.

❯ bro yoiu added the feature store but removed ann index, Q4. Serving & Scale Analysis
  Analyse the serving characteristics of your system:
  1. Index memory: Measure the memory footprint of your ANN index and feature store
  2. Latency: Measure p99 retrieval latency (candidate generation + re-ranking) for a single user
  request
  3. Cost/QPS: Back-of-envelope estimate of cost per 1000 queries at a target SLA (e.g., p99 <
  100ms)
  4. Scaling argument: How would your system scale to 10× the current load? What breaks first?
  5. A measured local benchmark plus a scaling argument suffices
  
  
  look at the question again and keep all of this in the latex'

● You're right — Q4.1 names the ANN index and the feature store as two separate things, and I collapsed the first into the second. The awkward bit is that they're physically the same array: `BruteForceANN` has no structure of its own beyond the feature store's embedding matrix. So the fix is to show both labels and say the array is counted once, rather than pick one.
  
  Also worth stating plainly: it's **brute-force exact cosine**, not an approximate index. A grader reading "ANN index" will notice, and owning it costs one clause.
  
  ```latex
  \section{Serving and scale}
  
  Measured locally: components built in isolated processes, so each figure is the
  exact array bytes where walkable and resident-set growth otherwise. Latency is
  single-threaded on an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores, 14 GiB RAM), 1{,}000 timed
  requests after 50 warm-up, $K=200$. Stage-1 log replay (0.02--0.03
  ms/impression) is excluded: a live system maintains those counters as events
  arrive.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{ANN index}} \\
      \quad Embedding matrix (exact cosine over the catalogue) & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Feature store}} \\
      \quad Article text (titles, abstracts)                   & 35.8 & 61.8 \\
      \quad Article embeddings                    & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\footnotesize same array as above, counted once} \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Other resident state}} \\
      \quad BM25 inverted index (history-title feature)        &  4.1 & 36.0 \\
      \quad As-of article statistics                           &  4.8 & 68.1 \\
      \quad Fresh-pool event log                               &  7.5 & 26.5 \\
      \quad LightGBM booster (174 / 168 trees)                 &  4.0 &  3.9 \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total (MiB)} & \textbf{90.7} & \textbf{291.9} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Our ANN index is a brute-force exact cosine over the feature store's embedding
  matrix, so it adds no structure of its own: the 34.5 / 95.6 MiB is the matrix
  itself, and the feature store and the ANN index share it. At 23k articles an
  exact scan is cheaper than an approximate index; the trade reverses with
  catalogue size, which is the scaling point below.
  
  The feature store dominates: text plus embeddings are 78\% of EB-NeRD's
  footprint and 54\% of MIND's. The two structures serving the history-similarity
  features (embeddings and BM25) cost 131.6 MiB on MIND, which buys a significant
  ranking gain there but none on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring}
      & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 & 3.42 & 2.58 & 5.97 & 10.50 \\
      MIND    & 0.56 & 2.72 & 0.14 & 3.47 &  8.00 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Milliseconds for a single user request, single core, $K=200$.
    Stage 1 is candidate generation, features and scoring are re-ranking.}
  \end{table}
  
  Feature building is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 is under 10\%. Neither
  retrieval nor the GBDT is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature
  assembly.
  
  \subsection{Cost per 1000 queries at the SLA}
  
  p99 is roughly 10$\times$ inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. At 170
  (EB-NeRD) and 253 (MIND) single-core QPS and \$0.17 per vCPU-hour, that is
  \$0.000278 and \$0.000186 per thousand queries --- under a dollar of compute for
  a million requests a day. Two caveats: these are laptop cores under boost, so a
  steady-state cloud vCPU would be slower, and the figure assumes a saturated
  core, so a deployment divides by its target utilisation (at 30\%, about
  3$\times$ the cores).
  
  \section{Where the pipeline breaks at 10$\times$}
  
  Latency is linear in $K$ with a fixed per-impression cost of about 1.2 ms
  (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K = 50 / 100 / 200$; roughly
  $1.2 + 0.023K$). $K$ is therefore the throughput knob, and halving it costs
  little recall (93.8\% at $K=100$ against 97.0\% at $K=200$).
  
  Ranked by what fails first:
  
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{As-of article statistics --- the binding constraint.} It must be
      resident and grows with total events: 68.1 MiB holds 8.9M MIND events, so
      about 680 MiB at 10$\times$ traffic and about 4.4 GB at MINDlarge scale.
      This is the first thing to shard or move to a windowed counter store.
    \item \textbf{CPU for feature assembly.} 170--253 QPS per core means about ten
      cores at 10$\times$. Parallel across requests, so a cost line rather than a
      wall.
    \item \textbf{The ANN index and feature store, on a different axis.} Both grow
      with \emph{catalogue size}, not traffic, so 10$\times$ traffic does not touch
      them --- but a 10$\times$ catalogue does, and it is the point where the
      brute-force scan stops paying and the embedding matrix needs a real ANN
      service (IVF or HNSW) instead of a resident array.
    \item \textbf{Nothing else.} The fresh pool is bounded by one hour of traffic
      rather than catalogue size, so it grows with QPS and not with corpus age.
      The booster is 4 MiB.
  \end{enumerate}
  
  Two things that break in deployment but are invisible offline: our pool is
  rebuilt by replaying a log, where production needs an incrementally maintained
  counter store with the same 10-minute click lag; and the model is retrained in
  batch, where a one-hour popularity window implies the article statistics drift
  within a single day.
  ```
  
  All five of Q4's points are covered: memory with both named components (§ Index memory), p99 for candidate generation + re-ranking with the split labelled in the caption (§ Latency), cost/QPS at p99 < 100 ms (§ Cost), the 10× argument with what breaks first (own section), and the measured-local-benchmark framing in the opening paragraph.
  
  If the shared-array row renders awkwardly in your template, the fallback is to drop that line and add "(shared with the ANN index above)" to the ANN row instead — same information, one less `\multicolumn`.

❯ wait are you sure its not an approximate and it uses the exact values??

● Fair challenge — let me check rather than trust the class name.

● Yes — confirmed exact. `src/ann_index.py:29`:
  
  ```python
  scores = self.embeddings @ q          # full dense matmul, all n_docs
  part = np.argpartition(-scores, k)[:k]  # exact selection, not approximate
  ```
  
  Every document is scored on every query. `argpartition` is introselect — an exact top-k selection, not a sampling or sketching step. `score_for_ids` (line 40) is the same thing restricted to caller-chosen ids: one exact dot product each.
  
  There's no index structure at all — no IVF lists, no HNSW graph, no PQ codebook. That's why the memory figure is just `embeddings.nbytes`: the "index" is the normalised matrix and nothing else. The module docstring says so explicitly, and notes the assignment permits brute force below ~100k articles.
  
  One honest caveat, which is about arithmetic rather than search: vectors are stored `float32`, so scores carry float32 rounding. The *retrieved set* is still exact — no candidate can be missed the way an approximate index can miss one.
  
  So the LaTeX line stands as written. If you want to be maximally precise, "exact cosine by brute-force scan" is better than "exact cosine" alone, since it names the method and pre-empts "where's your ANN index?":
  
  ```latex
      \quad Embedding matrix (brute-force exact cosine scan) & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
  ```
  
  And the justification sentence could carry the threshold, since it's the reason the choice is defensible rather than lazy:
  
  ```latex
  Our ANN index is a brute-force exact cosine scan over the feature store's
  embedding matrix, so it adds no structure of its own: the 34.5 / 95.6 MiB is
  the matrix itself, shared between the ANN index and the feature store. Both
  catalogues are under 100k articles, where a single matrix multiply is exact and
  faster than an approximate index; the trade reverses with catalogue size, which
  is the scaling point below.
  ```

● Here's the complete section with the ANN wording corrected:
  
  ```latex
  \section{Serving and scale}
  
  Measured locally: components built in isolated processes, so each figure is the
  exact array bytes where walkable and resident-set growth otherwise. Latency is
  single-threaded on an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores, 14 GiB RAM), 1{,}000 timed
  requests after 50 warm-up, $K=200$. Stage-1 log replay (0.02--0.03
  ms/impression) is excluded: a live system maintains those counters as events
  arrive.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{ANN index}} \\
      \quad Embedding matrix (brute-force exact cosine scan) & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Feature store}} \\
      \quad Article text (titles, abstracts)                 & 35.8 & 61.8 \\
      \quad Article embeddings      & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\footnotesize same array as above, counted once} \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Other resident state}} \\
      \quad BM25 inverted index (history-title feature)      &  4.1 & 36.0 \\
      \quad As-of article statistics                         &  4.8 & 68.1 \\
      \quad Fresh-pool event log                             &  7.5 & 26.5 \\
      \quad LightGBM booster (174 / 168 trees)               &  4.0 &  3.9 \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total (MiB)} & \textbf{90.7} & \textbf{291.9} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Our ANN index is a brute-force exact cosine scan over the feature store's
  embedding matrix, so it adds no structure of its own: the 34.5 / 95.6 MiB is
  the matrix itself, shared between the ANN index and the feature store. Both
  catalogues are under 100k articles, where a single matrix multiply is exact and
  faster than an approximate index; the trade reverses with catalogue size, which
  is the scaling point below.
  
  The feature store dominates: text plus embeddings are 78\% of EB-NeRD's
  footprint and 54\% of MIND's. The two structures serving the history-similarity
  features (embeddings and BM25) cost 131.6 MiB on MIND, which buys a significant
  ranking gain there but none on EB-NeRD.

  \begin{table}[htbp]

    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring}
      & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 & 3.42 & 2.58 & 5.97 & 10.50 \\
      MIND    & 0.56 & 2.72 & 0.14 & 3.47 &  8.00 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Milliseconds for a single user request, single core, $K=200$.
    Stage 1 is candidate generation, features and scoring are re-ranking.}
  \end{table}
  
  Feature building is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 is under 10\%. Neither
  retrieval nor the GBDT is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature
  assembly.
  ```

❯ yes verify those two numbers

● Both check out, and the first one is better than "verified" — it's exact.
  
  ## 8.9M MIND events ✓
  
  I counted the actual `(article, display)` pairs the index ingests across all three splits:
  
  | | impressions | display events | click events | total |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | MIND | 230,117 | 8,584,442 | 347,727 | **8,932,169** |
  | EB-NeRD | 50,080 | 583,054 | 50,273 | 633,327 |
  
  8,932,169 × 8 B = **68.1 MiB**, reproducing the table's MIND figure to the decimal. EB-NeRD: 633,327 × 8 B = **4.83 MiB**, matching its 4.8. So "8 bytes per event" isn't an approximation — `array.array("q")` holds raw int64 with no per-event Python object, which is exactly what the module docstring claims.
  
  Note it's displays **plus** clicks (two arrays per article), not displays alone — displays alone would give 65.5 MiB, not 68.1.
  
  The `~65×` MINDlarge multiplier also holds internally: 230,117 ≈ 230k, and 15M/230k = 65.2. That one rests on MINDlarge's published impression count rather than anything I measured here, so it's the only figure in the section I can't check from the repo.
  
  ## 93.8% / 97.0% recall ✓
  
  `DesignChoices.md:671`, the `popular` arm's recall@50 / @100 / @200:
  
  | arm | EB-NeRD | MIND |
  |---|---|---|
  | `popular` — 1 h pool, lagged clicks | 83.4 / **93.8** / **97.0%** | 81.6 / 88.6 / **93.9%** |
  
  Correct as quoted, for EB-NeRD.
  
  ## One tweak worth making
  
  Both figures in that sentence are EB-NeRD, but only the latency half says so — and MIND's recall pair is different (88.6 → 93.9). Naming the dataset once avoids the reader assuming the recall claim is universal:
  
  ```latex
  Latency is linear in $K$ with a fixed per-impression cost of about 1.2 ms
  (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K = 50 / 100 / 200$; roughly
  $1.2 + 0.023K$). $K$ is therefore the throughput knob, and on EB-NeRD halving
  it costs little recall (93.8\% at $K=100$ against 97.0\% at $K=200$; MIND
  88.6\% against 93.9\%).
  ```
  
  And since 8.93M is exact rather than a memory-derived estimate, item 1 can state it as a count:
  
  ```latex
      resident and grows with total events: MIND's 8.93M display and click events
      occupy 68.1 MiB at 8 bytes each, so about 680 MiB at 10$\times$ traffic and
      about 4.4 GB at MINDlarge scale ($\sim$65$\times$).
  ```
  
  That phrasing also makes the 8 B/event basis explicit, which is what makes the 10× and 65× extrapolations more than hand-waving.

❯ \section{Stage 1: Candidate generation}
  \subsection{The Problem}
      Assignment 1 retrieved the top-k similarity between the user's click history and the whole catalogue. We used the same pipeline to generate the top-200 candidates but the recall was very low  2.55\% (EB-NeRD) \& 2.90\% (MIND). The best configuration gave around 5\%.
  \subsection{The fix}
  
  
  i need to add to the section of The fix

● `DesignChoices.md` §2E has everything. Here's the subsection:
  
  ```latex
  \subsection{The fix}
  
  The ceiling was not inherent to the data --- it came from \emph{where} stage 1
  searched and \emph{what} it ranked by. Clicks go to whatever is in circulation
  at that moment: EB-NeRD's median clicked article is \textbf{3.1 hours old} at
  click time and 92\% are clicked within a day of publication, while A1 searched a
  catalogue reaching back to 2000. Two mistakes compounded.
  
  \textbf{Wrong search space.} Restricting A1's \emph{unchanged} BM25 to the
  articles the platform displayed in the previous hour lifts EB-NeRD recall@200
  from 2.55\% to 84.2\%, with no change to the scoring.
  
  \textbf{Wrong ranking signal.} Ranking that same pool by recent popularity
  instead of history similarity reaches 97.0\% / 93.9\%. History similarity adds
  nothing on top of it.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{EB-NeRD @50/@100/@200} & \textbf{MIND @50/@100/@200} \\
      \midrule
      \texttt{bm25} --- A1, whole catalogue   & 0.82 / 1.41 / \textbf{2.55\%}  & 1.27 / 1.96 / \textbf{2.90\%} \\
      \texttt{bm25\_fresh} --- A1 scoring, 1 h pool & 21.9 / 42.9 / \textbf{84.2\%} & 10.0 / 14.2 / \textbf{21.0\%} \\
      \texttt{popular} --- 1 h pool, lagged clicks & 83.4 / 93.8 / \textbf{97.0\%} & 81.6 / 88.6 / \textbf{93.9\%} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test recall@K over full populations. \texttt{bm25} reproduces the
    Assignment 1 figures exactly, so the arms are like-for-like.}
  \end{table}
  
  The pool is built only from events strictly before the impression time $t$:
  displays in $[t - 1\text{h},\, t)$; clicks from impressions in
  $[t - 1\text{h},\, t - 10\text{min})$, because a click lands after the
  impression that produced it; and a 24-hour display backfill when the one-hour
  pool holds fewer than $K$ articles. The window was chosen by measurement --- on
  2{,}000-impression samples 1 h beat both 6 h and 24 h, because longer windows let
  stale-but-popular articles crowd out fresh ones --- and the 10-minute click
  reporting lag cost 0 pp on EB-NeRD and about 1 pp on MIND.
  
  Two consequences are worth recording. First, \textbf{history similarity is too
  sparse to rank a fresh pool}: on MIND a title query scores a median of only
  \textasciitilde20 of \textasciitilde2{,}000 pool articles above zero, so
  \texttt{bm25\_fresh}'s top-200 is about 90\% ties, and its recall swung between
  10\% and 92\% depending purely on the tie-breaking rule. It now breaks ties with
  a seeded random draw, the only rule under which the arm measures BM25 and
  nothing else. Second, \textbf{the future-article shortcut disappears}: catalogue
  retrieval could return articles published after the impression (10.7\% of the
  catalogue per EB-NeRD test impression), whereas in \texttt{popular} candidates
  that falls to 0.003\% of rows --- all from four articles whose
  \texttt{published\_time} was bulk re-stamped after they were displayed. The
  guarantee the pool gives is ``displayed strictly before $t$'', not ``published
  before $t$''.
  ```
  
  Three things I'd keep even under length pressure, since they're what separates this from "we swapped the retriever and it got better":
  
  - **The `bm25_fresh` middle row.** It isolates the two mistakes — same scoring, different pool, 2.55 → 84.2 — so the table shows *which* change bought what, rather than one before/after jump.
  - **The tie-breaking paragraph.** A result that swings 10%→92% on the tie rule is a measurement artefact; saying so is what makes the 84.2% trustworthy.
  - **The future-article sentence.** It's your Q9 evidence that the recall gain isn't leakage, which is the first thing a skeptical reader would suspect of a 2.55 → 97.0 jump.
  
  One wording note: your problem statement says "the best configuration gave around 5%" — §2E's best A1 arm is 2.90% at K=200. If the 5% is a different K or a different metric, worth labelling it so the 2.55/2.90 in the table doesn't look like it contradicts the line above.
