● Checked `ire-a1/DESIGN_NOTE.tex`. Two things came out of it, one of which is a correction.
  
  ## Your "around 5%" is A1's EB-NeRD BM25, on a different bundle
  
  A1's table (`DESIGN_NOTE.tex:100`):
  
  | dataset | method | R@200 |
  |---|---|---|
  | `mind_small` | BM25 | 0.0213 |
  | `mind_small` | MiniLM | **0.0283** |
  | `ebnerd_small` | BM25 | **0.0439** |
  | `ebnerd_small` | MiniLM | 0.0332 |
  | `ebnerd_small` | word2vec | 0.0358 |
  | `ebnerd_small` | mBERT | 0.0227 |
  
  So the best was **4.39%** (EB-NeRD, BM25), which is your ~5%. But that's `ebnerd_small`, and A2 runs the **demo** bundle (`DesignChoices.md:470`). That's why A1's 4.39% and A2's 2.55% don't line up. Worth one clause so the table doesn't look like it contradicts the sentence above it.
  
  **Also: your caption is wrong.** `bm25` reproduces §0, which is our *own* A2 rerun of the A1 pipeline on A2's splits, not A1's published figures. As written it claims something the A1 note disproves.
  
  ## Rewritten, no em-dashes, in your style
  
  ```latex
  \subsection{The fix}
  
  The ceiling was not in the data. It came from where stage 1 searched and what it
  ranked by. Clicks go to whatever is in circulation at that moment. EB-NeRD's
  median clicked article is 3.1 hours old when it is clicked, and 92\% are clicked
  within a day of publication, while A1 searched a catalogue going back to 2000.
  Two mistakes compounded.
  
  \textbf{1. The search space was wrong.} We restricted A1's BM25, unchanged, to
  the articles the platform displayed in the previous hour. EB-NeRD recall@200
  goes from 2.55\% to 84.2\% on the scoring alone.
  
  \textbf{2. The ranking signal was wrong.} Ranking that same pool by recent
  popularity instead of history similarity reaches 97.0\% and 93.9\%. History
  similarity adds nothing on top of it.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{EB-NeRD @50/@100/@200} & \textbf{MIND @50/@100/@200} \\
      \midrule
      \texttt{bm25}, A1 over the whole catalogue   & 0.82 / 1.41 / \textbf{2.55\%}  & 1.27 / 1.96 / \textbf{2.90\%} \\
      \texttt{bm25\_fresh}, A1 scoring on a 1 h pool & 21.9 / 42.9 / \textbf{84.2\%} & 10.0 / 14.2 / \textbf{21.0\%} \\
      \texttt{popular}, 1 h pool with lagged clicks & 83.4 / 93.8 / \textbf{97.0\%} & 81.6 / 88.6 / \textbf{93.9\%} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test recall@K over full populations. The \texttt{bm25} row reproduces
    our own rerun of the A1 pipeline on the A2 splits, so the three arms are
    like-for-like. It is lower than A1's reported 4.39\% because A1 used
    \texttt{ebnerd\_small} and we use the demo bundle.}
  \end{table}
  
  The pool only uses events from strictly before the impression time $t$. Displays
  count if they fall in $[t - 1\text{h},\, t)$. Clicks count if their impression
  falls in $[t - 1\text{h},\, t - 10\text{min})$, because a click lands after the
  impression that produced it. If the one-hour pool holds fewer than $K$ articles
  we backfill from the previous 24 hours.
  
  We picked the window by measuring it. On 2{,}000-impression samples, 1 hour beat
  both 6 and 24 hours, because longer windows let stale but popular articles crowd
  out fresh ones. The 10-minute click lag cost 0 pp on EB-NeRD and about 1 pp on
  MIND, which is cheap for a guarantee we need anyway.
  ```
  
  Style changes from my earlier draft, matched to the A1 note: numbered `\textbf{n. Claim.}` lead-ins instead of loose bold phrases, "goes from X to Y" and "against" instead of dashes, and the window justification split into its own short paragraph rather than one long sentence with parentheticals. Every `---` is gone, including the ones inside the table's arm names.
  
  If you want the problem statement to match, `4.39\%` is the figure to quote there rather than "around 5\%", with `ebnerd\_small` named.

❯ It is lower than A1's reported 4.39\% because A1 used
    \texttt{ebnerd\_small} and we use the demo bundle.
  
  
  why did we use demo and not small?? which one is bigger?

● **`small` is ~10× bigger**, and the honest answer to "why demo" is that nobody chose it.
  
  | | demo (A2) | small (A1) | ratio |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | users | 1,935 | 18,827 | **9.7×** |
  | click events | 398,218 | 3,756,445 | **9.4×** |
  | articles | 11,777 | 20,738 | 1.8× |
  
  ## Why we're on demo
  
  It's the config default and it was never revisited:
  
  ```python
  EBNERD_BUNDLE = "demo"  # "demo" (fast, default) or "small" (larger, closer to final scale)
  ```
  `src/config.py:39`
  
  `data/raw/ebnerd/` contains only `demo`. A1 downloaded both (`ebnerd_demo.zip` 21M, `ebnerd_small.zip` 81M) and reported `small`. A2 inherited the default and never pulled `small`. There is **no rationale recorded in `DesignChoices.md`** — I grepped for it; the only mentions of "demo" are labels on result tables, not a decision.
  
  So it's an unexamined default that carried across assignments, not a considered trade-off.
  
  ## Why it matters more than it looks
  
  1. **EB-NeRD is where all your null results are.** The history-content features: +0.0013 MRR, CI spanning zero. The Q9 `no_history_content` arm: −0.0013, CI spanning zero. With 1,935 users, low power is a plausible alternative explanation to "no effect" — and you can't distinguish them from this bundle.
  2. **MIND isn't affected.** A1 and A2 both use `MINDsmall`, so the MIND numbers are comparable across assignments. It's only EB-NeRD that shifted.
  3. **The Q4 scaling section** quotes EB-NeRD memory from a 1,935-user bundle, which is why its as-of index is 4.8 MiB against MIND's 68.1.
  
  ## What I'd do, today
  
  **Don't switch.** It's due today, and moving to `small` means re-downloading, rebuilding the pipeline, regenerating candidates, retraining all four stage-1 arms plus NRMS, and rerunning Q4/Q5/Q9 — the last full rerun was 39 steps. On ~10× the EB-NeRD data with 5 GB of free RAM, that's not a same-day job.
  
  **Do state it.** An unstated bundle difference looks like an error; a stated one looks like scoping. Two places:
  
  ```latex
  % in the recall table caption
    \caption{Test recall@K over full populations. The \texttt{bm25} row reproduces
    our own rerun of the A1 pipeline on the A2 splits, so the three arms are
    like-for-like. A1 reported 4.39\% on \texttt{ebnerd\_small}; we run
    \texttt{ebnerd\_demo}, which has 1{,}935 users against small's 18{,}827.}
  ```
  
  And as a limitation, which is worth more marks than hiding it:
  
  ```latex
  We ran EB-NeRD on the demo bundle (1{,}935 users, 398k clicks) rather than small
  (18{,}827 users, 3.76M clicks), which keeps every EB-NeRD result on roughly a
  tenth of the available data. This is the most likely reason our EB-NeRD
  confidence intervals are wide enough to span zero where MIND's do not, and it is
  the first thing we would change with more compute. MIND uses the same
  \texttt{MINDsmall} split as Assignment 1, so those numbers are directly
  comparable.
  ```
  
  That last sentence does real work: it converts "our EB-NeRD gains weren't significant" from a weak result into a known, named limitation with a stated cause.
  
  If you'd rather know the actual cost before ruling it out, I can time the EB-NeRD-only path on `small` and give you a real estimate — say the word.

❯ how much time will it take to re run on ebnerd small?

● Measured from your two rerun logs rather than guessed. EB-NeRD only; MIND is untouched.
  
  | Step | demo (actual) | small (estimate) | Basis |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | parse + split | 14 s | ~3 min | 9.4× events |
  | embeddings (provided artifact) | seconds | ~1 min | 1.8× articles |
  | candidates ×4 methods | 2 min | ~25 min | impressions × catalogue ≈ 17× |
  | re-ranker ×4 methods | 6 min | ~60 min | **only if capped, see below** |
  | **NRMS ×2 arms** | **36 min** | **~5–6 h** | 8.4 min/epoch × 20 epochs × 2 |
  | Q9 ablation | 4 min | ~40 min | 4 arms + chunked eval |
  | extended eval ×2 | 3 min | ~30 min | |
  | verify + submit | 4 min | ~40 min | test impressions |
  | serving + pytest | 1 min | ~2 min | fixed request count |
  | **Total** | **~57 min** | **~8–10 h** | |
  
  **~9 hours**, and NRMS is two-thirds of it.
  
  The NRMS figure is the solid one. Per-impression-per-epoch cost is almost identical across your two existing runs — EB-NeRD demo 4.0×10⁻⁵ min, MIND 3.8×10⁻⁵ min — so extrapolating to EB-NeRD small's ~210k train impressions gives 8.4 min/epoch, 2.8 h per arm at the 20-epoch cap. Early stopping could cut that, but you can't count on it.
  
  No download needed, incidentally: `ire-a1/data/raw/ebnerd_small.zip` is already on disk (81M).
  
  ## The blocker isn't time, it's a code change
  
  ```python
  TRAIN_IMPRESSION_CAP = {"mind": 60_000}
  ```
  `src/reranker.py:85` — **there is no cap for EB-NeRD.**
  
  Demo's 22,401 train impressions don't need one. Small has ~210k, which is 3.5× MIND's cap. At 50 sampled candidates each that's ~10.5M training rows, against the 2.7M MIND produces today. With ~5 GB free, that OOMs.
  
  So a `small` run needs `"ebnerd": 60_000` added first. That's a one-line change and it arguably *improves* things — both datasets would then train on the same 60k budget, making the cross-dataset comparison cleaner than it is now. But it's a protocol change on the day of submission, and every number in the report moves.
  
  ## My recommendation: don't
  
  It's due today. Nine hours of compute, a protocol change, an OOM risk on the uncapped path, and then every EB-NeRD figure in the design note, DesignChoices §2E/§2G/§2H/§2J/§2K, and both Codabench submissions has to be re-derived and re-checked. The failure mode isn't "it takes too long", it's "it half-finishes at 2am and the report now mixes bundles".
  
  Write the limitation paragraph instead. It costs ten minutes, it's honest, and "we ran on a tenth of the data and here's what that likely cost us in statistical power" reads as a considered scope decision — which, given the numbers above, it now genuinely is.
  
  If you want it as a fallback: the ~3.5 h path is everything **except** NRMS. I'd skip that too — it would leave your re-ranker on small and your NRMS on demo, which is worse than being consistently on demo.

❯ what is the demo size when compared to MIND small?

● Measured on your actual processed data:
  
  | | EB-NeRD demo | MIND small | MIND / EB-NeRD |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | **users** | 1,935 | 94,057 | **48.6×** |
  | articles | 11,777 | 65,238 | 5.5× |
  | impressions | 50,080 | 230,117 | 4.6× |
  | display events | 583,054 | 8,584,442 | 14.7× |
  | clicks | 50,273 | 347,727 | 6.9× |
  | candidates / impression | 11.6 | 37.3 | 3.2× |
  | CTR | 8.62% | 4.05% | — |
  
  So **EB-NeRD demo is the small dataset in your report by a wide margin** — about a fifth of MIND by impressions, a fifteenth by display events, and a fiftieth by users. The two datasets are not comparably sized, which is worth knowing before anyone reads a dataset-vs-dataset conclusion.
  
  For reference, `ebnerd_small` (18,827 users, 20,738 articles) would have put EB-NeRD in roughly the same league as MIND rather than an order of magnitude below it.
  
  ## This explains your confidence intervals
  
  The paired bootstrap resamples **test impressions**, so the relevant n is 25,356 (EB-NeRD) against 73,152 (MIND) — 2.9×, predicting MIND's CIs should be √2.9 ≈ **1.7× narrower**.
  
  Observed half-widths on the re-ranker's MRR gain over stage 1:
  
  - EB-NeRD: (0.0510 − 0.0442)/2 = **0.0034**
  - MIND: (0.0183 − 0.0149)/2 = **0.0017**
  
  A ratio of **2.0×**, against 1.7× predicted by sample size alone. So EB-NeRD's wider intervals are almost entirely a sample-size effect, not extra noise in the data.
  
  That's a stronger version of the limitation paragraph than "we had less data":
  
  ```latex
  The two datasets are not comparably sized. EB-NeRD demo has 1{,}935 users,
  50{,}080 impressions and 583k display events, against MIND small's 94{,}057
  users, 230{,}117 impressions and 8.58M events. Our EB-NeRD confidence intervals
  are correspondingly wider: the half-width on the re-ranker's MRR gain is 0.0034
  against MIND's 0.0017, a ratio of 2.0 where the 2.9$\times$ difference in test
  impressions alone predicts 1.7. Sample size, rather than noisier data, accounts
  for almost all of the gap. Where an EB-NeRD interval spans zero we therefore
  report it as inconclusive rather than as a null result. Assignment 1 used
  \texttt{ebnerd\_small} (18{,}827 users), which would have put the two datasets
  on a similar footing.
  ```
  
  The useful move there is the second-to-last sentence: it stops your EB-NeRD nulls from being read as "the feature doesn't work" when what you actually have is "this bundle can't tell".

● Code verified. Here's what I found.
  
  ## Your two edits are correct — but they guard a stage the run never reached
  
  The OOM is in **`parse`**, not the re-ranker:
  
  ```
  Out of memory: Killed process 321410 (python)
  anon-rss:12010792kB          # 12 GB on a 14.6 GB box
  ```
  
  `src/parse.py:_parse_ebnerd_behaviors` copies each user's **entire history onto every one of their impressions**, across three list columns:
  
  | bundle | impressions | history entries | est. Arrow size |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | demo | 50,080 | 14.5M | ~1.6 GiB |
  | **small** | 477,534 | **139.4M** | **~15.6 GiB** |
  
  Mean history is 160 articles. The pandas frame itself is cheap — `.apply` hands every impression of a user the *same list object* — so the blowup is purely in Arrow serialisation, and it recurs on every `read_parquet` afterwards. That means chunked writing alone wouldn't save it: `split.py`, `feature_store.py`, `feature_pipeline.py`, `lexical_retrieval.py` and `semantic_retrieval.py` all read the full frame and would hit the same wall in turn.
  
  ## One thing needs fixing regardless
  
  `data/interim/ebnerd/` is currently **mixed**: `articles.parquet` is small (14:26), `behaviors.parquet` is still demo (Sep 17). `data/processed/` is untouched demo, so your committed results are safe and the 2.3 GB backup is intact — but that interim directory can't be left as-is.
  
  I also checked capping history length: even cap=100 truncates 47.7% of users and only gets to 4.7 GiB, and it would change `SEMANTIC_CANDIDATE_CONFIG`'s full-history pooling. That's a functionality loss, so I've ruled it out.

  - **What "bit-for-bit on demo" buys you** — this is the gate that makes the refactor safe to do on deadline day rather than reckless.

  ## EB-NeRD small vs MIND small
  
  | | EB-NeRD small | MIND small | |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | impressions | 477,534 | 230,117 | EB-NeRD **2.1×** |
  | displays (candidates) | 5,514,689 | 8,584,442 | MIND 1.6× |
  | articles | 20,738 | 65,238 | MIND 3.1× |

  | **history entries** | **139,392,429** | **7,470,153** | EB-NeRD **18.7×** |
  | history columns stored | 3 | 1 | 3× |
  
  So EB-NeRD small has fewer articles, fewer users and fewer displays than MIND small. What it has is **long histories** — a mean of 160 clicks per user against MIND's 32.5 — and our parse writes each user's full history onto every one of their impressions, in three columns rather than one.
  
  That's **139M history entries against MIND's 7.5M, in 3× as many columns: ~56× the history data.** That's the whole story, and it's why MIND small fits in 14.6 GB and EB-NeRD small doesn't.
  
  ## The honest version is also the better one
  
  "Too big for the laptop" is vague and checkable-wrong. The specific version shows you diagnosed it, and it names a fix — which reads as engineering judgement rather than an excuse:
  
  ```latex
  We ran EB-NeRD on the demo bundle (1{,}935 users, 50{,}080 impressions) rather
  than small (18{,}827 users, 477{,}534). Not because small is large in absolute
  terms: it has fewer articles, users and displayed candidates than MIND small,
  which we run in full. EB-NeRD's histories are simply long, averaging 160 clicks
  per user against MIND's 32.5, and our parse stores each user's full history on
  every one of their impressions across three list columns. That is 139M history
  entries against MIND's 7.5M, roughly 56$\times$ the history data, which exceeds
  the 14.6 GB on our machine during Parquet serialisation. Storing history once
  per user and joining it at read time would cut this 30$\times$, to 4.6M entries,
  and is the first thing we would change with more time.
  
  The cost is statistical power. Our EB-NeRD confidence intervals are wider in
  proportion: the half-width on the re-ranker's MRR gain is 0.0034 against MIND's
  0.0017, a ratio of 2.0 where the 2.9$\times$ difference in test impressions alone
  predicts 1.7. Sample size, not noisier data, accounts for almost all of the gap.
  Where an EB-NeRD interval spans zero we therefore report it as inconclusive
  rather than as a null result.
  ```
  
  Two things that paragraph buys you that the vague version doesn't: it explains *why* MIND and EB-NeRD are on different footings without sounding arbitrary, and the 160-vs-32.5 history-length contrast is a genuine dataset observation that fits naturally beside your embedding-geometry finding — both are "the two datasets differ in ways that change what the model can use."

  add: recorded why ebnerd stays on the demo bundle and capped ebnerd training
  ```
  
  ## What happened
  
  Your two edits were correct but guarded the wrong stage. The OOM was in **`parse`**, which the run reached long before the re-ranker:
  
  ```
  Out of memory: Killed process 321410 (python)
  anon-rss:12010792kB        # 12 GB on a 14.6 GB box
  ```
  
  `_parse_ebnerd_behaviors` writes each user's **entire history onto every one of their impressions**, across three list columns. On `small` that's **139,392,429 history entries**. The pandas frame is cheap — `.apply` shares one list object per user — so the blowup is purely Arrow serialisation, and it would recur on every read. Chunked writing wouldn't have saved it: `split.py`, `feature_store.py`, `feature_pipeline.py` and both retrieval modules read the full frame and would each hit the same wall.
  
  ## Why `small` fails where MIND small doesn't
  
  It isn't a size problem in the usual sense — EB-NeRD small has **fewer** articles, users and displays than MIND small:
  
  | | EB-NeRD small | MIND small | |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | mean history length | 160 | 32.5 | EB-NeRD 4.9× |
  | history entries | 139.4M | 7.5M | EB-NeRD 18.7× |
  | history columns | 3 | 1 | 3× |
  
  ~56× the history data. That's the whole cause, and it's the version worth putting in the report — "the dataset was too big" is vague and checkably wrong.

  \title{\vspace{-1.5em}CS4.406 Assignment 2: Design Note\\
  \large Learning from Click-Logs on EB-NeRD and MIND}
  \author{}

  \begin{document}
  \maketitle
  \vspace{-3em}
  
  \section*{What Was Built}
  A two stage retrieve-then-rank pipeline for the news recommender and evaluated on the MIND and the EB-NeRD and reproduced the NRMS baseline and one improvement to it by adding freshness effect on the history (freshness arm) will elaborate on this in later sections.
  
  \section{Data Pipeline}
  Both datasets are parsed into one schema: an articles table (title, abstract, body, category, entities, published\_time) and a behaviors table (one row per impression: history, candidates, labels, timestamp). EB-NeRD additionally carries session\_id and per-history-click dwell times; MIND has neither, and that asymmetry recurs throughout the results.
  
  The splits are temporal and not Random, To prevent the future leakage while training.
  
  And the Article features like the freshness,popularity,CTR are strictly calculated based on the data before the time T.
  
  Within Session Features count only the impressions that occurred before this impression.
  
  \section{Stage 1: Candidate generation}
  \subsection{The Problem}
      Assignment 1 retrieved the top-k similarity between the user's click history and the whole catalogue. We used the same pipeline to generate the top-200 candidates but the recall was very low  2.55\% (EB-NeRD) \& 2.90\% (MIND). The best configuration gave around 5\%.
  
  \subsection{The fix}
  
  The ceiling was not in the data. It came from where stage 1 searched and what it
  ranked by. Clicks go to whatever is in circulation at that moment. EB-NeRD's
  median clicked article is 3.1 hours old when it is clicked, and 92\% are clicked
  within a day of publication. A1 searched the full catalogue of 11{,}777
  articles, spanning publication dates from 2000 to 2023, where 17.2\% were
  published over a year before the test period. Ranking a week's worth of clicks
  against twenty years of articles is the wrong search space. Two mistakes
  compounded.
  
  \textbf{1. The search space was wrong.} We restricted A1's BM25, unchanged, to
  the articles the platform displayed in the previous hour. EB-NeRD recall@200
  goes from 2.55\% to 84.2\% on the scoring alone.
  
  \textbf{2. The ranking signal was wrong.} Ranking that same pool by recent
  popularity instead of history similarity reaches 97.0\% and 93.9\%. History
  similarity adds nothing on top of it.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{EB-NeRD @50/@100/@200} & \textbf{MIND @50/@100/@200} \\
      \midrule
      \texttt{bm25}, A1 over the whole catalogue   & 0.82 / 1.41 / \textbf{2.55\%}  & 1.27 / 1.96 / \textbf{2.90\%} \\
      \texttt{bm25\_fresh}, A1 scoring on a 1 h pool & 21.9 / 42.9 / \textbf{84.2\%} & 10.0 / 14.2 / \textbf{21.0\%} \\
      \texttt{popular}, 1 h pool with lagged clicks & 83.4 / 93.8 / \textbf{97.0\%} & 81.6 / 88.6 / \textbf{93.9\%} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test recall@K over full populations. The \texttt{bm25} row reproduces
    our own rerun of the A1 pipeline on the A2 splits, so the three arms are
    like-for-like. It is lower than A1's reported 4.39\% because A1 used
    \texttt{ebnerd\_small} and we use the demo bundle.}
  \end{table}
  
  The pool only uses events from strictly before the impression time $t$. Displays
  count if they fall in $[t - 1\text{h},\, t)$. Clicks count if their impression
  falls in $[t - 1\text{h},\, t - 10\text{min})$, because a click lands after the
  impression that produced it. If the one-hour pool holds fewer than $K$ articles
  we backfill from the previous 24 hours.
  
  We picked the window by measuring it. On 2{,}000-impression samples, 1 hour beat
  both 6 and 24 hours, because longer windows let stale but popular articles crowd
  out fresh ones. The 10-minute click lag cost 0 pp on EB-NeRD and about 1 pp on
  MIND, which is cheap for a guarantee we need anyway.
  
  \section{Stage 2: The Re-ranker}
      \subsection{The Objective}
      \textbf{LambdaMART}: Uses the Gradient Boosting Trees (MART) and the LambdaRank optimization which effectively learns to rank items which will improve the metrics like nDCG. It is a \textit{pairwise gradient which weighted by listwise metric}.\\
      We set \textit{lambdarank\_truncation\_level} to 200, the full group size, so that the full-group setting makes the \textbar{}ΔnDCG\textbar{} weights reflect the whole 200-item list the metrics are computed over.
  
      \subsection{The features}
      Used \textbf{24} features for training this model.
      
      \textbf{Click history: }click count, recency-weighted engagement, has\_history\_timestamps \\
      \textbf{Session: }hour, day-of-week, impression size, session position, impressions/clicks earlier in session, avg history dwell time + availability flag \\
      \textbf{Article: }click count, display count, log click count, smoothed CTR, log freshness, has\_known\_publish\_time. \\
      \textbf{history × candidate: } category binary match, category affinity, history\_title, history\_embedding \\
      \textbf{From Stage :1} retrieval rank, position bias, retrieval score
  
      \subsection{Results: }
  
  
  %Need to verify this @daga
  \section{Baseline reproduced, then improved (Q3)}
  
      \subsection{Baseline}
      NRMS (Wu et al., 2019) reproduced against ebnerd-benchmark's hparams\_nrms: multi-head self-attention news encoder, self-attention user encoder, dot-product scorer, Wu-2019 negative sampling (1 positive + 4 negatives, softmax cross-entropy). Deviations from the benchmark are documented rather than silent — including that it uses no attention masking, so pad tokens participate in attention, which we reproduce deliberately.
      
      \subsection{The Improvement: freshness weighting}
      NRMS reads only title tokens, so it has no channel at all for how old an article is when shown. By analyzing the data we found out that the freshness plays a key role in the ranking.
      We added age term to the NRMS score \\
      score = ⟨u, c⟩ + g(log(1 + age\_hours), known) \\
      where g is a 2→16→1 MLP and known marks whether the article's age is available. EB-NeRD ages come from published\_time. MIND has no publish dates, so we use the first time the article appears in the display log. The head adds 65 parameters to models. The term is added to the logit rather than concatenated into the news vector, which leaves the NRMS dot-product scorer unchanged.
  
      \subsection{Results: }
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering
        \small
        \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{\shortstack{EB-NeRD base $\to$\\+fresh}} 
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} 
          & \textbf{\shortstack{MIND base $\to$\\+fresh}} 
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          AUC     & $0.5425 \to 0.6112$ & \textbf{+0.0686} [$+0.0662$, $+0.0710$] & $0.6040 \to 0.6042$ & $+0.0002$ [$-0.0006$, $+0.0010$] \\
          \midrule
          MRR     & $0.3394 \to 0.3962$ & \textbf{+0.0567} [$+0.0538$, $+0.0595$] & $0.2750 \to 0.2743$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0014$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \midrule
          nDCG@5  & $0.3770 \to 0.4427$ & \textbf{+0.0657} [$+0.0628$, $+0.0685$] & $0.2961 \to 0.2949$ & $-0.0012$ [$-0.0021$, $-0.0003$] \\
          \midrule
          nDCG@10 & $0.4570 \to 0.5095$ & \textbf{+0.0525} [$+0.0501$, $+0.0548$] & $0.3617 \to 0.3610$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0015$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
      \end{table}
  
      \textbf{EB-NeRD }all four metrics improved but for \textbf{MIND} there was no effect,  every change is smaller than 0.0015.\\
      \textbf{Why MIND shows nothing: }The age term is additive on the logit, so it can only reorder articles \emph{within} an impression --- and MIND's impressions are near-uniform in age (median within-impression spread \textbf{27.5\,h}, against \textbf{1{,}057\,h} on EB-NeRD), so a term that is nearly constant across a row cannot reorder it. It is not a data gap (99.9\% of MIND candidates and clicks carry an age) and not the first-seen proxy either: EB-NeRD has both age definitions, and swapping real publish times for the proxy costs only \textbf{0.013} AUC (0.6765 $\to$ 0.6631). The defensible claim is thus ``freshness-as-first-seen does not help on MIND'', not ``freshness does not help''.
  
  \section{Serving and scale}
  
  Measured locally: components built in isolated processes, so each figure is the
  exact array bytes where walkable and resident-set growth otherwise. Latency is
  single-threaded on an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores, 14 GiB RAM), 1{,}000 timed
  requests after 50 warm-up, $K=200$. Stage-1 log replay (0.02--0.03
  ms/impression) is excluded: a live system maintains those counters as events
  arrive.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{ANN index}} \\
      \quad Embedding matrix (brute-force exact cosine scan) & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Feature store}} \\
      \quad Article text (titles, abstracts)                 & 35.8 & 61.8 \\
      \quad Article embeddings      & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\footnotesize same array as above, counted once} \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Other resident state}} \\
      \quad BM25 inverted index (history-title feature)      &  4.1 & 36.0 \\
      \quad As-of article statistics                         &  4.8 & 68.1 \\
      \quad Fresh-pool event log                             &  7.5 & 26.5 \\
      \quad LightGBM booster (174 / 168 trees)               &  4.0 &  3.9 \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total (MiB)} & \textbf{90.7} & \textbf{291.9} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Our ANN index is a brute-force exact cosine scan over the feature store's
  embedding matrix, so it adds no structure of its own: the 34.5 / 95.6 MiB is
  the matrix itself, shared between the ANN index and the feature store. Both
  catalogues are under 100k articles, where a single matrix multiply is exact and
  faster than an approximate index; the trade reverses with catalogue size, which
  is the scaling point below.
  
  The feature store dominates: text plus embeddings are 78\% of EB-NeRD's
  footprint and 54\% of MIND's. The two structures serving the history-similarity
  features (embeddings and BM25) cost 131.6 MiB on MIND, which buys a significant
  ranking gain there but none on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring}
      & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 & 3.42 & 2.58 & 5.97 & 10.50 \\
      MIND    & 0.56 & 2.72 & 0.14 & 3.47 &  8.00 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Milliseconds for a single user request, single core, $K=200$.
    Stage 1 is candidate generation, features and scoring are re-ranking.}
  \end{table}
  
  Feature building is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 is under 10\%. Neither
  retrieval nor the GBDT is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature
  assembly.
  
  \subsection{Cost per 1000 queries at the SLA}
  
  p99 is roughly 10$\times$ inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. At 170
  (EB-NeRD) and 253 (MIND) single-core QPS and \$0.17 per vCPU-hour, that is
  \$0.000278 and \$0.000186 per thousand queries, under a dollar of compute for
  a million requests a day. 
  
  \section{Where the pipeline breaks at 10$\times$}
  
  Latency is linear in $K$ with a fixed per-impression cost of about 1.2 ms
  (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K = 50 / 100 / 200$; roughly
  $1.2 + 0.023K$). $K$ is therefore the throughput knob, and on EB-NeRD halving it costs little recall (93.8\% at $K=100$ against 97.0\% at $K=200$; MIND
  88.6\% against 93.9\%).
  
  Ranked by what fails first:
  
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{As-of article statistics: the binding constraint.} It must be
      resident and grows with total events: MIND's 8.93M display and click events
      occupy 68.1 MiB at 8 bytes each, so about 680 MiB at 10$\times$ traffic and
      about 4.4 GB at MINDlarge scale ($\sim$65$\times$).
      This is the first thing to shard or move to a windowed counter store.
    \item \textbf{CPU for feature assembly.} 170--253 QPS per core means about ten
      cores at 10$\times$. Parallel across requests, so a cost line rather than a
      wall.
    \item \textbf{The ANN index and feature store, on a different axis.} Both grow
      with \emph{catalogue size}, not traffic, so 10$\times$ traffic does not touch
      them, but a 10$\times$ catalogue does, and it is the point where the
      brute-force scan stops paying and the embedding matrix needs a real
      approximate index instead of a resident array.
  \end{enumerate}
  
  
  \section{Design Choices}
      \subsection{Re-Ranker Objective}
      The choices were pointwise \textit{binary} , LambdaMART and the list wise \textit{rank\_xendcg}. RankNet was rejected because it minimizes the number of inversions and doesnt treat the inversion between rank 1, 2 and rank 100, 101 differently. The pointwise is rejected because they predict the absolute relevance score of each item in isolation, completely ignoring how those items compare to each other. 
      
      \subsection{Stage-1 candidate source}
      In the stage 1 the BM25 and the semantic retrieval recovered only \textbf{2.55\% (EB-NeRD) / 2.90\% (MIND)} of impressions at $K=200$. And we found that the median clicked-article age at click time is 3.1\,h. A display-log fresh pool reached \textbf{97.0\% / 93.9\%}. Within that pool, a 1\,h window beat 6\,h and 24\,h (EB-NeRD 97.4\% vs.\ 88.9\% @200) because longer windows let stale-but-popular articles crowd out fresh ones.
  
      \subsection{NRMS improvement (Q3)}
      The initial idea was adding the positional encoding on the user encoder. It was rejected on measurement: at \texttt{HISTORY\_SIZE}~=~20, \textbf{51.1\%} of MIND impressions carry fewer than 20 clicks and \textbf{29.4\%} of history slots are padding, and \texttt{layers.py} applies no masking  so PE would attach position embeddings to padding on exactly the dataset where padding dominates. \textbf{Freshness} won on a category difference rather than a magnitude one: article age is not in NRMS's input \emph{at all} and cannot be learned from title tokens at any amount of training.

  \title{\vspace{-1.5em}CS4.406 Assignment 2: Design Note\\

  \section*{What Was Built}
  A two stage retrieve-then-rank pipeline for the news recommender and evaluated on the MIND and the EB-NeRD and reproduced the NRMS baseline and one improvement to it by adding freshness effect on the history (freshness arm) will elaborate on this in later sections.
  
  \section{Data Pipeline}
  Both datasets are parsed into one schema: an articles table (title, abstract, body, category, entities, published\_time) and a behaviors table (one row per impression: history, candidates, labels, timestamp). EB-NeRD additionally carries session\_id and per-history-click dwell times; MIND has neither, and that asymmetry recurs throughout the results.
  
  The splits are temporal and not Random, To prevent the future leakage while training.
  
  And the Article features like the freshness,popularity,CTR are strictly calculated based on the data before the time T.
  
  Within Session Features count only the impressions that occurred before this impression.
  
  \section{Stage 1: Candidate generation}
  \subsection{The Problem}
      Assignment 1 retrieved the top-k similarity between the user's click history and the whole catalogue. We used the same pipeline to generate the top-200 candidates but the recall was very low  2.55\% (EB-NeRD) \& 2.90\% (MIND). The best A1 configuration reached 4.39\% (EB-NeRD, BM25), and that too on the
  larger \texttt{ebnerd\_small} bundle; on the demo bundle we run here the same
  pipeline gives 2.55\%.
  
  \subsection{The fix}
  
  The ceiling was not in the data. It came from where stage 1 searched and what it
  ranked by. Clicks go to whatever is in circulation at that moment. EB-NeRD's
  median clicked article is 3.1 hours old when it is clicked, and 92\% are clicked
  within a day of publication. A1 searched the full catalogue of 11{,}777
  articles, spanning publication dates from 2000 to 2023, where 17.2\% were
  published over a year before the test period. Ranking a week's worth of clicks
  against twenty years of articles is the wrong search space. Two mistakes
  compounded.
  
  \textbf{1. The search space was wrong.} We restricted A1's BM25, unchanged, to
  the articles the platform displayed in the previous hour. EB-NeRD recall@200
  goes from 2.55\% to 84.2\% on the scoring alone.
  
  \textbf{2. The ranking signal was wrong.} Ranking that same pool by recent
  popularity instead of history similarity reaches 97.0\% and 93.9\%. History
  similarity adds nothing on top of it.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{EB-NeRD @50/@100/@200} & \textbf{MIND @50/@100/@200} \\
      \midrule
      \texttt{bm25}, A1 over the whole catalogue   & 0.82 / 1.41 / \textbf{2.55\%}  & 1.27 / 1.96 / \textbf{2.90\%} \\
      \texttt{bm25\_fresh}, A1 scoring on a 1 h pool & 21.9 / 42.9 / \textbf{84.2\%} & 10.0 / 14.2 / \textbf{21.0\%} \\
      \texttt{popular}, 1 h pool with lagged clicks & 83.4 / 93.8 / \textbf{97.0\%} & 81.6 / 88.6 / \textbf{93.9\%} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test recall@K over full populations. The \texttt{bm25} row reproduces
    our own rerun of the A1 pipeline on the A2 splits, so the three arms are
    like-for-like. It is lower than A1's reported 4.39\% because A1 used
    \texttt{ebnerd\_small} and we use the demo bundle.}
  \end{table}
  
  The pool only uses events from strictly before the impression time $t$. Displays
  count if they fall in $[t - 1\text{h},\, t)$. Clicks count if their impression
  falls in $[t - 1\text{h},\, t - 10\text{min})$, because a click lands after the
  impression that produced it. If the one-hour pool holds fewer than $K$ articles
  we backfill from the previous 24 hours.
  
  We picked the window by measuring it. On 2{,}000-impression samples, 1 hour beat
  both 6 and 24 hours, because longer windows let stale but popular articles crowd
  out fresh ones. The 10-minute click lag cost 0 pp on EB-NeRD and about 1 pp on
  MIND, which is cheap for a guarantee we need anyway.
  
  \section{Stage 2: The Re-ranker}
      \subsection{The Objective}
      \textbf{LambdaMART}: Uses the Gradient Boosting Trees (MART) and the LambdaRank optimization which effectively learns to rank items which will improve the metrics like nDCG. It is a \textit{pairwise gradient which weighted by listwise metric}.\\
      We set \textit{lambdarank\_truncation\_level} to 200, the full group size, so that the full-group setting makes the \textbar{}ΔnDCG\textbar{} weights reflect the whole 200-item list the metrics are computed over.
  
      \subsection{The features}
      Used \textbf{24} features for training this model.
      
      \textbf{Click history: }click count, recency-weighted engagement, has\_history\_timestamps \\
      \textbf{Session: }hour, day-of-week, impression size, session position, impressions/clicks earlier in session, avg history dwell time + availability flag \\
      \textbf{Article: }click count, display count, log click count, smoothed CTR, log freshness, has\_known\_publish\_time. \\
      \textbf{history × candidate: } category binary match, category affinity, history\_title, history\_embedding \\
      \textbf{From Stage :1} retrieval rank, position bias, retrieval score
  
      \subsection{Results}
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering\small
        \begin{tabular}{lccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Re-ranked} & \textbf{$\Delta$ MRR (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          EB-NeRD AUC     & 0.8815 & \textbf{0.9219} & \\
          EB-NeRD MRR     & 0.1639 & \textbf{0.2115} & \textbf{+0.0476} [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$] \\
          EB-NeRD nDCG@5  & 0.1471 & 0.2026 & \\
          EB-NeRD nDCG@10 & 0.1994 & 0.2705 & \\
          \midrule
          MIND AUC        & 0.8655 & \textbf{0.8891} & \\
          MIND MRR        & 0.2237 & \textbf{0.2403} & \textbf{+0.0167} [$+0.0149$, $+0.0183$] \\
          MIND nDCG@5     & 0.2451 & 0.2618 & \\
          MIND nDCG@10    & 0.2932 & 0.3150 & \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
        \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates, $K=200$. Paired
        bootstrap over impressions, 2{,}000 resamples. Both gains clear zero.}
      \end{table}
  
  
  %Need to verify this @daga
  \section{Baseline reproduced, then improved (Q3)}
  
      \subsection{Baseline}
      NRMS (Wu et al., 2019) reproduced against ebnerd-benchmark's hparams\_nrms: multi-head self-attention news encoder, self-attention user encoder, dot-product scorer, Wu-2019 negative sampling (1 positive + 4 negatives, softmax cross-entropy). Deviations from the benchmark are documented rather than silent — including that it uses no attention masking, so pad tokens participate in attention, which we reproduce deliberately.
      
      \subsection{The Improvement: freshness weighting}
      NRMS reads only title tokens, so it has no channel at all for how old an article is when shown. By analyzing the data we found out that the freshness plays a key role in the ranking.
      We added age term to the NRMS score \\
      score = ⟨u, c⟩ + g(log(1 + age\_hours), known) \\
      where g is a 2→16→1 MLP and known marks whether the article's age is available. EB-NeRD ages come from published\_time. MIND has no publish dates, so we use the first time the article appears in the display log. The head adds 65 parameters to models. The term is added to the logit rather than concatenated into the news vector, which leaves the NRMS dot-product scorer unchanged.
  
      \subsection{Results: }
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering
        \small
        \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{\shortstack{EB-NeRD base $\to$\\+fresh}} 
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} 
          & \textbf{\shortstack{MIND base $\to$\\+fresh}} 
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          AUC     & $0.5425 \to 0.6112$ & \textbf{+0.0686} [$+0.0662$, $+0.0710$] & $0.6040 \to 0.6042$ & $+0.0002$ [$-0.0006$, $+0.0010$] \\
          \midrule
          MRR     & $0.3394 \to 0.3962$ & \textbf{+0.0567} [$+0.0538$, $+0.0595$] & $0.2750 \to 0.2743$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0014$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \midrule
          nDCG@5  & $0.3770 \to 0.4427$ & \textbf{+0.0657} [$+0.0628$, $+0.0685$] & $0.2961 \to 0.2949$ & $-0.0012$ [$-0.0021$, $-0.0003$] \\
          \midrule
          nDCG@10 & $0.4570 \to 0.5095$ & \textbf{+0.0525} [$+0.0501$, $+0.0548$] & $0.3617 \to 0.3610$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0015$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
      \end{table}
  
      \textbf{EB-NeRD }all four metrics improved but for \textbf{MIND} there was no effect,  every change is smaller than 0.0015.\\
      \textbf{Why MIND shows nothing: }The age term is additive on the logit, so it can only reorder articles \emph{within} an impression --- and MIND's impressions are near-uniform in age (median within-impression spread \textbf{27.5\,h}, against \textbf{1{,}057\,h} on EB-NeRD), so a term that is nearly constant across a row cannot reorder it. It is not a data gap (99.9\% of MIND candidates and clicks carry an age) and not the first-seen proxy either: EB-NeRD has both age definitions, and swapping real publish times for the proxy costs only \textbf{0.013} AUC (0.6765 $\to$ 0.6631). The defensible claim is thus ``freshness-as-first-seen does not help on MIND'', not ``freshness does not help''.
  
  \section{Serving and scale}
  
  Measured locally: components built in isolated processes, so each figure is the
  exact array bytes where walkable and resident-set growth otherwise. Latency is
  single-threaded on an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores, 14 GiB RAM), 1{,}000 timed
  requests after 50 warm-up, $K=200$. Stage-1 log replay (0.02--0.03
  ms/impression) is excluded: a live system maintains those counters as events
  arrive.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{ANN index}} \\
      \quad Embedding matrix (brute-force exact cosine scan) & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Feature store}} \\
      \quad Article text (titles, abstracts)                 & 35.8 & 61.8 \\
      \quad Article embeddings      & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\footnotesize same array as above, counted once} \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Other resident state}} \\
      \quad BM25 inverted index (history-title feature)      &  4.1 & 36.0 \\
      \quad As-of article statistics                         &  4.8 & 68.1 \\
      \quad Fresh-pool event log                             &  7.5 & 26.5 \\
      \quad LightGBM booster (174 / 168 trees)               &  4.0 &  3.9 \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total (MiB)} & \textbf{90.7} & \textbf{291.9} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Our ANN index is a brute-force exact cosine scan over the feature store's
  embedding matrix, so it adds no structure of its own: the 34.5 / 95.6 MiB is
  the matrix itself, shared between the ANN index and the feature store. Both
  catalogues are under 100k articles, where a single matrix multiply is exact and
  faster than an approximate index; the trade reverses with catalogue size, which
  is the scaling point below.
  
  The feature store dominates: text plus embeddings are 78\% of EB-NeRD's
  footprint and 54\% of MIND's. The two structures serving the history-similarity
  features (embeddings and BM25) cost 131.6 MiB on MIND, which buys a significant
  ranking gain there but none on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring}
      & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 & 3.42 & 2.58 & 5.97 & 10.50 \\
      MIND    & 0.56 & 2.72 & 0.14 & 3.47 &  8.00 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Milliseconds for a single user request, single core, $K=200$.
    Stage 1 is candidate generation, features and scoring are re-ranking.}
  \end{table}
  
  Feature building is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 is under 10\%. Neither
  retrieval nor the GBDT is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature
  assembly.
  
  \subsection{Cost per 1000 queries at the SLA}
  
  p99 is roughly 10$\times$ inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. At 170
  (EB-NeRD) and 253 (MIND) single-core QPS and \$0.17 per vCPU-hour, that is
  \$0.000278 and \$0.000186 per thousand queries, under a dollar of compute for
  a million requests a day. 
  
  \section{Where the pipeline breaks at 10$\times$}
  
  Latency is linear in $K$ with a fixed per-impression cost of about 1.2 ms
  (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K = 50 / 100 / 200$; roughly
  $1.2 + 0.023K$). $K$ is therefore the throughput knob, and on EB-NeRD halving it costs little recall (93.8\% at $K=100$ against 97.0\% at $K=200$; MIND
  88.6\% against 93.9\%).
  
  Ranked by what fails first:
  
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{As-of article statistics: the binding constraint.} It must be
      resident and grows with total events: MIND's 8.93M display and click events
      occupy 68.1 MiB at 8 bytes each, so about 680 MiB at 10$\times$ traffic and
      about 4.4 GB at MINDlarge scale ($\sim$65$\times$).
      This is the first thing to shard or move to a windowed counter store.
    \item \textbf{CPU for feature assembly.} 170--253 QPS per core means about ten
      cores at 10$\times$. Parallel across requests, so a cost line rather than a
      wall.
    \item \textbf{The ANN index and feature store, on a different axis.} Both grow
      with \emph{catalogue size}, not traffic, so 10$\times$ traffic does not touch
      them, but a 10$\times$ catalogue does, and it is the point where the
      brute-force scan stops paying and the embedding matrix needs a real
      approximate index instead of a resident array.
  \end{enumerate}
  
  
  \section{Design Choices}
      \subsection{Re-Ranker Objective}
      The choices were pointwise \textit{binary} , LambdaMART and the list wise \textit{rank\_xendcg}. RankNet was rejected because it minimizes the number of inversions and doesnt treat the inversion between rank 1, 2 and rank 100, 101 differently. The pointwise is rejected because they predict the absolute relevance score of each item in isolation, completely ignoring how those items compare to each other. 
      
      \subsection{Stage-1 candidate source}
      In the stage 1 the BM25 and the semantic retrieval recovered only \textbf{2.55\% (EB-NeRD) / 2.90\% (MIND)} of impressions at $K=200$. And we found that the median clicked-article age at click time is 3.1\,h. A display-log fresh pool reached \textbf{97.0\% / 93.9\%}. Within that pool, a 1\,h window beat 6\,h and 24\,h (EB-NeRD 97.4\% vs.\ 88.9\% @200) because longer windows let stale-but-popular articles crowd out fresh ones.
  
      \subsection{NRMS improvement (Q3)}
      The initial idea was adding the positional encoding on the user encoder. It was rejected on measurement: at \texttt{HISTORY\_SIZE}~=~20, \textbf{51.1\%} of MIND impressions carry fewer than 20 clicks and \textbf{29.4\%} of history slots are padding, and \texttt{layers.py} applies no masking  so PE would attach position embeddings to padding on exactly the dataset where padding dominates. \textbf{Freshness} won on a category difference rather than a magnitude one: article age is not in NRMS's input \emph{at all} and cannot be learned from title tokens at any amount of training.

  ## Q9
  
  ```latex
  \section{Metrics with and without serving-time features (Q9)}
  
  Every feature is computed as-of strictly before the impression it scores, so
  nothing in the model reads the future. That is necessary but not sufficient: a
  feature can be leak-free and still be unavailable to a live system, or be
  contaminated by the outcome through how it was measured. We retrain the
  re-ranker without those features and report both numbers.
  
  Two features do not survive the test.
  
  \textbf{1.} \texttt{impression\_size} is the length of the impression's own
  in-view list. On EB-NeRD that list is the articles that came \emph{into view},
  which grows with how long the reader stays on the page: it correlates with that
  same impression's \texttt{read\_time} at Spearman \textbf{0.50}. It is measured
  from an outcome we only know afterwards. On MIND it is the platform's list
  length for the impression being ranked, which a two-stage system choosing its
  own 200 candidates would not be handed either.
  
  \textbf{2.} \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time} is read off the dataset's later
  \texttt{published\_time} snapshot, and flags the articles bulk re-stamped after
  the logs were collected. No live system could know it.
  
  A third, \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}, is dropped in a stricter arm. A real
  session store would hold most of it, but unlike the article statistics it
  applies no click reporting lag.
  
  Every arm is trained exactly as the shipped model is trained: same impressions,
  same negative sampling, same seed, differing only in the feature list. The
  \texttt{all} arm therefore has to reproduce the shipped report, and does, to
  within $10^{-14}$.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{AUC} & \textbf{MRR}
      & \textbf{nDCG@5} & \textbf{nDCG@10} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & stage 1 only           & --- & 0.8815 & 0.1639 & 0.1471 & 0.1994 \\
        & all                    & 24 & \textbf{0.9219} & \textbf{0.2115} & 0.2026 & 0.2705 \\
        & serving-safe           & 22 & 0.9102 & 0.1897 & 0.1754 & 0.2372 \\
        & serving-safe (strict)  & 21 & 0.9103 & 0.1887 & 0.1739 & 0.2369 \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & stage 1 only           & --- & 0.8655 & 0.2237 & 0.2451 & 0.2932 \\
        & all                    & 24 & \textbf{0.8891} & \textbf{0.2403} & 0.2618 & 0.3150 \\
        & serving-safe           & 22 & 0.8866 & 0.2397 & 0.2619 & 0.3133 \\
        & serving-safe (strict)  & 21 & 0.8859 & 0.2375 & 0.2599 & 0.3112 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates. Serving-safe drops
    \texttt{impression\_size} and \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time}; strict also
    drops \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{EB-NeRD's headline nearly halves.} Against stage 1 the full model gains
  $+0.0476$ MRR [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$], and the serving-safe model gains
  $+0.0258$ [$+0.0229$, $+0.0287$]. Dropping the two features costs $-0.0218$
  [$-0.0246$, $-0.0191$], an interval well clear of zero. The honest claim for
  EB-NeRD is $+0.0258$, not $+0.0476$.
  
  \textbf{MIND's holds.} $+0.0167$ [$+0.0149$, $+0.0183$] becomes $+0.0160$
  [$+0.0145$, $+0.0175$], and the cost of dropping the two features is $-0.0007$
  with a confidence interval spanning zero.
  
  The gain shares explain the split. \texttt{impression\_size} carries 5.1\% of
  total split gain on EB-NeRD and 10.6\% on MIND, but MIND's re-ranker has the
  history-similarity features to fall back on and EB-NeRD's does not, so removing
  it collapses EB-NeRD's model from 174 trees to 19 while MIND's only falls to
  108. \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time} carries \textbf{0.000\%} on both, which
  confirms that the fresh pool already closed the future-article shortcut
  described in Stage 1.
  ```
  
  `\multirow` needs `\usepackage{multirow}`. If you'd rather not add it, replace the two `\multirow` cells with a plain `\textit{EB-NeRD}` row above each block.
  
  ## Q5 beyond-accuracy
  
  ```latex
  \section{Beyond accuracy (Q5)}
  
  Accuracy alone does not say whether a recommender is worth deploying. We report
  coverage, diversity and novelty on the top-10 lists, over a 5{,}000-impression
  sample with 1{,}000 bootstrap resamples, plus two slices.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Metric (top-10)} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      Catalogue coverage      & 0.0696 [0.0690, 0.0701] & 0.0065 [0.0063, 0.0067] \\
      \quad catalogue size    & 11{,}777 & 65{,}238 \\
      Diversity (raw)         & 0.0370 [0.0363, 0.0378] & 0.9008 [0.8997, 0.9019] \\
      \quad random baseline   & 0.0484 & 0.9402 \\
      \quad \textbf{ratio to random} & \textbf{0.765} & \textbf{0.958} \\
      Novelty ($-\log_2 p$)   & 14.456 & 17.339 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Raw diversity is not comparable across the two datasets, and reporting
  it as though it were would be wrong.} It is dominated by how anisotropic the
  embedding space is. A random pair of EB-NeRD articles already scores 0.048 under
  the provided multilingual BERT vectors, because those vectors sit in a narrow
  cone: a random pair has cosine 0.95. A random MIND pair scores 0.940 under
  MiniLM, which is contrastively trained. The comparable quantity is the ratio to
  that random baseline, where 1.0 means ``as diverse as picking at random''. On
  that reading EB-NeRD's lists are \textbf{0.765} as diverse as random and MIND's
  are \textbf{0.958}, so it is EB-NeRD that concentrates, which the raw numbers
  suggest the opposite of.
  
  \textbf{Coverage is very low on both, and that is the cost of the Stage 1
  design.} The pipeline recommends from a one-hour display window, so it can only
  ever surface what was in circulation. MIND reaches 0.65\% of a 65{,}238-article
  catalogue. This is the direct trade for the recall gain in Stage 1, and it is
  the number to watch if long-tail exposure matters: a fresh pool that fixes
  recall also guarantees a small, recent, popular recommendation set.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llcc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Slice} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{MRR (95\% CI)} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) &     67 & 0.2678 [0.2005, 0.3447] \\
        & warm                       & 25{,}289 & 0.2114 [0.2079, 0.2146] \\
        & head article clicked       &    197 & 0.0072 [0.0062, 0.0082] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 25{,}159 & 0.2131 [0.2099, 0.2165] \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) & 12{,}982 & 0.2426 [0.2375, 0.2476] \\
        & warm                       & 60{,}170 & 0.2399 [0.2375, 0.2421] \\
        & head article clicked       &  9{,}150 & 0.1613 [0.1568, 0.1661] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 64{,}002 & 0.2516 [0.2494, 0.2541] \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Head is the top 20\% of articles by train click count.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Cold start is not a weakness here.} On MIND cold-start users score
  0.2426 against warm users' 0.2399, and the intervals overlap. That is expected
  rather than surprising: most of the signal is article-side popularity and
  freshness, which does not need a history at all. EB-NeRD's cold-start slice
  holds only 67 impressions, so its interval is far too wide to read as a result.
  
  \textbf{The head slice is where the pipeline actually fails.} When the clicked
  article is one of the 20\% most-clicked in \emph{train}, EB-NeRD MRR collapses
  to 0.0072, against 0.2131 on the tail. The cause is the same one that motivated
  Stage 1: articles popular during training are old news by test time, so they are
  rarely in a one-hour fresh pool, and if a candidate is not retrieved no
  re-ranker can recover it. MIND shows the same effect more mildly, 0.1613 against
  0.2516. It affects 0.8\% of EB-NeRD impressions and 12.5\% of MIND's.
  ```
  
  Both use `\multirow`. The head-slice paragraph is the one I'd keep if you're short on space — it's a failure mode you can explain mechanically, which reads better than a table of metrics that are all fine.
  
  One flag: these come from `--beyond-sample 5000`, while `DesignChoices.md` §2H quotes an earlier run at 2000 (coverage 0.0679 against 0.0692). The numbers above are the current committed ones, so the report is self-consistent — but §2H and the report disagree until one is re-run or annotated. That's the open item I raised a while back and it's still unresolved.

❯ its fine DesignChoices is not important, the report should match what was actually ran

● Confirmed — the numbers in both sections are exactly what produced the committed artifacts:
  
  | Section | Source | Run config |
  |---|---|---|
  | Q9 | `results/serving_features_ablation_{ebnerd,mind}.json` | 2,000 resamples, seed 0 |
  | Q5 | `results/extended_eval_{ebnerd,mind}_popular.json` | `--beyond-sample 5000`, 1,000 resamples, seed 42, top-10 |
  
  So nothing to change. §2H's 0.0679 is the stale one, from the older 2,000-sample run; the report's 0.0696 is current.
  
  One small thing to be aware of, not an error: the two sections use different resample counts (2,000 for the Q9 and Stage 2 *delta* CIs, 1,000 for Q5's). They're measuring different things — paired deltas versus absolute per-metric intervals — so they don't clash, but if a reader compares an interval across sections it's worth a half-sentence in the Q5 preamble: "1,000 resamples here, against 2,000 for the paired deltas elsewhere, because these are absolute intervals rather than paired differences."

❯ \section{Metrics with and without serving-time features}
  
  Every feature is computed as-of strictly before the impression it scores, so
  nothing in the model reads the future. That is necessary but not sufficient: a
  feature can be leak-free and still be unavailable to a live system, or be
  contaminated by the outcome through how it was measured. We retrain the
  re-ranker without those features and report both numbers.
  
  Two features do not survive the test.
  
  \textbf{1.} \texttt{impression\_size} is the length of the impression's own
  in-view list. On EB-NeRD that list is the articles that came \emph{into view},
  which grows with how long the reader stays on the page: it correlates with that
  same impression's \texttt{read\_time} at Spearman \textbf{0.50}. It is measured
  from an outcome we only know afterwards. On MIND it is the platform's list
  length for the impression being ranked, which a two-stage system choosing its
  own 200 candidates would not be handed either.
  
  \textbf{2.} \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time} is read off the dataset's later
  \texttt{published\_time} snapshot, and flags the articles bulk re-stamped after
  the logs were collected. No live system could know it.
  
  A third, \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}, is dropped in a stricter arm. A real
  session store would hold most of it, but unlike the article statistics it
  applies no click reporting lag.
  
  Every arm is trained exactly as the shipped model is trained: same impressions,
  same negative sampling, same seed, differing only in the feature list. The
  \texttt{all} arm therefore has to reproduce the shipped report, and does, to
  within $10^{-14}$.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{AUC} & \textbf{MRR}
      & \textbf{nDCG@5} & \textbf{nDCG@10} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & stage 1 only           & --- & 0.8815 & 0.1639 & 0.1471 & 0.1994 \\
        & all                    & 24 & \textbf{0.9219} & \textbf{0.2115} & 0.2026 & 0.2705 \\
        & serving-safe           & 22 & 0.9102 & 0.1897 & 0.1754 & 0.2372 \\
        & serving-safe (strict)  & 21 & 0.9103 & 0.1887 & 0.1739 & 0.2369 \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & stage 1 only           & --- & 0.8655 & 0.2237 & 0.2451 & 0.2932 \\
        & all                    & 24 & \textbf{0.8891} & \textbf{0.2403} & 0.2618 & 0.3150 \\
        & serving-safe           & 22 & 0.8866 & 0.2397 & 0.2619 & 0.3133 \\
        & serving-safe (strict)  & 21 & 0.8859 & 0.2375 & 0.2599 & 0.3112 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates. Serving-safe drops
    \texttt{impression\_size} and \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time}; strict also
    drops \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{EB-NeRD's headline nearly halves.} Against stage 1 the full model gains
  $+0.0476$ MRR [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$], and the serving-safe model gains
  $+0.0258$ [$+0.0229$, $+0.0287$]. Dropping the two features costs $-0.0218$
  [$-0.0246$, $-0.0191$], an interval well clear of zero. The honest claim for
  EB-NeRD is $+0.0258$, not $+0.0476$.
  
  \textbf{MIND's holds.} $+0.0167$ [$+0.0149$, $+0.0183$] becomes $+0.0160$
  [$+0.0145$, $+0.0175$], and the cost of dropping the two features is $-0.0007$
  with a confidence interval spanning zero.
  
  The gain shares explain the split. \texttt{impression\_size} carries 5.1\% of
  total split gain on EB-NeRD and 10.6\% on MIND, but MIND's re-ranker has the
  history-similarity features to fall back on and EB-NeRD's does not, so removing
  it collapses EB-NeRD's model from 174 trees to 19 while MIND's only falls to
  108. \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time} carries \textbf{0.000\%} on both, which
  confirms that the fresh pool already closed the future-article shortcut
  described in Stage 1.
  
  make this a lot shorter, it is way too long right now

❯ \section{Beyond accuracy (Q5)}
  
  Accuracy alone does not say whether a recommender is worth deploying. We report
  coverage, diversity and novelty on the top-10 lists, over a 5{,}000-impression
  sample with 1{,}000 bootstrap resamples, plus two slices.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Metric (top-10)} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      Catalogue coverage      & 0.0696 [0.0690, 0.0701] & 0.0065 [0.0063, 0.0067] \\
      \quad catalogue size    & 11{,}777 & 65{,}238 \\
      Diversity (raw)         & 0.0370 [0.0363, 0.0378] & 0.9008 [0.8997, 0.9019] \\
      \quad random baseline   & 0.0484 & 0.9402 \\
      \quad \textbf{ratio to random} & \textbf{0.765} & \textbf{0.958} \\
      Novelty ($-\log_2 p$)   & 14.456 & 17.339 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Raw diversity is not comparable across the two datasets, and reporting
  it as though it were would be wrong.} It is dominated by how anisotropic the
  embedding space is. A random pair of EB-NeRD articles already scores 0.048 under
  the provided multilingual BERT vectors, because those vectors sit in a narrow
  cone: a random pair has cosine 0.95. A random MIND pair scores 0.940 under
  MiniLM, which is contrastively trained. The comparable quantity is the ratio to
  that random baseline, where 1.0 means ``as diverse as picking at random''. On
  that reading EB-NeRD's lists are \textbf{0.765} as diverse as random and MIND's
  are \textbf{0.958}, so it is EB-NeRD that concentrates, which the raw numbers
  suggest the opposite of.
  
  \textbf{Coverage is very low on both, and that is the cost of the Stage 1
  design.} The pipeline recommends from a one-hour display window, so it can only
  ever surface what was in circulation. MIND reaches 0.65\% of a 65{,}238-article
  catalogue. This is the direct trade for the recall gain in Stage 1, and it is
  the number to watch if long-tail exposure matters: a fresh pool that fixes
  recall also guarantees a small, recent, popular recommendation set.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llcc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Slice} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{MRR (95\% CI)} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) &     67 & 0.2678 [0.2005, 0.3447] \\
        & warm                       & 25{,}289 & 0.2114 [0.2079, 0.2146] \\
        & head article clicked       &    197 & 0.0072 [0.0062, 0.0082] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 25{,}159 & 0.2131 [0.2099, 0.2165] \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) & 12{,}982 & 0.2426 [0.2375, 0.2476] \\
        & warm                       & 60{,}170 & 0.2399 [0.2375, 0.2421] \\
        & head article clicked       &  9{,}150 & 0.1613 [0.1568, 0.1661] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 64{,}002 & 0.2516 [0.2494, 0.2541] \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Head is the top 20\% of articles by train click count.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Cold start is not a weakness here.} On MIND cold-start users score
  0.2426 against warm users' 0.2399, and the intervals overlap. That is expected
  rather than surprising: most of the signal is article-side popularity and
  freshness, which does not need a history at all. EB-NeRD's cold-start slice
  holds only 67 impressions, so its interval is far too wide to read as a result.
  
  \textbf{The head slice is where the pipeline actually fails.} When the clicked
  article is one of the 20\% most-clicked in \emph{train}, EB-NeRD MRR collapses
  to 0.0072, against 0.2131 on the tail. The cause is the same one that motivated
  Stage 1: articles popular during training are old news by test time, so they are
  rarely in a one-hour fresh pool, and if a candidate is not retrieved no
  re-ranker can recover it. MIND shows the same effect more mildly, 0.1613 against
  0.2516. It affects 0.8\% of EB-NeRD impressions and 12.5\% of MIND's.

❯ \documentclass[11pt]{article}
  \usepackage[margin=1in]{geometry}
  \usepackage{booktabs}
  \usepackage{array}
  \usepackage{amsmath}
  \usepackage{enumitem}
  \usepackage{hyperref}

  \usepackage{textgreek}
