  \setlist{nosep, leftmargin=1.4em}
  \setlength{\parskip}{0.35em}
  \setlength{\parindent}{0pt}
  
  \sloppy
  \title{\vspace{-1.5em}CS4.406 Assignment 2: Design Note\\
  \large Learning from Click-Logs on EB-NeRD and MIND}
  \author{}
  \date{}
  
  \begin{document}
  \maketitle
  \vspace{-3em}
  
  \section*{What Was Built}
  A two stage retrieve-then-rank pipeline for the news recommender and evaluated on the MIND and the EB-NeRD and reproduced the NRMS baseline and one improvement to it by adding freshness effect on the history (freshness arm) will elaborate on this in later sections.
  
  \section{Data Pipeline}
  Both datasets are parsed into one schema: an articles table (title, abstract, body, category, entities, published\_time) and a behaviors table (one row per impression: history, candidates, labels, timestamp). EB-NeRD additionally carries session\_id and per-history-click dwell times; MIND has neither, and that asymmetry recurs throughout the results.
  
  The splits are temporal and not Random, To prevent the future leakage while training.
  
  And the Article features like the freshness,popularity,CTR are strictly calculated based on the data before the time T.
  
  Within Session Features count only the impressions that occurred before this impression.
  
  \section{Stage 1: Candidate generation}
  \subsection{The Problem}
      Assignment 1 retrieved the top-k similarity between the user's click history and the whole catalogue. We used the same pipeline to generate the top-200 candidates but the recall was very low  2.55\% (EB-NeRD) \& 2.90\% (MIND). The best A1 configuration reached 4.39\% (EB-NeRD, BM25), and that too on the
  larger \texttt{ebnerd\_small} bundle; on the demo bundle we run here the same
  pipeline gives 2.55\%.
  
  \subsection{The fix}
  
  The ceiling was not in the data. It came from where stage 1 searched and what it
  ranked by. Clicks go to whatever is in circulation at that moment. EB-NeRD's
  median clicked article is 3.1 hours old when it is clicked, and 92\% are clicked
  within a day of publication. A1 searched the full catalogue of 11{,}777
  articles, spanning publication dates from 2000 to 2023, where 17.2\% were
  published over a year before the test period. Ranking a week's worth of clicks
  against two decades of articles is the wrong search space. Two mistakes
  compounded.
  
  \textbf{1. The search space was wrong.} We restricted A1's BM25, unchanged, to
  the articles the platform displayed in the previous hour. EB-NeRD recall@200
  goes from 2.55\% to 84.2\% on the scoring alone.
  
  \textbf{2. The ranking signal was wrong.} Ranking that same pool by recent
  popularity instead of history similarity reaches 97.0\% and 93.9\%. History
  similarity adds nothing on top of it.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{EB-NeRD @50/@100/@200} & \textbf{MIND @50/@100/@200} \\
      \midrule
      \texttt{bm25}, A1 over the whole catalogue   & 0.82 / 1.41 / \textbf{2.55\%}  & 1.27 / 1.96 / \textbf{2.90\%} \\
      \texttt{bm25\_fresh}, A1 scoring on a 1 h pool & 21.9 / 42.9 / \textbf{84.2\%} & 10.0 / 14.2 / \textbf{21.0\%} \\
      \texttt{popular}, 1 h pool with lagged clicks & 83.4 / 93.8 / \textbf{97.0\%} & 81.6 / 88.6 / \textbf{93.9\%} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test recall@K over full populations. The \texttt{bm25} row reproduces
    our own rerun of the A1 pipeline on the A2 splits, so the three arms are
    like-for-like. It is lower than A1's reported 4.39\% because A1 used
    \texttt{ebnerd\_small} and we use the demo bundle.}
  \end{table}
  
  If the one-hour pool holds fewer than $K$ articles
  we backfill from the previous 24 hours. We picked the window by measuring it. On 2{,}000-impression samples, 1 hour beat
  both 6 and 24 hours, because longer windows let stale but popular articles crowd
  out fresh ones. 
  
  \section{Stage 2: The Re-ranker}
      \subsection{The Objective}
      \textbf{LambdaMART}: Uses the Gradient Boosting Trees (MART) and the LambdaRank optimization which effectively learns to rank items which will improve the metrics like nDCG. It is a \textit{pairwise gradient which weighted by listwise metric}.\\
      We set \textit{lambdarank\_truncation\_level} to 200, the full group size, so that the full-group setting makes the \textbar{}ΔnDCG\textbar{} weights reflect the whole 200-item list the metrics are computed over.
  
      \subsection{The features}
      Used \textbf{24} features for training this model.
      
      \textbf{Click history: }click count, recency-weighted engagement, has\_history\_timestamps, avg history dwell time + availability flag \\
      \textbf{Session: }hour, day-of-week, impression size, session position, impressions/clicks earlier in session\\
      \textbf{Article: }click count, display count, log click count, smoothed CTR, log freshness, has\_known\_publish\_time. \\
      \textbf{history × candidate: } category binary match, category affinity, history\_title, history\_embedding \\
      \textbf{From Stage :1} retrieval rank, position bias, retrieval score
  
      \subsection{Results}
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering\small
        \begin{tabular}{lccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Re-ranked} & \textbf{$\Delta$ MRR (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          EB-NeRD AUC     & 0.8815 & \textbf{0.9219} & \\
          EB-NeRD MRR     & 0.1639 & \textbf{0.2115} & \textbf{+0.0476} [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$] \\
          EB-NeRD nDCG@5  & 0.1471 & 0.2026 & \\
          EB-NeRD nDCG@10 & 0.1994 & 0.2705 & \\
          \midrule
          MIND AUC        & 0.8655 & \textbf{0.8891} & \\
          MIND MRR        & 0.2237 & \textbf{0.2403} & \textbf{+0.0167} [$+0.0149$, $+0.0183$] \\
          MIND nDCG@5     & 0.2451 & 0.2618 & \\
          MIND nDCG@10    & 0.2932 & 0.3150 & \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
        \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates, $K=200$. Paired
        bootstrap over impressions, 2{,}000 resamples. Both gains clear zero.}
      \end{table}
  
  
  \section{Baseline reproduced, then improved (Q3)}
  
      \subsection{Baseline}
      NRMS (Wu et al., 2019) reproduced against ebnerd-benchmark's hparams\_nrms: multi-head self-attention news encoder, self-attention user encoder, dot-product scorer, Wu-2019 negative sampling (1 positive + 4 negatives, softmax cross-entropy). Deviations from the benchmark are documented rather than silent — including that it uses no attention masking, so pad tokens participate in attention, which we reproduce deliberately.
      
      \subsection{The Improvement: freshness weighting}
      NRMS reads only title tokens, so it has no channel at all for how old an article is when shown. By analyzing the data we found out that the freshness plays a key role in the ranking.
      We added age term to the NRMS score \\
      score = ⟨u, c⟩ + g(log(1 + age\_hours), known) \\
      where g is a 2→16→1 MLP and known marks whether the article's age is available. EB-NeRD ages come from published\_time. MIND has no publish dates, so we use the first time the article appears in the display log. The head adds 65 parameters to models. The term is added to the logit rather than concatenated into the news vector, which leaves the NRMS dot-product scorer unchanged.
  
      \subsection{Results}
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering
        \small
        \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{\shortstack{EB-NeRD base $\to$\\+fresh}} 
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} 
          & \textbf{\shortstack{MIND base $\to$\\+fresh}} 
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          AUC     & $0.5425 \to 0.6112$ & \textbf{+0.0686} [$+0.0662$, $+0.0710$] & $0.6040 \to 0.6042$ & $+0.0002$ [$-0.0006$, $+0.0010$] \\
          \midrule
          MRR     & $0.3394 \to 0.3962$ & \textbf{+0.0567} [$+0.0538$, $+0.0595$] & $0.2750 \to 0.2743$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0014$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \midrule
          nDCG@5  & $0.3770 \to 0.4427$ & \textbf{+0.0657} [$+0.0628$, $+0.0685$] & $0.2961 \to 0.2949$ & $-0.0012$ [$-0.0021$, $-0.0003$] \\
          \midrule
          nDCG@10 & $0.4570 \to 0.5095$ & \textbf{+0.0525} [$+0.0501$, $+0.0548$] & $0.3617 \to 0.3610$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0015$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
      \end{table}
  
      \textbf{EB-NeRD }all four metrics improved but for \textbf{MIND} there was no effect,  every change is smaller than 0.0015.\\
      \textbf{Why MIND shows nothing: }The age term is additive on the logit, so it can only reorder articles \emph{within} an impression --- and MIND's impressions are near-uniform in age (median within-impression spread \textbf{27.5\,h}, against \textbf{1{,}057\,h} on EB-NeRD), so a term that is nearly constant across a row cannot reorder it. It is not a data gap (99.9\% of MIND candidates and clicks carry an age) and not the first-seen proxy either: EB-NeRD has both age definitions, and swapping real publish times for the proxy costs only \textbf{0.013} AUC (0.6765 $\to$ 0.6631). The defensible claim is thus ``freshness-as-first-seen does not help on MIND'', not ``freshness does not help''.
  
  \section{Serving and scale}
  
  Measured locally: components built in isolated processes, so each figure is the
  exact array bytes where walkable and resident-set growth otherwise. Latency is
  single-threaded on an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores, 14 GiB RAM), 1{,}000 timed
  requests after 50 warm-up, $K=200$. Stage-1 log replay (0.02--0.03
  ms/impression) is excluded: a live system maintains those counters as events
  arrive.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{ANN index}} \\
      \quad Embedding matrix (brute-force exact cosine scan) & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Feature store}} \\
      \quad Article text (titles, abstracts)                 & 35.8 & 61.8 \\
      \quad Article embeddings      & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\footnotesize same array as above, counted once} \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Other resident state}} \\
      \quad BM25 inverted index (history-title feature)      &  4.1 & 36.0 \\
      \quad As-of article statistics                         &  4.8 & 68.1 \\
      \quad Fresh-pool event log                             &  7.5 & 26.5 \\
      \quad LightGBM booster (174 / 168 trees)               &  4.0 &  3.9 \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total (MiB)} & \textbf{90.7} & \textbf{291.9} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Our ANN index is a brute-force exact cosine scan over the feature store's
  embedding matrix, so it adds no structure of its own: the 34.5 / 95.6 MiB is
  the matrix itself, shared between the ANN index and the feature store. Both
  catalogues are under 100k articles, where a single matrix multiply is exact and
  faster than an approximate index; the trade reverses with catalogue size, which
  is the scaling point below.
  
  The feature store dominates: text plus embeddings are 78\% of EB-NeRD's
  footprint and 54\% of MIND's. The two structures serving the history-similarity
  features (embeddings and BM25) cost 131.6 MiB on MIND, which buys a significant
  ranking gain there but none on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring}
      & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 & 3.42 & 2.58 & 5.97 & 10.50 \\
      MIND    & 0.56 & 2.72 & 0.14 & 3.47 &  8.00 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Milliseconds for a single user request, single core, $K=200$.
    Stage 1 is candidate generation, features and scoring are re-ranking.}
  \end{table}
  
  Feature building is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 is under 10\%. Neither
  retrieval nor the GBDT is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature
  assembly.
  
  \subsection{Cost per 1000 queries at the SLA}
  
  p99 is roughly 10$\times$ inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. At 170
  (EB-NeRD) and 253 (MIND) single-core QPS and \$0.17 per vCPU-hour, that is
  \$0.000278 and \$0.000186 per thousand queries, under a dollar of compute for
  a million requests a day. 
  
  \section{Where the pipeline breaks at 10$\times$}
  
  Latency is linear in $K$ with a fixed per-impression cost of about 1.2 ms
  (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K = 50 / 100 / 200$; roughly
  $1.2 + 0.023K$). $K$ is therefore the throughput knob, and on EB-NeRD halving it costs little recall (93.8\% at $K=100$ against 97.0\% at $K=200$; MIND
  88.6\% against 93.9\%).
  
  Ranked by what fails first:
  
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{As-of article statistics: the binding constraint.} It must be
      resident and grows with total events: MIND's 8.93M display and click events
      occupy 68.1 MiB at 8 bytes each, so about 680 MiB at 10$\times$ traffic and
      about 4.4 GB at MINDlarge scale ($\sim$65$\times$).
      This is the first thing to shard or move to a windowed counter store.
    \item \textbf{CPU for feature assembly.} 170--253 QPS per core means about ten
      cores at 10$\times$. Parallel across requests, so a cost line rather than a
      wall.
    \item \textbf{The ANN index and feature store, on a different axis.} Both grow
      with \emph{catalogue size}, not traffic, so 10$\times$ traffic does not touch
      them, but a 10$\times$ catalogue does, and it is the point where the
      brute-force scan stops paying and the embedding matrix needs a real
      approximate index instead of a resident array.
  \end{enumerate}
  
  
  \section{Beyond accuracy (Q5)}
  
  Coverage, diversity and novelty on the top-10 lists, over a 5{,}000-impression
  sample with 1{,}000 bootstrap resamples, plus two slices.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Metric (top-10)} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      Catalogue coverage      & 0.0696 [0.0690, 0.0701] & 0.0065 [0.0063, 0.0067] \\
      \quad catalogue size    & 11{,}777 & 65{,}238 \\
      Diversity (raw)         & 0.0370 [0.0363, 0.0378] & 0.9008 [0.8997, 0.9019] \\
      \quad random baseline   & 0.0484 & 0.9402 \\
      \quad \textbf{ratio to random} & \textbf{0.765} & \textbf{0.958} \\
      Novelty ($-\log_2 p$)   & 14.456 & 17.339 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Raw diversity is not comparable across datasets.} It is dominated by how
  anisotropic the embedding space is: a random EB-NeRD pair already scores 0.048
  under the provided multilingual BERT vectors (cosine 0.95, a narrow cone),
  against 0.940 for MiniLM on MIND. Read the ratio to that baseline, where 1.0 is
  ``as diverse as random''. EB-NeRD sits at \textbf{0.765} and MIND at
  \textbf{0.958}, so EB-NeRD concentrates, which the raw numbers suggest the
  opposite of.
  
  \textbf{Coverage is low on both, and that is the price of Stage 1.} Recommending
  from a one-hour display window can only surface what was in circulation, so MIND
  reaches 0.65\% of its catalogue. The fresh pool that fixed recall also
  guarantees a small, recent, popular recommendation set.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llcc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Slice} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{MRR (95\% CI)} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) &     67 & 0.2678 [0.2005, 0.3447] \\
        & warm                       & 25{,}289 & 0.2114 [0.2079, 0.2146] \\
        & head article clicked       &    197 & 0.0072 [0.0062, 0.0082] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 25{,}159 & 0.2131 [0.2099, 0.2165] \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) & 12{,}982 & 0.2426 [0.2375, 0.2476] \\
        & warm                       & 60{,}170 & 0.2399 [0.2375, 0.2421] \\
        & head article clicked       &  9{,}150 & 0.1613 [0.1568, 0.1661] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 64{,}002 & 0.2516 [0.2494, 0.2541] \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Head is the top 20\% of articles by train click count.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Cold start is not a weakness.} MIND's cold-start users score 0.2426
  against warm users' 0.2399 with overlapping intervals, because most of the
  signal is article-side popularity and freshness, which needs no history.
  EB-NeRD's slice holds 67 impressions and is too wide to read.
  
  \textbf{The head slice is where the pipeline fails.} When the clicked article is
  among the 20\% most-clicked in \emph{train}, EB-NeRD MRR collapses to 0.0072
  against 0.2131 on the tail, and MIND falls to 0.1613 against 0.2516. Same cause
  as Stage 1: train-popular articles are old news by test time, so they are rarely
  in a one-hour pool, and an unretrieved candidate cannot be recovered by any
  re-ranker. It affects 0.8\% of EB-NeRD impressions and 12.5\% of MIND's.
  
  \section{Design Choices}
      \subsection{Re-Ranker Objective}
      The choices were pointwise \textit{binary} , LambdaMART and the list wise \textit{rank\_xendcg}. RankNet was rejected because it minimizes the number of inversions and doesnt treat the inversion between rank 1, 2 and rank 100, 101 differently. The pointwise is rejected because they predict the absolute relevance score of each item in isolation, completely ignoring how those items compare to each other. 
      
      \subsection{Stage-1 candidate source}
      In the stage 1 the BM25 and the semantic retrieval recovered only \textbf{2.55\% (EB-NeRD) / 2.90\% (MIND)} of impressions at $K=200$. And we found that the median clicked-article age at click time is 3.1\,h. A display-log fresh pool reached \textbf{97.0\% / 93.9\%}. Within that pool, a 1\,h window beat 6\,h and 24\,h (EB-NeRD 97.4\% vs.\ 88.9\% @200) because longer windows let stale-but-popular articles crowd out fresh ones.
  
      \subsection{NRMS improvement (Q3)}
      The initial idea was adding the positional encoding on the user encoder. It was rejected on measurement: at \texttt{HISTORY\_SIZE}~=~20, \textbf{51.1\%} of MIND impressions carry fewer than 20 clicks and \textbf{29.4\%} of history slots are padding, and \texttt{layers.py} applies no masking  so PE would attach position embeddings to padding on exactly the dataset where padding dominates. \textbf{Freshness} won on a category difference rather than a magnitude one: article age is not in NRMS's input \emph{at all} and cannot be learned from title tokens at any amount of training.
  
  \section{Metrics with and without serving-time features}
  
  Every feature is as-of strictly before the impression it scores, so nothing
  reads the future. That is necessary but not sufficient: a feature can be
  leak-free and still be unavailable to a live system. Two fail that test.
  
  \textbf{\texttt{impression\_size}}, the length of the impression's own in-view
  list. On EB-NeRD that list grows with how long the reader stays on the page, and
  correlates with the same impression's \texttt{read\_time} at Spearman
  \textbf{0.50}, so it is measured from an outcome. On MIND it is the platform's
  list length, which a system choosing its own candidates is never handed.
  
  \textbf{\texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time}}, read off the dataset's later
  \texttt{published\_time} snapshot. No live system could know it.
  
  A stricter arm also drops \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}, which a session
  store would mostly hold but which applies no click reporting lag. Arms differ
  only in the feature list; the \texttt{all} arm reproduces the shipped report to
  within $10^{-14}$.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{AUC} & \textbf{MRR}
      & \textbf{nDCG@5} & \textbf{nDCG@10} \\
      & \textbf{nDCG@5} & \textbf{nDCG@10} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & stage 1 only          & --- & 0.8815 & 0.1639 & 0.1471 & 0.1994 \\
        & all                   & 24 & \textbf{0.9219} & \textbf{0.2115} & 0.2026 & 0.2705 \\
        & serving-safe          & 22 & 0.9102 & 0.1897 & 0.1754 & 0.2372 \\
        & serving-safe (strict) & 21 & 0.9103 & 0.1887 & 0.1739 & 0.2369 \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & stage 1 only          & --- & 0.8655 & 0.2237 & 0.2451 & 0.2932 \\
        & all                   & 24 & \textbf{0.8891} & \textbf{0.2403} & 0.2618 & 0.3150 \\
        & serving-safe          & 22 & 0.8866 & 0.2397 & 0.2619 & 0.3133 \\
        & serving-safe (strict) & 21 & 0.8859 & 0.2375 & 0.2599 & 0.3112 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates. Serving-safe drops
    \texttt{impression\_size} and \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time}; strict also
    drops \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{EB-NeRD's headline nearly halves.} Its gain over stage 1 falls from
  $+0.0476$ MRR [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$] to $+0.0258$ [$+0.0229$, $+0.0287$]. The
  honest claim is $+0.0258$. \textbf{MIND's holds}, $+0.0167$ [$+0.0149$,
  $+0.0183$] to $+0.0160$ [$+0.0145$, $+0.0175$], because it has the
  history-similarity features to fall back on and EB-NeRD does not: dropping
  \texttt{impression\_size} collapses EB-NeRD's model from 174 trees to 19, MIND's
  only to 108. \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time} carries \textbf{0.000\%} of
  split gain on both, confirming the fresh pool already closed the future-article
  shortcut.

  \title{\vspace{-1.5em}CS4.406 Assignment 2: Design Note\\
  \large Learning from Click-Logs on EB-NeRD and MIND}

  \begin{document}
  \maketitle
  \vspace{-3em}
  
  \section*{What Was Built}
  A two stage retrieve-then-rank pipeline for the news recommender and evaluated on the MIND and the EB-NeRD and reproduced the NRMS baseline and one improvement to it by adding freshness effect on the history (freshness arm) will elaborate on this in later sections.
  
  \section{Data Pipeline}
  Both datasets are parsed into one schema: an articles table (title, abstract, body, category, entities, published\_time) and a behaviors table (one row per impression: history, candidates, labels, timestamp). EB-NeRD additionally carries session\_id and per-history-click dwell times; MIND has neither, and that asymmetry recurs throughout the results.
  
  The splits are temporal and not Random, To prevent the future leakage while training.
  
  And the Article features like the freshness,popularity,CTR are strictly calculated based on the data before the time T.
  
  Within Session Features count only the impressions that occurred before this impression.
  
  \section{Stage 1: Candidate generation}
  \subsection{The Problem}
      Assignment 1 retrieved the top-k similarity between the user's click history and the whole catalogue. We used the same pipeline to generate the top-200 candidates but the recall was very low  2.55\% (EB-NeRD) \& 2.90\% (MIND). The best A1 configuration reached 4.39\% (EB-NeRD, BM25), and that too on the
  larger \texttt{ebnerd\_small} bundle; on the demo bundle we run here the same
  pipeline gives 2.55\%.
  
  \subsection{The fix}
  
  The ceiling was not in the data. It came from where stage 1 searched and what it
  ranked by. Clicks go to whatever is in circulation at that moment. EB-NeRD's
  median clicked article is 3.1 hours old when it is clicked, and 92\% are clicked
  within a day of publication. A1 searched the full catalogue of 11{,}777
  articles, spanning publication dates from 2000 to 2023, where 17.2\% were
  published over a year before the test period. Ranking a week's worth of clicks
  against two decades of articles is the wrong search space. Two mistakes
  compounded.
  
  \textbf{1. The search space was wrong.} We restricted A1's BM25, unchanged, to
  the articles the platform displayed in the previous hour. EB-NeRD recall@200
  goes from 2.55\% to 84.2\% on the scoring alone.
  
  \textbf{2. The ranking signal was wrong.} Ranking that same pool by recent
  popularity instead of history similarity reaches 97.0\% and 93.9\%. History
  similarity adds nothing on top of it.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{EB-NeRD @50/@100/@200} & \textbf{MIND @50/@100/@200} \\
      \midrule
      \texttt{bm25}, A1 over the whole catalogue   & 0.82 / 1.41 / \textbf{2.55\%}  & 1.27 / 1.96 / \textbf{2.90\%} \\
      \texttt{bm25\_fresh}, A1 scoring on a 1 h pool & 21.9 / 42.9 / \textbf{84.2\%} & 10.0 / 14.2 / \textbf{21.0\%} \\
      \texttt{popular}, 1 h pool with lagged clicks & 83.4 / 93.8 / \textbf{97.0\%} & 81.6 / 88.6 / \textbf{93.9\%} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test recall@K over full populations. The \texttt{bm25} row reproduces
    our own rerun of the A1 pipeline on the A2 splits, so the three arms are
    like-for-like. It is lower than A1's reported 4.39\% because A1 used
    \texttt{ebnerd\_small} and we use the demo bundle.}
  \end{table}
  
  If the one-hour pool holds fewer than $K$ articles
  we backfill from the previous 24 hours. We picked the window by measuring it. On 2{,}000-impression samples, 1 hour beat
  both 6 and 24 hours, because longer windows let stale but popular articles crowd
  out fresh ones. 
  
  \section{Stage 2: The Re-ranker}
      \subsection{The Objective}
      \textbf{LambdaMART}: Uses the Gradient Boosting Trees (MART) and the LambdaRank optimization which effectively learns to rank items which will improve the metrics like nDCG. It is a \textit{pairwise gradient which weighted by listwise metric}.\\
      We set \textit{lambdarank\_truncation\_level} to 200, the full group size, so that the full-group setting makes the \textbar{}ΔnDCG\textbar{} weights reflect the whole 200-item list the metrics are computed over.
  
      \subsection{The features}
      Used \textbf{24} features for training this model.
      
      \textbf{Click history: }click count, recency-weighted engagement, has\_history\_timestamps, avg history dwell time + availability flag \\
      \textbf{Session: }hour, day-of-week, impression size, session position, impressions/clicks earlier in session\\
      \textbf{Article: }click count, display count, log click count, smoothed CTR, log freshness, has\_known\_publish\_time. \\
      \textbf{history × candidate: } category binary match, category affinity, history\_title, history\_embedding \\
      \textbf{From Stage :1} retrieval rank, position bias, retrieval score
  
      \subsection{Results}
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering\small
        \begin{tabular}{lccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Re-ranked} & \textbf{$\Delta$ MRR (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          EB-NeRD AUC     & 0.8815 & \textbf{0.9219} & \\
          EB-NeRD MRR     & 0.1639 & \textbf{0.2115} & \textbf{+0.0476} [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$] \\
          EB-NeRD nDCG@5  & 0.1471 & 0.2026 & \\
          EB-NeRD nDCG@10 & 0.1994 & 0.2705 & \\
          \midrule
          MIND AUC        & 0.8655 & \textbf{0.8891} & \\
          MIND MRR        & 0.2237 & \textbf{0.2403} & \textbf{+0.0167} [$+0.0149$, $+0.0183$] \\
          MIND nDCG@5     & 0.2451 & 0.2618 & \\
          MIND nDCG@10    & 0.2932 & 0.3150 & \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
        \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates, $K=200$. Paired
        bootstrap over impressions, 2{,}000 resamples. Both gains clear zero.}
      \end{table}
  
  
  \section{Baseline reproduced, then improved (Q3)}
  
      \subsection{Baseline}
      NRMS (Wu et al., 2019) reproduced against ebnerd-benchmark's hparams\_nrms: multi-head self-attention news encoder, self-attention user encoder, dot-product scorer, Wu-2019 negative sampling (1 positive + 4 negatives, softmax cross-entropy). Deviations from the benchmark are documented rather than silent — including that it uses no attention masking, so pad tokens participate in attention, which we reproduce deliberately.
      
      \subsection{The Improvement: freshness weighting}
      NRMS reads only title tokens, so it has no channel at all for how old an article is when shown. By analyzing the data we found out that the freshness plays a key role in the ranking.
      We added age term to the NRMS score \\
      score = ⟨u, c⟩ + g(log(1 + age\_hours), known) \\
      where g is a 2→16→1 MLP and known marks whether the article's age is available. EB-NeRD ages come from published\_time. MIND has no publish dates, so we use the first time the article appears in the display log. The head adds 65 parameters to models. The term is added to the logit rather than concatenated into the news vector, which leaves the NRMS dot-product scorer unchanged.
  
      \subsection{Results}
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering
        \small
        \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{\shortstack{EB-NeRD base $\to$\\+fresh}} 
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} 
          & \textbf{\shortstack{MIND base $\to$\\+fresh}} 
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          AUC     & $0.5425 \to 0.6112$ & \textbf{+0.0686} [$+0.0662$, $+0.0710$] & $0.6040 \to 0.6042$ & $+0.0002$ [$-0.0006$, $+0.0010$] \\
          \midrule
          MRR     & $0.3394 \to 0.3962$ & \textbf{+0.0567} [$+0.0538$, $+0.0595$] & $0.2750 \to 0.2743$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0014$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \midrule
          nDCG@5  & $0.3770 \to 0.4427$ & \textbf{+0.0657} [$+0.0628$, $+0.0685$] & $0.2961 \to 0.2949$ & $-0.0012$ [$-0.0021$, $-0.0003$] \\
          \midrule
          nDCG@10 & $0.4570 \to 0.5095$ & \textbf{+0.0525} [$+0.0501$, $+0.0548$] & $0.3617 \to 0.3610$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0015$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
      \end{table}
  
      \textbf{EB-NeRD }all four metrics improved but for \textbf{MIND} there was no effect,  every change is smaller than 0.0015.\\
      \textbf{Why MIND shows nothing: }The age term is additive on the logit, so it can only reorder articles \emph{within} an impression --- and MIND's impressions are near-uniform in age (median within-impression spread \textbf{27.5\,h}, against \textbf{1{,}057\,h} on EB-NeRD), so a term that is nearly constant across a row cannot reorder it. It is not a data gap (99.9\% of MIND candidates and clicks carry an age) and not the first-seen proxy either: EB-NeRD has both age definitions, and swapping real publish times for the proxy costs only \textbf{0.013} AUC (0.6765 $\to$ 0.6631). The defensible claim is thus ``freshness-as-first-seen does not help on MIND'', not ``freshness does not help''.
  
  \section{Serving and scale}
  
  Measured locally: components built in isolated processes, so each figure is the
  exact array bytes where walkable and resident-set growth otherwise. Latency is
  single-threaded on an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores, 14 GiB RAM), 1{,}000 timed
  requests after 50 warm-up, $K=200$. Stage-1 log replay (0.02--0.03
  ms/impression) is excluded: a live system maintains those counters as events
  arrive.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{ANN index}} \\
      \quad Embedding matrix (brute-force exact cosine scan) & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Feature store}} \\
      \quad Article text (titles, abstracts)                 & 35.8 & 61.8 \\
      \quad Article embeddings      & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\footnotesize same array as above, counted once} \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Other resident state}} \\
      \quad BM25 inverted index (history-title feature)      &  4.1 & 36.0 \\
      \quad As-of article statistics                         &  4.8 & 68.1 \\
      \quad Fresh-pool event log                             &  7.5 & 26.5 \\
      \quad LightGBM booster (174 / 168 trees)               &  4.0 &  3.9 \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total (MiB)} & \textbf{90.7} & \textbf{291.9} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Our ANN index is a brute-force exact cosine scan over the feature store's
  embedding matrix, so it adds no structure of its own: the 34.5 / 95.6 MiB is
  the matrix itself, shared between the ANN index and the feature store. Both
  catalogues are under 100k articles, where a single matrix multiply is exact and
  faster than an approximate index; the trade reverses with catalogue size, which
  is the scaling point below.
  
  The feature store dominates: text plus embeddings are 78\% of EB-NeRD's
  footprint and 54\% of MIND's. The two structures serving the history-similarity
  features (embeddings and BM25) cost 131.6 MiB on MIND, which buys a significant
  ranking gain there but none on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring}
      & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 & 3.42 & 2.58 & 5.97 & 10.50 \\
      MIND    & 0.56 & 2.72 & 0.14 & 3.47 &  8.00 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Milliseconds for a single user request, single core, $K=200$.
    Stage 1 is candidate generation, features and scoring are re-ranking.}
  \end{table}
  
  Feature building is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 is under 10\%. Neither
  retrieval nor the GBDT is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature
  assembly.
  
  \subsection{Cost per 1000 queries at the SLA}
  
  p99 is roughly 10$\times$ inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. At 170
  (EB-NeRD) and 253 (MIND) single-core QPS and \$0.17 per vCPU-hour, that is
  \$0.000278 and \$0.000186 per thousand queries, under a dollar of compute for
  a million requests a day. 
  
  \section{Where the pipeline breaks at 10$\times$}
  
  Latency is linear in $K$ with a fixed per-impression cost of about 1.2 ms
  (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K = 50 / 100 / 200$; roughly
  $1.2 + 0.023K$). $K$ is therefore the throughput knob, and on EB-NeRD halving it costs little recall (93.8\% at $K=100$ against 97.0\% at $K=200$; MIND
  88.6\% against 93.9\%).
  
  Ranked by what fails first:
  
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{As-of article statistics: the binding constraint.} It must be
      resident and grows with total events: MIND's 8.93M display and click events
      occupy 68.1 MiB at 8 bytes each, so about 680 MiB at 10$\times$ traffic and
      about 4.4 GB at MINDlarge scale ($\sim$65$\times$).
      This is the first thing to shard or move to a windowed counter store.
    \item \textbf{CPU for feature assembly.} 170--253 QPS per core means about ten
      cores at 10$\times$. Parallel across requests, so a cost line rather than a
      wall.
    \item \textbf{The ANN index and feature store, on a different axis.} Both grow
      with \emph{catalogue size}, not traffic, so 10$\times$ traffic does not touch
      them, but a 10$\times$ catalogue does, and it is the point where the
      brute-force scan stops paying and the embedding matrix needs a real
      approximate index instead of a resident array.
  \end{enumerate}
  
  
  \section{Beyond accuracy (Q5)}
  
  Coverage, diversity and novelty on the top-10 lists, over a 5{,}000-impression
  sample with 1{,}000 bootstrap resamples, plus two slices.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Metric (top-10)} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      Catalogue coverage      & 0.0696 [0.0690, 0.0701] & 0.0065 [0.0063, 0.0067] \\
      \quad catalogue size    & 11{,}777 & 65{,}238 \\
      Diversity (raw)         & 0.0370 [0.0363, 0.0378] & 0.9008 [0.8997, 0.9019] \\
      \quad random baseline   & 0.0484 & 0.9402 \\
      \quad \textbf{ratio to random} & \textbf{0.765} & \textbf{0.958} \\
      Novelty ($-\log_2 p$)   & 14.456 & 17.339 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Raw diversity is not comparable across datasets.} It is dominated by how
  anisotropic the embedding space is: a random EB-NeRD pair already scores 0.048
  under the provided multilingual BERT vectors (cosine 0.95, a narrow cone),
  against 0.940 for MiniLM on MIND. Read the ratio to that baseline, where 1.0 is
  ``as diverse as random''. EB-NeRD sits at \textbf{0.765} and MIND at
  \textbf{0.958}, so EB-NeRD concentrates, which the raw numbers suggest the
  opposite of.
  
  \textbf{Coverage is low on both, and that is the price of Stage 1.} Recommending
  from a one-hour display window can only surface what was in circulation, so MIND
  reaches 0.65\% of its catalogue. The fresh pool that fixed recall also
  guarantees a small, recent, popular recommendation set.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llcc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Slice} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{MRR (95\% CI)} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) &     67 & 0.2678 [0.2005, 0.3447] \\
        & warm                       & 25{,}289 & 0.2114 [0.2079, 0.2146] \\
        & head article clicked       &    197 & 0.0072 [0.0062, 0.0082] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 25{,}159 & 0.2131 [0.2099, 0.2165] \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) & 12{,}982 & 0.2426 [0.2375, 0.2476] \\
        & warm                       & 60{,}170 & 0.2399 [0.2375, 0.2421] \\
        & head article clicked       &  9{,}150 & 0.1613 [0.1568, 0.1661] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 64{,}002 & 0.2516 [0.2494, 0.2541] \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Head is the top 20\% of articles by train click count.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Cold start is not a weakness.} MIND's cold-start users score 0.2426
  against warm users' 0.2399 with overlapping intervals, because most of the
  signal is article-side popularity and freshness, which needs no history.
  EB-NeRD's slice holds 67 impressions and is too wide to read.
  
  \textbf{The head slice is where the pipeline fails.} When the clicked article is
  among the 20\% most-clicked in \emph{train}, EB-NeRD MRR collapses to 0.0072
  against 0.2131 on the tail, and MIND falls to 0.1613 against 0.2516. Same cause
  as Stage 1: train-popular articles are old news by test time, so they are rarely
  in a one-hour pool, and an unretrieved candidate cannot be recovered by any
  re-ranker. It affects 0.8\% of EB-NeRD impressions and 12.5\% of MIND's.
  
  \section{Design Choices}
      \subsection{Re-Ranker Objective}
      The choices were pointwise \textit{binary} , LambdaMART and the list wise \textit{rank\_xendcg}. RankNet was rejected because it minimizes the number of inversions and doesnt treat the inversion between rank 1, 2 and rank 100, 101 differently. The pointwise is rejected because they predict the absolute relevance score of each item in isolation, completely ignoring how those items compare to each other. 
      
      \subsection{Stage-1 candidate source}
      In the stage 1 the BM25 and the semantic retrieval recovered only \textbf{2.55\% (EB-NeRD) / 2.90\% (MIND)} of impressions at $K=200$. And we found that the median clicked-article age at click time is 3.1\,h. A display-log fresh pool reached \textbf{97.0\% / 93.9\%}. Within that pool, a 1\,h window beat 6\,h and 24\,h (EB-NeRD 97.4\% vs.\ 88.9\% @200) because longer windows let stale-but-popular articles crowd out fresh ones.
  
      \subsection{NRMS improvement (Q3)}
      The initial idea was adding the positional encoding on the user encoder. It was rejected on measurement: at \texttt{HISTORY\_SIZE}~=~20, \textbf{51.1\%} of MIND impressions carry fewer than 20 clicks and \textbf{29.4\%} of history slots are padding, and \texttt{layers.py} applies no masking  so PE would attach position embeddings to padding on exactly the dataset where padding dominates. \textbf{Freshness} won on a category difference rather than a magnitude one: article age is not in NRMS's input \emph{at all} and cannot be learned from title tokens at any amount of training.
  
  \section{Metrics with and without serving-time features}
  
  Every feature is as-of strictly before the impression it scores, so nothing
  reads the future. That is necessary but not sufficient: a feature can be
  leak-free and still be unavailable to a live system. Two fail that test.
  
  \textbf{\texttt{impression\_size}}, the length of the impression's own in-view
  list. On EB-NeRD that list grows with how long the reader stays on the page, and
  correlates with the same impression's \texttt{read\_time} at Spearman
  \textbf{0.50}, so it is measured from an outcome. On MIND it is the platform's
  list length, which a system choosing its own candidates is never handed.
  
  \textbf{\texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time}}, read off the dataset's later
  \texttt{published\_time} snapshot. No live system could know it.
  
  A stricter arm also drops \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}, which a session
  store would mostly hold but which applies no click reporting lag. Arms differ
  only in the feature list; the \texttt{all} arm reproduces the shipped report to
  within $10^{-14}$.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{AUC} & \textbf{MRR}
      & \textbf{nDCG@5} & \textbf{nDCG@10} \\
      & \textbf{nDCG@5} & \textbf{nDCG@10} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & stage 1 only          & --- & 0.8815 & 0.1639 & 0.1471 & 0.1994 \\
        & all                   & 24 & \textbf{0.9219} & \textbf{0.2115} & 0.2026 & 0.2705 \\
        & serving-safe          & 22 & 0.9102 & 0.1897 & 0.1754 & 0.2372 \\
        & serving-safe (strict) & 21 & 0.9103 & 0.1887 & 0.1739 & 0.2369 \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & stage 1 only          & --- & 0.8655 & 0.2237 & 0.2451 & 0.2932 \\
        & all                   & 24 & \textbf{0.8891} & \textbf{0.2403} & 0.2618 & 0.3150 \\
        & serving-safe          & 22 & 0.8866 & 0.2397 & 0.2619 & 0.3133 \\
        & serving-safe (strict) & 21 & 0.8859 & 0.2375 & 0.2599 & 0.3112 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates. Serving-safe drops
    \texttt{impression\_size} and \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time}; strict also
    drops \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{EB-NeRD's headline nearly halves.} Its gain over stage 1 falls from
  $+0.0476$ MRR [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$] to $+0.0258$ [$+0.0229$, $+0.0287$]. The
  honest claim is $+0.0258$. \textbf{MIND's holds}, $+0.0167$ [$+0.0149$,
  $+0.0183$] to $+0.0160$ [$+0.0145$, $+0.0175$], because it has the
  history-similarity features to fall back on and EB-NeRD does not: dropping
  \texttt{impression\_size} collapses EB-NeRD's model from 174 trees to 19, MIND's
  only to 108. \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time} carries \textbf{0.000\%} of
  split gain on both, confirming the fresh pool already closed the future-article
  shortcut.

  \sloppy
  \title{\vspace{-1.5em}CS4.406 Assignment 2: Design Note\\
  \large Learning from Click-Logs on EB-NeRD and MIND}
  \author{}
  \date{}
  
  \begin{document}
  \maketitle
  \vspace{-3em}
  
  \section*{What Was Built}
  A two-stage retrieve-then-rank news recommender, evaluated on EB-NeRD and MIND,
  plus a reproduction of the NRMS baseline and one improvement to it: a freshness
  term that gives the model a channel for how old an article is when shown.
  
  \section{Data Pipeline}
  Both datasets are parsed into one schema: an articles table (title, abstract, body, category, entities, published\_time) and a behaviors table (one row per impression: history, candidates, labels, timestamp). EB-NeRD additionally carries session\_id and per-history-click dwell times; MIND has neither, and that asymmetry recurs throughout the results.
  
  Splits are temporal rather than random, to prevent future leakage during training. Article features such as freshness, popularity and CTR are computed strictly from data before the impression time $t$, and within-session features count only impressions that occurred earlier in the same session.
  
  \section{Stage 1: Candidate generation}
  \subsection{The Problem}
      Assignment 1 retrieved the top-k similarity between the user's click history and the whole catalogue. We used the same pipeline to generate the top-200 candidates but the recall was very low, 2.55\% (EB-NeRD) and 2.90\% (MIND). The best A1 configuration reached 4.39\% (EB-NeRD, BM25), and that too on the larger \texttt{ebnerd\_small} bundle; on the demo bundle we run here the same pipeline gives 2.55\%.
  
  \subsection{The fix}
  
  The ceiling was not in the data. It came from where stage 1 searched and what it
  ranked by. Clicks go to whatever is in circulation at that moment. EB-NeRD's
  median clicked article is 3.1 hours old when it is clicked, and 92\% are clicked
  within a day of publication. A1 searched the full catalogue of 11{,}777
  articles, spanning publication dates from 2000 to 2023, where 17.2\% were
  published over a year before the test period. Ranking a week's worth of clicks
  against two decades of articles is the wrong search space. Two mistakes
  compounded.
  
  \textbf{1. The search space was wrong.} We restricted A1's BM25, unchanged, to
  the articles the platform displayed in the previous hour. EB-NeRD recall@200
  goes from 2.55\% to 84.2\% on the scoring alone.
  
  \textbf{2. The ranking signal was wrong.} Ranking that same pool by recent
  popularity instead of history similarity reaches 97.0\% and 93.9\%. History
  similarity adds nothing on top of it.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{EB-NeRD @50/@100/@200} & \textbf{MIND @50/@100/@200} \\
      \midrule
      \texttt{bm25}, A1 over the whole catalogue   & 0.82 / 1.41 / \textbf{2.55\%}  & 1.27 / 1.96 / \textbf{2.90\%} \\
      \texttt{bm25\_fresh}, A1 scoring on a 1 h pool & 21.9 / 42.9 / \textbf{84.2\%} & 10.0 / 14.2 / \textbf{21.0\%} \\
      \texttt{popular}, 1 h pool with lagged clicks & 83.4 / 93.8 / \textbf{97.0\%} & 81.6 / 88.6 / \textbf{93.9\%} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test recall@K over full populations. The \texttt{bm25} row reproduces
    our own rerun of the A1 pipeline on the A2 splits, so the three arms are
    like-for-like.}
  \end{table}
  
  The pool uses only events strictly before the impression time $t$: displays in
  $[t-1\text{h},\,t)$, and clicks from impressions in
  $[t-1\text{h},\,t-10\text{min})$, because a click lands after the impression
  that produced it. If the one-hour pool holds fewer than $K$ articles we backfill
  from the previous 24 hours. We picked the window by measuring it: on
  2{,}000-impression samples, 1 hour beat both 6 and 24 hours (EB-NeRD 97.4\%
  against 88.9\% @200), because longer windows let stale but popular articles
  crowd out fresh ones.
  
  \section{Stage 2: The Re-ranker}
      \subsection{The Objective}
      \textbf{LambdaMART}: Uses the Gradient Boosting Trees (MART) and the LambdaRank optimization which effectively learns to rank items which will improve the metrics like nDCG. It is a \textit{pairwise gradient which weighted by listwise metric}.\\
      We set \textit{lambdarank\_truncation\_level} to 200, the full group size, so that the full-group setting makes the \textbar{}ΔnDCG\textbar{} weights reflect the whole 200-item list the metrics are computed over.
  
      \subsection{The features}
      Used \textbf{24} features for training this model.
  
      \textbf{Click history: }click count, recency-weighted engagement, has\_history\_timestamps, avg history dwell time + availability flag \\
      \textbf{Session: }hour, day-of-week, impression size, session position, impressions/clicks earlier in session\\
      \textbf{Article: }click count, display count, log click count, smoothed CTR, log freshness, has\_known\_publish\_time. \\
      \textbf{history × candidate: } category binary match, category affinity, history\_title, history\_embedding \\
      \textbf{From Stage 1: } retrieval rank, position bias, retrieval score
  
      \subsection{Results}
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering\small
        \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{AUC} & \textbf{MRR} & \textbf{nDCG@5} & \textbf{nDCG@10}
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ MRR (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          EB-NeRD stage 1 & 0.8815 & 0.1639 & 0.1471 & 0.1994 & \\
          \quad re-ranked & \textbf{0.9219} & \textbf{0.2115} & 0.2026 & 0.2705
            & \textbf{+0.0476} [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$] \\
          \addlinespace
          MIND stage 1    & 0.8655 & 0.2237 & 0.2451 & 0.2932 & \\
          \quad re-ranked & \textbf{0.8891} & \textbf{0.2403} & 0.2618 & 0.3150
            & \textbf{+0.0167} [$+0.0149$, $+0.0183$] \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
        \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates, $K=200$. Paired
        bootstrap over impressions, 2{,}000 resamples. Both gains clear zero.}
      \end{table}
  
  \section{Baseline reproduced, then improved (Q3)}
  
      \subsection{Baseline}
      NRMS (Wu et al., 2019) reproduced against ebnerd-benchmark's hparams\_nrms: multi-head self-attention news encoder, self-attention user encoder, dot-product scorer, Wu-2019 negative sampling (1 positive + 4 negatives, softmax cross-entropy). Deviations from the benchmark are documented rather than silent, including that it uses no attention masking, so pad tokens participate in attention, which we reproduce deliberately.
  
      \subsection{The Improvement: freshness weighting}
      NRMS reads only title tokens, so it has no channel at all for how old an article is when shown. By analyzing the data we found out that the freshness plays a key role in the ranking.
      We added age term to the NRMS score \\
      score = ⟨u, c⟩ + g(log(1 + age\_hours), known) \\
      where g is a 2→16→1 MLP and known marks whether the article's age is available. EB-NeRD ages come from published\_time. MIND has no publish dates, so we use the first time the article appears in the display log. The head adds 65 parameters to models. The term is added to the logit rather than concatenated into the news vector, which leaves the NRMS dot-product scorer unchanged.
  
      \subsection{Results}
      \begin{table}[htbp]
        \centering
        \small
        \begin{tabular}{lcccc}
          \toprule
          & \textbf{\shortstack{EB-NeRD base $\to$\\+fresh}}
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)}
          & \textbf{\shortstack{MIND base $\to$\\+fresh}}
          & \textbf{$\Delta$ (95\% CI)} \\
          \midrule
          AUC     & $0.5425 \to 0.6112$ & \textbf{+0.0686} [$+0.0662$, $+0.0710$] & $0.6040 \to 0.6042$ & $+0.0002$ [$-0.0006$, $+0.0010$] \\
          MRR     & $0.3394 \to 0.3962$ & \textbf{+0.0567} [$+0.0538$, $+0.0595$] & $0.2750 \to 0.2743$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0014$, $+0.0001$] \\
          nDCG@5  & $0.3770 \to 0.4427$ & \textbf{+0.0657} [$+0.0628$, $+0.0685$] & $0.2961 \to 0.2949$ & $-0.0012$ [$-0.0021$, $-0.0003$] \\
          nDCG@10 & $0.4570 \to 0.5095$ & \textbf{+0.0525} [$+0.0501$, $+0.0548$] & $0.3617 \to 0.3610$ & $-0.0007$ [$-0.0015$, $+0.0001$] \\
          \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
      \end{table}
  
      \textbf{Why MIND shows nothing: }The age term is additive on the logit, so it can only reorder articles \emph{within} an impression, and MIND's impressions are near-uniform in age (median within-impression spread \textbf{27.5\,h}, against \textbf{1{,}057\,h} on EB-NeRD), so a term that is nearly constant across a row cannot reorder it. It is not a data gap (99.9\% of MIND candidates and clicks carry an age) and not the first-seen proxy either: EB-NeRD has both age definitions, and swapping real publish times for the proxy costs only \textbf{0.013} AUC (0.6765 $\to$ 0.6631). The defensible claim is thus ``freshness-as-first-seen does not help on MIND'', not ``freshness does not help''.
  
  \section{Serving and scale}
  
  Measured locally: components built in isolated processes, so each figure is the
  exact array bytes where walkable and resident-set growth otherwise. Latency is
  single-threaded on an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (16 cores, 14 GiB RAM), 1{,}000 timed
  requests after 50 warm-up, $K=200$. Stage-1 log replay (0.02--0.03
  ms/impression) is excluded: a live system maintains those counters as events
  arrive.
  
  \subsection{Index memory}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Component} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{ANN index}} \\
      \quad Embedding matrix (brute-force exact cosine scan) & 34.5 & 95.6 \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Feature store}} \\
      \quad Article text (titles, abstracts)                 & 35.8 & 61.8 \\
      \quad Article embeddings      & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\footnotesize same array as above, counted once} \\
      \addlinespace
      \multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Other resident state}} \\
      \quad BM25 inverted index (history-title feature)      &  4.1 & 36.0 \\
      \quad As-of article statistics                         &  4.8 & 68.1 \\
      \quad Fresh-pool event log                             &  7.5 & 26.5 \\
      \quad LightGBM booster (174 / 168 trees)               &  4.0 &  3.9 \\
      \midrule
      \textbf{Total (MiB)} & \textbf{90.7} & \textbf{291.9} \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  Our ANN index is a brute-force exact cosine scan over the feature store's
  embedding matrix, so it adds no structure of its own: the 34.5 / 95.6 MiB is the
  matrix itself, shared between the two. Both catalogues are under 100k articles,
  where a single matrix multiply is exact and faster than an approximate index.
  The feature store dominates, at 78\% of EB-NeRD's footprint and 54\% of MIND's,
  and the two structures serving the history-similarity features (embeddings and
  BM25) cost 131.6 MiB on MIND, which buys a significant ranking gain there but
  none on EB-NeRD.
  
  \subsection{Latency}
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Stage 1} & \textbf{Features} & \textbf{Scoring}
      & \textbf{Total p50} & \textbf{Total p99} \\
      \midrule
      EB-NeRD & 0.12 & 3.42 & 2.58 & 5.97 & 10.50 \\
      MIND    & 0.56 & 2.72 & 0.14 & 3.47 &  8.00 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Milliseconds for a single user request, single core, $K=200$.
    Stage 1 is candidate generation, features and scoring are re-ranking.}
  \end{table}
  
  Feature building is 60--75\% of a request and stage 1 is under 10\%. Neither
  retrieval nor the GBDT is the bottleneck, so latency work belongs in feature
  assembly.
  
  \subsection{Cost per 1000 queries at the SLA}
  
  p99 is roughly 10$\times$ inside the 100 ms SLA on both datasets. At 170
  (EB-NeRD) and 253 (MIND) single-core QPS and \$0.17 per vCPU-hour, that is
  \$0.000278 and \$0.000186 per thousand queries, under a dollar of compute for
  a million requests a day.
  
  \section{Where the pipeline breaks at 10$\times$}
  
  Latency is linear in $K$ with a fixed per-impression cost of about 1.2 ms
  (EB-NeRD 2.40 / 3.50 / 5.88 ms mean at $K = 50 / 100 / 200$; roughly
  $1.2 + 0.023K$). $K$ is therefore the throughput knob, and on EB-NeRD halving it
  costs little recall (93.8\% at $K=100$ against 97.0\% at $K=200$; MIND 88.6\%
  against 93.9\%).
  
  Ranked by what fails first:
  
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{As-of article statistics: the binding constraint.} It must be
      resident and grows with total events: MIND's 8.93M display and click events
      occupy 68.1 MiB at 8 bytes each, so about 680 MiB at 10$\times$ traffic and
      about 4.4 GB at MINDlarge scale ($\sim$65$\times$).
      This is the first thing to shard or move to a windowed counter store.
    \item \textbf{CPU for feature assembly.} 170--253 QPS per core means about ten
      cores at 10$\times$. Parallel across requests, so a cost line rather than a
      wall.
    \item \textbf{The ANN index and feature store, on a different axis.} Both grow
      with \emph{catalogue size}, not traffic, so 10$\times$ traffic does not touch
      them, but a 10$\times$ catalogue does, and it is the point where the
      brute-force scan stops paying and the embedding matrix needs a real
      approximate index instead of a resident array.
  \end{enumerate}
  
  \section{Beyond accuracy (Q5)}
  
  Coverage, diversity and novelty on the top-10 lists, over a 5{,}000-impression
  sample with 1{,}000 bootstrap resamples, plus two slices.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{lcc}
      \toprule
      \textbf{Metric (top-10)} & \textbf{EB-NeRD} & \textbf{MIND} \\
      \midrule
      Catalogue coverage      & 0.0696 [0.0690, 0.0701] & 0.0065 [0.0063, 0.0067] \\
      \quad catalogue size    & 11{,}777 & 65{,}238 \\
      Diversity (raw)         & 0.0370 [0.0363, 0.0378] & 0.9008 [0.8997, 0.9019] \\
      \quad random baseline   & 0.0484 & 0.9402 \\
      \quad \textbf{ratio to random} & \textbf{0.765} & \textbf{0.958} \\
      Novelty ($-\log_2 p$)   & 14.456 & 17.339 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Raw diversity is not comparable across datasets.} It is dominated by how
  anisotropic the embedding space is: a random EB-NeRD pair already scores 0.048
  under the provided multilingual BERT vectors (cosine 0.95, a narrow cone),
  against 0.940 for MiniLM on MIND. Read the ratio to that baseline, where 1.0 is
  ``as diverse as random''. EB-NeRD sits at \textbf{0.765} and MIND at
  \textbf{0.958}, so EB-NeRD concentrates, which the raw numbers suggest the
  opposite of.
  
  \textbf{Coverage is low on both, and that is the price of Stage 1.} Recommending
  from a one-hour display window can only surface what was in circulation, so MIND
  reaches 0.65\% of its catalogue. The fresh pool that fixed recall also
  guarantees a small, recent, popular recommendation set.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llcc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Slice} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{MRR (95\% CI)} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) &     67 & 0.2678 [0.2005, 0.3447] \\
        & warm                       & 25{,}289 & 0.2114 [0.2079, 0.2146] \\
        & head article clicked       &    197 & 0.0072 [0.0062, 0.0082] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 25{,}159 & 0.2131 [0.2099, 0.2165] \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & cold start ($\le$5 clicks) & 12{,}982 & 0.2426 [0.2375, 0.2476] \\
        & warm                       & 60{,}170 & 0.2399 [0.2375, 0.2421] \\
        & head article clicked       &  9{,}150 & 0.1613 [0.1568, 0.1661] \\
        & tail article clicked       & 64{,}002 & 0.2516 [0.2494, 0.2541] \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Head is the top 20\% of articles by train click count.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{Cold start is not a weakness.} MIND's cold-start users score 0.2426
  against warm users' 0.2399 with overlapping intervals, because most of the
  signal is article-side popularity and freshness, which needs no history.
  EB-NeRD's slice holds 67 impressions and is too wide to read.
  
  \textbf{The head slice is where the pipeline fails.} When the clicked article is
  among the 20\% most-clicked in \emph{train}, EB-NeRD MRR collapses to 0.0072
  against 0.2131 on the tail, and MIND falls to 0.1613 against 0.2516. Same cause
  as Stage 1: train-popular articles are old news by test time, so they are rarely
  in a one-hour pool, and an unretrieved candidate cannot be recovered by any
  re-ranker. It affects 0.8\% of EB-NeRD impressions and 12.5\% of MIND's.
  
  \section{Metrics with and without serving-time features (Q9)}
  
  Every feature is as-of strictly before the impression it scores, so nothing
  reads the future. That is necessary but not sufficient: a feature can be
  leak-free and still be unavailable to a live system. Two fail that test.
  
  \textbf{\texttt{impression\_size}}, the length of the impression's own in-view
  list. On EB-NeRD that list grows with how long the reader stays on the page, and
  correlates with the same impression's \texttt{read\_time} at Spearman
  \textbf{0.50}, so it is measured from an outcome. On MIND it is the platform's
  list length, which a system choosing its own candidates is never handed.
  
  \textbf{\texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time}}, read off the dataset's later
  \texttt{published\_time} snapshot. No live system could know it.
  
  A stricter arm also drops \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}, which a session
  store would mostly hold but which applies no click reporting lag. Arms differ
  only in the feature list; the \texttt{all} arm reproduces the shipped report to
  within $10^{-14}$.
  
  \begin{table}[htbp]
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}{llccccc}
      \toprule
      & \textbf{Arm} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{AUC} & \textbf{MRR}
      & \textbf{nDCG@5} & \textbf{nDCG@10} \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{EB-NeRD}
        & stage 1 only          & --- & 0.8815 & 0.1639 & 0.1471 & 0.1994 \\
        & all                   & 24 & \textbf{0.9219} & \textbf{0.2115} & 0.2026 & 0.2705 \\
        & serving-safe          & 22 & 0.9102 & 0.1897 & 0.1754 & 0.2372 \\
        & serving-safe (strict) & 21 & 0.9103 & 0.1887 & 0.1739 & 0.2369 \\
      \midrule
      \multirow{4}{*}{MIND}
        & stage 1 only          & --- & 0.8655 & 0.2237 & 0.2451 & 0.2932 \\
        & all                   & 24 & \textbf{0.8891} & \textbf{0.2403} & 0.2618 & 0.3150 \\
        & serving-safe          & 22 & 0.8866 & 0.2397 & 0.2619 & 0.3133 \\
        & serving-safe (strict) & 21 & 0.8859 & 0.2375 & 0.2599 & 0.3112 \\
      \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Test split, \texttt{popular} candidates. Serving-safe drops
    \texttt{impression\_size} and \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time}; strict also
    drops \texttt{session\_clicks\_before}.}
  \end{table}
  
  \textbf{EB-NeRD's headline nearly halves.} Its gain over stage 1 falls from
  $+0.0476$ MRR [$+0.0442$, $+0.0510$] to $+0.0258$ [$+0.0229$, $+0.0287$]. The
  honest claim is $+0.0258$. \textbf{MIND's holds}, $+0.0167$ [$+0.0149$,
  $+0.0183$] to $+0.0160$ [$+0.0145$, $+0.0175$], because it has the
  history-similarity features to fall back on and EB-NeRD does not: dropping
  \texttt{impression\_size} collapses EB-NeRD's model from 174 trees to 19, MIND's
  only to 108. \texttt{has\_known\_publish\_time} carries \textbf{0.000\%} of
  split gain on both, confirming the fresh pool already closed the future-article
  shortcut.
  
  \section{Design Choices}
      \subsection{Re-Ranker Objective}
      The choices were pointwise \textit{binary} , LambdaMART and the list wise \textit{rank\_xendcg}. RankNet was rejected because it minimizes the number of inversions and doesnt treat the inversion between rank 1, 2 and rank 100, 101 differently. The pointwise is rejected because they predict the absolute relevance score of each item in isolation, completely ignoring how those items compare to each other.
  
      \subsection{NRMS improvement (Q3)}
      The initial idea was adding the positional encoding on the user encoder. It was rejected on measurement: at \texttt{HISTORY\_SIZE}~=~20, \textbf{51.1\%} of MIND impressions carry fewer than 20 clicks and \textbf{29.4\%} of history slots are padding, and \texttt{layers.py} applies no masking, so PE would attach position embeddings to padding on exactly the dataset where padding dominates. \textbf{Freshness} won on a category difference rather than a magnitude one: article age is not in NRMS's input \emph{at all} and cannot be learned from title tokens at any amount of training.

❯ how to reduce the whitespace between paragrpahs

● The biggest lever isn't `\parskip` — it's `\setstretch{1.25}`, which is your own choice, not a spec requirement. A2.pdf only mandates **11pt and 1-inch margins**. Line spacing at 1.25 inflates every line in the document, tables included.
  
  Ranked by what they actually save:
  
  ```latex
  % 1. Line spacing: 1.25 -> 1.0.  Biggest single win, roughly a full page.
  \setstretch{1.0}          % or 1.1 if 1.0 looks too dense
  
  % 2. Paragraph spacing
  \setlength{\parskip}{0.2em}   % from 0.35em
  
  % 3. Float spacing -- you have 8 tables and 2 figures, so this adds up
  \setlength{\textfloatsep}{8pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}   % float <-> text (default ~20pt)
  \setlength{\intextsep}{8pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}      % for [h] floats
  \setlength{\abovecaptionskip}{4pt}                  % default 10pt
  \setlength{\belowcaptionskip}{0pt}
  
  % 4. Tighter section headings (needs \usepackage{titlesec})
  \usepackage{titlesec}
  \titlespacing*{\section}{0pt}{1.2ex plus .2ex}{0.6ex}
  \titlespacing*{\subsection}{0pt}{1.0ex plus .2ex}{0.4ex}
  ```
  
  Drop that block in after your existing `\setlength{\parindent}{0pt}`.
  
  Rough combined effect: **~1.5 pages**, most of it from items 1 and 3.
  
  Two cautions:
  
  **Don't set `\parskip` to 0.** You have `\parindent{0pt}`, so paragraph breaks are signalled *only* by that vertical space. At 0 your paragraphs merge visually. If you want it tighter than 0.2em, restore indentation instead:
  
  ```latex
  \setlength{\parindent}{1.2em}
  \setlength{\parskip}{0pt}
  ```
  
  That's the classic journal look and saves the most, but it's a visible style change.
  
  **Don't touch `geometry` or the `11pt` class option.** Those two *are* specified, and shrinking margins is the one change a marker will notice immediately.
  
  If you only do one thing, do `\setstretch{1.0}` — it's a single character and worth more than everything else combined.

❯ EB-NeRD’s slice holds 67 impressions and is too wide to read.
  
  what does this mean

● It means the **confidence interval** is too wide, not the slice. Bad phrasing on my part — "slice... too wide to read" reads like the slice is wide.
  
  The point: EB-NeRD's cold-start slice has only **67 impressions**, so its MRR interval is [0.2005, 0.3447] — a width of **0.144**, against the warm slice's 0.0067. That's **21× wider**. The point estimate looks higher than warm (0.2678 vs 0.2114), but with an interval that wide you can't tell whether cold-start users genuinely do better, the same, or worse. It's not evidence either way.
  
  Context for why it's so small: 67 of 25,356 impressions, **0.26%** of EB-NeRD. Almost nobody in this bundle has ≤5 clicks of history — a consequence of EB-NeRD's long histories (mean 160 clicks). MIND's cold-start slice has 12,982, which is why only MIND's number is worth stating.
  
  Clearer rewrite:
  
  ```latex
  \textbf{Cold start is not a weakness.} MIND's cold-start users score 0.2426
  against warm users' 0.2399 with overlapping intervals, because most of the
  signal is article-side popularity and freshness, which needs no history.
  EB-NeRD has only 67 cold-start impressions (0.26\% of its test split, since its
  users average 160 history clicks), so that interval is 21$\times$ wider than the
  warm one and supports no conclusion either way.
  ```
  
  That says which number is untrustworthy, by how much, and why the slice is so small — and it turns a vague hedge into a stated limitation, which reads better.

❯ impression size, the length of the impression’s own in-view list. On EB-NeRD that list grows
  with how long the reader stays on the page, and correlates with the same impression’s read time
  at Spearman 0.50, so it is measured from an outcome. On MIND it is the platform’s list length,
  which a system choosing its own candidates is never handed.
  
  what is spearman 0.5 here?

● **Spearman's rank correlation** between two things: `impression_size` (how many articles were in that impression's in-view list) and `read_time` (how long the user spent on that page). Measured on **24,724 EB-NeRD demo train impressions**.
  
  It runs −1 to +1. Rank all impressions by size, rank them again by read time, and measure how well the two orderings agree. 0 means no monotonic relationship; 1 means identical ordering.
  
  **0.50 is moderate-to-strong.** Not a near-duplicate (that'd be 0.9+), but nowhere near independent. Practically: an impression in the top quartile by size is substantially more likely than chance to be in the top quartile by read time.
  
  ## Why it's the thing that disqualifies the feature
  
  `read_time` is an **outcome** — you only know it after the session ends. And EB-NeRD's `article_ids_inview` is literally the articles that scrolled *into view*, so the list grows as the reader keeps scrolling. The feature isn't a property of the request; it's partly a recording of what the user went on to do.
  
  So a model using `impression_size` is partly reading the answer. It's not leakage in the usual sense — nothing looks at a future timestamp, and all our leakage tests pass — which is exactly why it needed a separate check. That's the distinction the section opens with: leak-free is necessary but not sufficient.
  
  Spearman rather than Pearson because both variables are skewed counts with long tails, and rank correlation doesn't assume a straight-line relationship or get dragged by a few very long sessions.
  
  If you want the sentence to carry its own provenance:
  
  ```latex
  correlates with the same impression's \texttt{read\_time} at Spearman
  \textbf{0.50} ($n = 24{,}724$), so it is partly a recording of the outcome
  ```
